Interview prep · 01

Your own work — the project deep-dive (P0)

Almost every startup round opens with "walk me through something you built". For each system below, prepare the same 10 pieces and be able to draw it on a whiteboard in 3 minutes.

The 10 pieces for 1.11.21.31.41.51.61.71.81.91.101.111.121.131.14

The 10 pieces for every project

  • The problem in one sentence (who suffered, what was slow/broken/missing)
    Answer

    If I get one minute, I tell the Omni story: AiRA's realtime voice assistant backend, which I designed and built in August 2026.

    The problem: AiRA is a voice-first assistant in beta on iOS and Android, and by mid-2026 a conversation could go through three separate backends, live voice mode, text chat and onboarding, each with its own socket handling and its own idea of what a turn is. Every voice fix had to be made three times, and nothing owned the whole turn: what the user said, what actually went out to them, and what a tool did.

    What I built is one pipeline for voice and text over a single authenticated WebSocket. Mic audio streams to Cartesia speech-to-text. The model's text streams into Cartesia speech while it's still being generated, and every audio frame carries the exact words in it, so captions line up and, when the user cuts in, the turn is saved as only the words that actually went out. Every turn is journaled in a Redis write-ahead log, so a crash leaves a failed row, never a missing one. One number: on 22 August I deleted about 17.8 thousand lines, the three old pipelines plus websocket v1, because Omni had replaced them.

    One line each for the others. Jigsaw, a recruiting startup: I built AI candidate-recommendation and job-extraction pipelines and a NestJS backend for referrals, assessments and LinkedIn outreach through UniPile; none of that code is on my GitHub, so I can describe it but not show it. StreetDine, a restaurant-software startup where I was a founding member: I built the live order board staff ran service on, and reporting APIs where one shared, time-zone-aware Mongo aggregation replaced four near-identical copies. Lumen: my open-source TypeScript engine where an LLM writes a small JSON script, Lumen validates and repairs it, and a Canvas engine renders a narrated video in which every frame is a pure function of time; it powers AiRA's text-to-video. Perplexity Mini: a two-and-a-half-week RAG side project, a Redis queue feeding a Playwright crawler 10 pages at a time, 384-dimension vectors in Qdrant, and Llama 3.1 8B on two Ollama instances behind nginx.

    Open source: Lumen is public on my GitHub under Apache 2.0, with CI; the npm release workflow is set up but the beta isn't published yet, and I say that plainly. Perplexity Mini is public too, warts included. AiRA's code is private. The company did open-source a frontend snapshot, aira-client-os, but I have no commits in it and only about 15 of its 14 thousand lines are mine, so I don't present it as my project.

    bullets.md (code-verified bullets per system) · career/profile/resume.tex · techinterviewhandbook.org/behavioral-interview/
  • The users and the scale (requests/sec, users, data size, latency target)
    Answer

    Straight answer: AiRA is a beta, and I won't quote a user count or a peak RPS I can't back with data. We never load-tested the socket path; the only k6 script we have hits a REST endpoint. What I can describe exactly is the load shape, the budgets in code, and what we changed as it grew.

    Through October, the whole backend was one Azure App Service container running one uvicorn process. That was a decision, not an accident: it was enough for the beta and it kept the voice path in-process. The load parameter that matters isn't requests per second, it's concurrent voice sessions. Each one holds a WebSocket, a Cartesia STT stream with 16 kHz audio coming in at about 256 kbps, 24 kHz speech going out at about 384 kbps, and a few model calls per turn. The latency budgets live in code rather than an SLO: the pre-model gate gets 1.5 seconds, and the planner and memory retrieval have 4- and 3-second timeouts that a teammate added in September; each falls back to a degraded answer instead of holding the turn.

    How we scaled. As the API grew to several workers, what broke first wasn't throughput, it was correctness across workers. In February two overlapping turns could both speak, so I added a Redis-locked voice slot. When I ported the reminder scheduler, crons would have fired once per worker, so every firing takes a Redis SET NX lock; later I rebuilt reminders so due rows are settled in one bulk write before they run, which makes each fire at most once even on a crash. In May I built the inbound bus: heavy commands went on a Redis list, RPUSH in and BLPOP out, so turns load-balanced across workers, and since a stop could land on a different worker than the streaming answer, cancels were broadcast over Redis Pub/Sub into an in-memory set the stream checks on every event.

    Then, for voice, we went the other way. In June my teammate Ananda moved us off gunicorn workers to running uvicorn directly, to stop WebSocket 1011 drops, and in July he built the v2 socket with direct dispatch, where every command runs on the process that holds the socket. Omni's audio, text and tool frames went straight to the local socket, with no Redis hop; Redis Pub/Sub stayed only for older cross-instance notifications. Simpler and faster, at the cost of a single-instance assumption baked into a few places, like boot recovery.

    So what did I change first and why? The things users could feel: duplicate reminders, a stop that didn't stop, two voices at once. Capacity was never our constraint. Had it become one on my design, I'd have measured p95 time-to-first-audio and event-loop lag against concurrent sessions, and the first change would have been sticky routing by user across instances. In November the team merged Ananda's first-principles rewrite: Redis is gone, storage moved to Cosmos, APScheduler is gone, and the API now runs in two Azure regions. What I describe is what I built and ran through October.

    DDIA ch1 "Describing load" (pdf p31–33) · DDIA ch1 "Describing performance" (pdf p33–37)
  • The architecture diagram (boxes: clients, API, workers, queues, DBs, caches, external APIs, LLMs)
    Answer

    As I built and ran it through October, it's a modular monolith: one FastAPI service, deployed as one container, with the realtime voice path, REST, background agents and crons all inside it, and hard module boundaries in the code.

    Left to right. The phone, an Expo React Native app, talks to api.airaai.in, which is a CNAME to Azure Front Door, in front of an Azure App Service container. Inside: one uvicorn process on uvloop. It serves REST and one WebSocket, /v2/ws, authenticated with the session JWT on the handshake. Behind the socket sit the Omni turn pipeline, the durable agent manager with three slots per user, APScheduler crons, and a Redis notify listener for older cross-instance notifications; Omni's own frames skip Redis and go straight to the socket. Code is layered in what we call MACH, hexagonal without the ports layer: controllers, then services, then entities with the business logic, then adapters, one per external system, and imports only go one way.

    Data: MongoDB, hosted on Azure, is the source of truth for users, sessions, turns, agent runs, approvals and the credential vault. Redis Cluster holds short-lived and atomic things: the per-turn write-ahead log, screen deltas, locks, slot counters, OAuth nonces, and the Pub/Sub notify channel. Qdrant holds memory vectors, embedded locally at 384 dimensions, so there's no embedding API call per write. Azure Blob holds files and generated pictures behind signed URLs, and Azure Key Vault holds secrets and the key that wraps each user's vault key. There's no external queue: background work is Mongo state machines plus Redis.

    External APIs: Cartesia for speech-to-text and speech, OpenAI GPT-6 models as the main brains through a multi-provider facade that also speaks DeepSeek, Gemini, Anthropic, Groq and Cerebras, a typed evaluation model from TypeSafe for the fast pre-model gate, Gemini for images, Browserbase with Stagehand for the cloud browser, Google APIs for Gmail, Calendar, Drive and Sheets, Swiggy over MCP, Stripe Link, Serper for search, and Firebase for push.

    Who chose it: the base, FastAPI, Mongo, Redis and Azure, was set up by my teammate Ananda in May 2025, before I joined. The choices I made or drove: the realtime channel, SSE, then Pub/Sub, then in February 2026 the switch to a WebSocket, which teammates then built on; Cartesia for STT, which let us drop Deepgram; Browserbase and Stagehand, after a measured feasibility study I ran; Key Vault wrapping for the vault; and Lumen for video.

    Would I split anything? We already merged one thing back: my v1 bus sent work through a Redis queue to any worker, and Ananda's v2 socket runs it on the socket's own process. The one split I'd make next is the agent runtime into its own worker process on the same Mongo and Redis, because a long browser task should never compete with live audio for the event loop. On my design, a separate realtime gateway only paid off once we ran more than one instance. Because the layers are clean, either one is a deployment change. In November the team merged Ananda's first-principles rewrite: Redis is gone, storage moved to Cosmos, APScheduler is gone, and the API now runs in two Azure regions. What I describe is what I built and ran through October. The rewrite is Ananda's, not mine.

    AIE ch10 "AI Engineering Architecture" (pdf p473–489) · DDIA ch1 "Thinking About Data Systems" (pdf p24–26) · excalidraw.com
  • The data model (main tables/collections/indexes, what is stored where and why)
    Answer

    This is the model as I built and ran it through October; in November the team's rewrite removed Redis and moved storage to Cosmos, so the Redis parts are history there. Mongo holds the durable records, Redis holds what's short-lived or needs to be atomic, Qdrant holds memory vectors, and almost every index starts with user_id, because every read is per user, which also makes it the natural shard key later.

    The conversation core is three collections. OmniSessions: title, pinned, last_seq and stored_seq, indexed on user and last update for the list screen, plus last_activity_at for sweeps. OmniTurns: one row per sealed turn, with its sequence number, whether it was audio, mode, status, the model messages, the screen state, the gate's decision, skills and per-stage usage. Indexes are user, session and seq for replay, user, session and newest first for recent context, and user and newest first across sessions. OmniCheckpoints are summaries of sequence ranges, unique on user, session, seq start and seq end, so a long session replays as summaries plus recent turns, not every message.

    Around that: Users, unique on an HMAC token of the email, so we can look people up without storing a searchable plain email, plus a partial unique index on the Google subject. AgentRuns, the background-task state machine, has indexes like status plus wake deadline, which the minute sweep uses to find sleeping tasks that are due. Approvals have status plus expiry, and a TTL index on purge_at. The vault has credentials unique on user and handle, and wrapped data keys unique on user and key version. Reminders are indexed by trigger time.

    Redis: one write-ahead log per live turn, deleted when the Mongo row seals; screen changes in a hash, omni:uidelta:<user>:<session>, with a 24-hour TTL; slots:{user} counters for agent slots; SET NX EX 50 locks for crons; a lease for the Gmail reader. Qdrant: mid-term memory clusters with a centroid and summaries, long-term facts, and file chunks, all 384-dimension cosine, with a keyword payload index on user_id so every search is filtered to one user.

    Why a document store: turns, runs and approvals are nested and change shape every few weeks, Pydantic models give us the schema in code, and we read with typed projections so we fetch only the fields we need.

    The data structures I leaned on most were Redis primitives: a list with BLPOP as a work queue, Pub/Sub for fan-out, GETDEL as an atomic claim on a buffer, SET NX locks, INCR and DECR counters with rollback. And in Python, asyncio tasks plus sets and dicts keyed by user and session. What I'd change: those in-process dicts are exactly where the single-instance assumption lives, so with a second instance, session claims move to Redis leases with owner tokens and a fencing number. On the shared Redis notify path, socket writes should go through a bounded queue per socket instead of being awaited inline, so one slow phone can't delay others. And AgentRuns has about ten indexes on a hot collection; every write pays for all of them, so I'd check which queries really run and drop the rest.

    DDIA ch2 "Relational Model vs. Document Model" (pdf p48–64) · DDIA ch3 "Data Structures that Power Your Database" (pdf p90–107)
  • The request flow, step by step, for the main use case
    Answer

    Take the main case: the user asks AiRA something out loud.

    First the phone opens one WebSocket, /v2/ws, and the server checks the session JWT on the handshake, from a cookie or a Bearer header. Text frames are JSON commands validated by one Pydantic discriminated union; binary frames are mic audio. The phone streams raw 16 kHz, 16-bit PCM, and the server forwards each chunk straight into that user's Cartesia Ink-2 realtime STT connection. When the final transcript arrives, the turn is spawned as a detached task, so the receive loop never blocks.

    The turn opens by journaling an opener line in its Redis write-ahead log before any other I/O, so even a crash during setup leaves a record of what the user said. Then it loads the session's context, the checkpoint summaries plus every turn after the last checkpoint, and resolves any uploads, in parallel with asyncio.gather. Next is the gate: a fast typed evaluation model, given 1.5 seconds, decides whether to fetch memory, search the web, load a skill, which acknowledgment sound to play, and where to move the screen. If it asks for memory, retrieval gets 3 seconds. Session naming is spawned beside it, off the critical path.

    Then the main model streams. Text deltas pass a filter that pulls out the streamed UI language and a mood filter, and are pushed straight into a Cartesia TTS context, so speech starts long before the reply is finished. That context is multiplexed on one persistent TTS WebSocket per worker. A receiver task reads audio back, and each frame goes to the phone directly from this process, no Redis hop: a 4-byte length, a small JSON header with the turn id and the exact words in that chunk with their start times, then 24 kHz PCM. That's why captions sync for free, and the server records which words actually shipped. The screen streams in parallel as lines of OpenUI Lang. If the model calls tools, they run, each result is capped at 40 thousand and appended to the log the moment it lands, and the loop continues.

    If the user talks over it, the server sees the first transcribed word and sends omni.transcript_start. Since late September, a teammate's hold-and-judge flow in the app decides, within 1.6 seconds, whether that's a real interruption, and since October the server never stops a turn on speech by itself; barge-in is the client's call. When the app cancels, the server stops delivery, and stop_voice cancels the TTS receiver before it closes the audio window, so audio Cartesia already made never reaches the phone. The reply is sealed to the words that actually shipped. A 500 ms half-duplex gate only runs when echo cancellation is off.

    Otherwise omni.audio_end goes out only after the receiver drains. Then the turn seals: one OmniTurns row with the messages, screen state, gate decision and per-stage time and token usage, and the Redis log is deleted. The reply also goes out as a push notification; in my build that fires whatever the app state, and adding an app-open check is on the list. The trade-off of this shape is that everything runs on the process holding the socket, which is fastest, and is exactly why we ran one instance. That's the flow as I built it through October; in November the team's rewrite removed Redis, so the write-ahead log and screen deltas there work differently.

    DDIA ch4 "Modes of Data Flow" (pdf p146–157) · DHANESH_AIRA_CONTRIBUTIONS.md "Feature index"
  • Two alternatives you rejected and why (the trade-off)
    Answer

    Two real ones, both decisions about how data moves between the phone and the server.

    First, WebRTC for voice. It's the textbook answer: UDP, loss tolerance, a jitter buffer, Opus. In February 2026 I moved our realtime channel to a single WebSocket instead, and teammates built the voice path on it. What we gave up is real: on TCP one lost packet stalls everything behind it, and we ship raw PCM, about 256 kbps up and 384 down, where Opus would be a fraction of that. What we got: one channel for audio, captions, commands and the streamed screen; one auth check; no media server or TURN to run; and the server owns the turn, so it knows exactly which words went out and can stop synthesis in the right order on a barge-in. The echo cancellation people pick WebRTC for, the app gets from iOS's .voiceChat audio mode anyway. For a beta on one instance that was the right call.

    We did revisit it, and I was the one who pushed. On 12 September I wrote the first proposal to move voice onto OpenAI's GPT-Live model, with the phone holding a WebRTC connection to OpenAI and our server attached on a sideband socket, and two days later I built a throwaway harness to try it. Shashwat then wrote the integration plan the team agreed on, in that same shape, with our WebSocket kept for the screen and events. Neither shipped; both were archived in November. The trigger to pick it up again is measured stalls on bad networks, or a speech-to-speech model good enough to own the conversation.

    Second, a replayable event log for notifications. On 23 February I built server-to-phone notifications as SSE with a Redis Stream per user: XADD, approximate trimming to about a thousand entries, and replay on reconnect from Last-Event-ID. The next day I replaced it with plain Redis Pub/Sub, which stores nothing and can't replay. The trade-off: Mongo was already the source of truth, and the app reloads state over REST when it reconnects, so a second copy with cursors, trimming and TTLs cost a write per event for a guarantee we already had elsewhere. I also had a gap in the stream version, no TTL on the key, so idle users' streams would have lived forever. Three days later I switched the transport to a WebSocket, because voice needs the phone to send audio and commands up, which SSE can't do.

    A third, briefly, if you want a non-transport one: for AiRA's explainer videos we didn't use a video model. They paint every pixel, which is slow, costly and hard to edit. Instead the model writes a small JSON script and my Lumen engine renders it on Canvas, so it starts playing while it's still being generated and stays seekable.

    The common thread is that each time I gave up a theoretical guarantee, I could name where that guarantee already lived, or what measurement would make me take it back.

    adr.github.io (write one decision record per choice) · DDIA ch2 "Relational vs. document databases today" (pdf p60–64)
  • What broke in production and how you found/fixed it (logs, metrics, a real incident)
    Answer

    My hardest bug was a barge-in stall in Omni in September 2026, because it hid between the phone and the server. To be precise, I caught it testing on a device against my dev backend, and the numbers are what I saw in my own debugging session, not a dashboard.

    The symptom: after interrupting AiRA, the next question sat for 30 to 40 seconds and the thinking pose stuck. I didn't start in the code. I pulled recent OmniTurns rows: the id is minted when a turn opens and created_at when it seals, so I get wall time, and usage shows output tokens per call, so a long turn with tiny output proves the time wasn't generation. Then I read the phone's console history through the Hermes inspector for the same minutes. It showed the audio queue cleared for barge-in with no cancel sent after it, and the turn rows showed the interrupted turn finishing only after a 67-second audio drain. The phone went quiet, but the server kept synthesizing an answer nobody heard, and the next question queued behind it. Root cause: the cancel had been moved to the send path, at the final transcript, but the onset teardown stops the player first, so a second later the send path saw nothing playing and never sent the cancel. My fix, on 15 September: at speech onset, check whether the reply is the thing speaking before clearing, then cancel on the wire. On the server, stopping cancels the TTS receiver before closing the audio window. The client logic has moved on since, a teammate's hold-and-judge flow decides now, but the rule from this bug stayed.

    Memory: the closest I've come to a leak was in Redis, not the Python heap. In early July the team hit a Redis out-of-memory problem, and Ananda shipped that fix. The same week I found something unbounded in my own code: old live mode appended raw mic PCM to a Redis key per session for speaker identification, in every mode and with no expiry, so abandoned buffers never went away. I can't prove it was the main cause, but it had no bound. I buffered only in passive mode, pipelined APPEND with EXPIRE NX 60, and deleted on reconnect, stop and release. I also built a Redis memory view with bounded key-family scans, so growth shows before an outage. I don't have a Jigsaw leak story I can back with code, so I won't tell one.

    Provider resilience. In 2025 I built an OpenRouter to OpenAI to Gemini fallback chain with JSON repair, and backoff retries on transient Qdrant errors. On Deepgram, my part was the graceful stop: CloseStream and a bounded 2-second drain instead of cancelling, so the last words survive; reconnect and a keepalive loop sat around it. In Omni every pre-model stage has a timeout and a degraded answer: 1.5 seconds for the gate, and planner and memory timeouts Shashwat added. The lesson that taught me most came from my own live testing in September 2026: a research run failed on its first call because I'd asked for a strict response schema on a call that also offered tools. GPT-6 writes a commentary line before tool calls; the SDK parsed it as the answer, failed, and dropped the tool calls. Now tool rounds never carry a schema, and a separate tool-less closing call produces the structured verdict.

    If something goes down, in the design I ran through October: if Redis goes, log appends are best-effort so the live turn continues, and the Pub/Sub subscriber reconnects with exponential backoff. If Mongo goes, turns can't load context or seal; the Redis log keeps each turn under its TTL, and the next boot seals it as failed. We have no external queue. If Cartesia TTS fails mid-turn, the rest goes out as text. For the main model there was no second provider in that design; only the open-weights model falls back from Cerebras to Groq. That's a gap I'd close. Since November the team's rewrite runs without Redis and in two regions, so some of these answers have changed there.

    A Linux VM, honestly: we don't run VMs at AiRA, it's a managed App Service container, so I haven't debugged prod over SSH there; my tools are turn rows, console history and docker exec into a local Mongo. On a VM I'd start with top, free, ss and journalctl.

    sre.google/sre-book/postmortem-culture/ · DDIA ch1 "Reliability" (pdf p26–30) · DMLS ch8 "Monitoring and Observability" (pdf p270–281)
  • The numbers (latency, cost, lines removed, hours saved, % improvements) — and how you measured them
    Answer

    My rule is that a number needs a baseline, the same conditions, and someone who measured it. Several numbers on my resume are counts from code or git, or estimates, not performance measurements, and I say which is which.

    Where's the bottleneck? The one I've actually measured and written up is the browser agent. Before building it I ran a feasibility study, 21 Browserbase sessions and 103 browser actions. Each Stagehand action took a median of 7.2 seconds, p90 12.1. The finder model's inference was only about 2 seconds of that; the rest was page capture, the action and settling. The finder also ate a median of about 15 and a half thousand input tokens per action. So the bottleneck is Stagehand's per-step page work, not our worker, and seven seconds is the floor a user feels per action. For voice, honestly, I never had an end-to-end p50 or p95 time-to-first-audio. In mid-2026 I instrumented TTS first audio and STT connect and first transcript, with p50-to-p99 views on a dashboard base Ananda built, but that analytics subsystem was removed in July. In the Omni build I ran, every turn row stores each stage's wall time and token counts, and I diagnose single turns from those. The right measurement is phone-side: from the moment the user stops speaking to the first audio frame played, as a distribution under the same network.

    The resume numbers. About 200 ms is detection time from the live-mode era: the phone's energy detector needed two roughly 100 millisecond chunks above −45 dBFS. It is not end-to-end latency, it was never measured end to end, and Omni's barge-in works differently now. 17,000 lines is a git diff: the 22 August commit deleting live mode, chat, onboarding and websocket v1 is minus 17,845 and plus 503. 7,700 lines is the same kind of count on the app side, legacy card and duplicate activity code removed from the Omni screen. I dropped the StreetDine line count from the resume: that −715 diff mostly moved reports to other branches, and the real dedup took the sales report from about 320 lines to about 215. 1,000 emails and 3 parallel tasks are limits in the code, not throughput. Over 95% on LongMemEval is 95.2% on LongMemEval-S, a team result in our MIND paper, so that's a "we". The $1.60 per three-minute video is my own estimate, not an invoice or a measured bill, and I present it only as an estimate. The Jigsaw numbers come from my time there, but that code isn't on my GitHub, so I can't show the measurement.

    Biggest cost saving: prompt caching on the main model. The LLM facade's streaming call had been sending no cache markers, so on top of teammates' cache-marker code I wired cache settings through it, made tool arrays byte-stable and moved volatile context to the end of the prompt. Then I probed it myself on GPT-6 Luna with our real prompt builder: on ordinary tool calls the next request reused about 99% of the previous one's tokens, and cached input is billed at a steep discount. The one miss: when a background tool result lands, the cache hits only up to that call, 8.8 thousand of 11.1 thousand tokens in my probe, then recovers on the next request. That's an observation from my own test, not a production metric, and I haven't turned it into a monthly dollar figure from billing; I'd say so.

    bullets.md (code-verified numbers) · DDIA ch1 "Describing performance" (pdf p33–37; percentiles) · AIE ch9 "Inference Performance Metrics" (pdf p436–443)
  • Your part vs the team's part (say "I" for yours, "we" for the team's — interviewers check this)
    Answer

    My rule: "I" only for what git shows as mine, "we" for the rest, and I name the teammate. The first resume bullet, founding engineer leading a four-person team, is mine to state; the Jigsaw bullets are my own account, with no code on my GitHub to show. The team, roughly: Ananda set up the repo and the deploy pipeline, built the v2 socket with direct dispatch and the later connector core, and led the November rewrite that removed Redis; Shashwat built the 2025 orchestrator loop and later IRIS work; Sahad and Sanket own most of the app; Sandy, Sumit and Shivam own smaller pieces.

    The realtime channel: SSE with Redis Streams replay, the cursor key, the Pub/Sub switch, the move to a WebSocket in February 2026 and the inbound command bus in May are mine. Shashwat added binary audio frames the day after the switch and the TTS payload side, and Ananda built the router in the restructured codebase in March and the v2 socket in July. Voice: I built Omni, the pipeline, the STT path, the token-to-speech queue, the turn log, and I deleted the old pipelines; the Cartesia adapters and FFmpeg processing were Shashwat's; the app's audio engine is Sahad's. The September onset-cancel barge-in fix in the app is mine; the hold-and-judge flow that replaced it is Sahad's. Generative UI: the OpenUI Lang backend is mine; the renderer is client-side and partly contested; the paced-reveal design and prompts in the API are mine, but the client engine files also appear under Sahad's name, byte for byte, so I call it shared and don't claim it. Lumen is mine alone, 64 commits, and so is the film pipeline in the API; teammates added TTS emotions. Memory: "we", and the LongMemEval number is a team result. In the 2025 graph memory, the schemas, traversals, indexes, batch writes, keep-alive and the second storage pipeline are mine; the skeletons and storage LLM calls are Ananda's and Sumit's. In COSMOS, the mid-term store is Ananda's; the flush to long-term memory, contradiction checks, groups, AiRA Notes, decay and the browse API are mine. Browser and vault: the runtime and integrations are mine; Google OAuth, token encryption and the IRIS base are mostly teammates'.

    Greenfield versus foundations: Omni, Lumen, the agent runtime and the vault are mostly new code. COSMOS sits on Ananda's mid-term store, connectors on his connector core, and the LLM work on teammates' adapter; prompt caching is the clearest case. The cache-marker code is Shashwat's and Ananda's, and the OpenAI cache key Omni uses is Shashwat's, so I say I "wired prompt caching through the facade", not that I added it. That's also the bullet a teammate could fairly claim, and I'd agree and name the split.

    The rest, quickly. Google connectors: the Gmail, Calendar and Sheets agents, the per-scope token store and the refresh decorators are mine; the later connector core is Ananda's. The 2025 orchestrator: the loop is Shashwat's; the typed tool framework, credential injection and the first registry wiring are mine, and Ananda later reworked that wiring. IRIS: the design, the April port and TEMPEX are mine; the later iris_ent is mostly Shashwat's. Sheets and the LinkedIn integration through Unipile are mine alone, with Drive and Sheets later reworked onto Ananda's core. Multimodal input, speech-to-text, the resume parser and the image agent are mine; some Telegram helpers aren't. Attachments: the entity, on-demand open, search and downloads are mine; the Firebase wrapper is Ananda's and the Gemini enrichment Shashwat's. Research tools and the research agent are mine; Shashwat added timeouts and retries. News feeds, triage and the fact store are mine; posts and Asks are shared with Shashwat and Ananda. Group monitoring: ingestion, HMAC checks, sync, ids, memory, mentions and the unreplied scan are mine; the rules and web app are Shashwat's and the classifier Shivam's. Canvas: v1, slides and Word with deep-merge, and the skill files are mine; the HTML user data and rewrite are Sandy's. Front end: the dashboard route guard and MIND page v1 are mine, the CSP headers Sandy's; recap blocks sit on Sanket's screens. The native UI engine, about 55 components and an expression language, is mine, but it lived on a branch and never shipped.

    WhatsApp: I didn't build WAHA. I integrated it: webhook, HMAC, sessions, LID handling, sync. In my relay I wrote a MongoDB store for the Go WhatsApp library, about 2 thousand lines, but the upstream GOWS source is private, so "new" is an inference I can't fully prove. And aira-client-os is a company open-source snapshot: zero commits from me, about 15 of 14 thousand lines mine.

    The client. My app commits mostly shipped inside Sahad's and Sanket's squash PRs, where I show up as co-author; I say that plainly and point to my own commits. At StreetDine, the socket client was Rohit's; my part was wiring it into every order view, so I say that. The merged Razorpay webhook there was his too, and my signature check stayed on a branch, so I say I "integrated" payments and name exactly what was mine.

    AI assistance. The durable-agents runtime landed as one commit on 8 October, about 21 thousand lines in 121 files, under a commit message that undersold it. Much of the typing was done by AI coding agents working from a plan and rules I wrote. I own it because the design, contracts and decisions are mine and I can walk any file, and I checked it with strict types and lint, out-of-repo test suites that fake the external services, a boot check and live runs. Review still isn't proof: my hardest bugs have lived in the contract between app and server, like the lost barge-in cancel in September, so I now write the contract first and check both sides of it.

    DHANESH_AIRA_CONTRIBUTIONS.md "Verify before using in a resume" · bullets.md · techinterviewhandbook.org/behavioral-interview/
  • What you would do differently / what's next at 10× scale
    Answer

    On the system I built and ran through October, the first thing to break at 10× is the single-instance design, and that's a choice I'd unwind on purpose, not a surprise. In November the team merged Ananda's rewrite, which removed Redis, moved storage to Cosmos and deployed two API regions, so some of this is now being answered there; what follows is my own analysis of my design.

    What breaks first: one uvicorn process holds every socket, every Cartesia STT stream and the shared TTS socket, plus every turn, the agent manager and the crons, so event-loop lag climbs and time-to-first-audio goes with it. Next is the shared Redis notify listener that older features still use: it awaits each socket send inline, so one slow phone there delays others; Omni frames go direct, where a slow phone only slows its own turn. Then provider limits, the per-key concurrency at OpenAI and Cartesia, and database throughput. I'd confirm the order by measuring loop lag and p95 first audio against concurrent sessions before touching anything.

    What I'd change: run several instances with sticky routing by user, so a user's socket, STT stream, turn log and session state stay on one box. Move session claims from in-process sets to Redis leases with owner tokens and a fencing number. Give every turn log an instance id and heartbeat, because boot recovery in my design seals every leftover log as failed, which is only right with one instance. Put a bounded queue and writer task per socket. And move the agent runtime into its own worker process.

    At a million users, memory. On my design it's one set of Qdrant collections with a keyword index on user_id, and every search is filtered to one user. I'd shard by user: mark user_id as the tenant key so each user's vectors are stored together, and route by a hash of the user. Most Mongo indexes already lead with user_id, so that's the shard key there too; the few global ones, like the wake-deadline sweep, become per-shard sweeps. And no cross-user queries on the hot path.

    Two Azure regions: the team has now deployed two regions in that rewrite, which I didn't build. How I'd have done it on my design: Front Door is already global, so I'd add an App Service and Redis in the second region and give every user a home region, because a turn needs its socket, STT stream and log in one place. The database would replicate across regions, with each user's record, sessions and turns written in their home region. Key Vault keys stay per region, and a failover means users reconnect and the app reloads over REST, which already works. I haven't built this, so that's a design, not experience.

    Rebuilding today, and the debt I left: tests in CI, not just lint and types; the tests/ folder was gitignored on my branch and the local suites cover mostly permissions and approvals. A typed event contract shared by server and app, because our hardest bugs, like the lost barge-in cancel in September, lived between the two. And time-to-first-audio measured from the phone from day one. Was the single instance the right call? Yes for a beta through October: it made the voice path simpler and faster, and every place that assumes it is something I can name.

    Memory again: I'd keep facts with where they came from and when they were true, so a contradiction is resolved by time rather than overwritten, and I'd run a fixed LongMemEval-S subset in CI, so every memory change is measured instead of argued.

    DDIA ch1 "Approaches for coping with load" (pdf p37–38) · DDIA ch6 "Partitioning of key-value data" (pdf p215–219)
  • How you tested and deployed it, how you rolled back a bad release, and what a reviewer finds in the repo
    Answer

    Honestly, the test story at AiRA is the weakest part, and I'd rather say so first.

    In CI, every pull request to master runs Ruff with every rule on, a format check, and ty type checking set to treat everything as an error. That catches a lot in a typed codebase, but it's not testing. The tests/ folder is gitignored, so there are no tests in the repo; there are eleven local pytest files, mostly for the permission, approval and card logic. The durable-agents work has test suites kept outside the repo that fake the external services. Most verification is live: harness scripts against real providers, the app in the iOS simulator with its console tailed, and Mongo turn rows to check what a turn did. Not tested: load, and end-to-end voice latency. It was acceptable for a beta on one instance with a fast way back, but it isn't for scale, and I wouldn't defend it beyond that. Lumen is different: CI type-checks, builds, checks the public API and package contents, smoke-tests an install, runs npm audit and gitleaks, and there are scripted render-fidelity tests.

    Deploy, which my teammate Ananda built: every merged PR gets its own preview slot on Azure App Service. The image is built, pushed to the container registry and health-checked on /health. It's then promoted to an immutable tag by commit SHA, both the preview slot and live production must pass a health check, and then the slot swaps into production. Azure warms the new slot before routing, so it's zero-downtime, and merges go through one at a time.

    Rollback: because every release is an immutable SHA image, rolling back means redeploying the previous SHA, or reverting the merge and letting the pipeline ship it. The real case I can point to was the team's, not mine: in June a performance PR that changed the Dockerfile and many adapters was merged one evening and reverted the next day. For the voice path, as I ran it through October, there was one more net: if a restart cuts a turn mid-answer, boot recovery seals it as a failed row, so it never silently disappears.

    If you open my GitHub, explain these first. AiRA's code is private; what's public is Lumen and my personal repos. Perplexity Mini has no tests, committed dist/ early on, and has known bugs I'd point out myself: chat streaming is simulated, two-stage retrieval has been silently flat since a refactor, and cleanup leaves orphaned vectors. Some older repos committed node_modules, and a few committed .env files with keys, which need rotating and purging from history. Some docs are stale, for example one describes Redux in an app that doesn't use it. Lumen's npm release is prepared but not published. And some commit messages undersell big changes. I own all of it, and I can show what changed since.

Generic questions for every project

  1. Walk me through the architecture. Where is the bottleneck?
  2. Why this database / queue / framework and not X?
  3. What happens if <component> goes down? How do you recover?
  4. How did you test it? How do you know it works in production?
  5. What was the hardest bug? How did you debug it?
  6. If traffic grows 10×, what breaks first?
  7. What would you change if you rebuilt it today?
  8. What exactly did you build vs your team?

1.1 AiRA — realtime voice assistant

  • One WebSocket carrying audio up and audio/text down (why not HTTP/SSE/WebRTC)
    Answer

    The phone talks to one authenticated WebSocket, /v2/ws, and Cartesia STT, Cartesia TTS and the model all sit behind our server, because the server has to own the turn. It needs the transcript anyway to run the model, and it has to know which words it actually shipped so an interrupted reply can be cut to what the user heard. One socket also means one auth check, the session JWT on the handshake, no vendor keys on the phone, and one ordered channel for audio, captions, commands and the streamed screen. The cost is an extra hop: mic audio goes phone, then us, then Cartesia.

    Framing went through three shapes. Text frames are JSON commands validated by a Pydantic discriminated union; binary frames are audio. When I built the upward side in May, the first audio frame copied our downstream layout, a 4-byte length plus a JSON header. Two days later I replaced it with a 1-byte channel id, 0x01, so there was no JSON parsing on the audio hot path. In Omni today the upstream frame is just raw 16 kHz 16-bit PCM with no prefix at all, because the mic is one slot per user, so audio can only belong to one place. Downstream is a 4-byte little-endian length, a small JSON meta with the turn id and the exact words in that chunk with their start times, then 24 kHz PCM. That meta is why captions sync for free.

    In FastAPI the handler is one receive loop on the worker that holds the socket. Bytes go straight to the user's STT connection if the mic is bound, text goes to the command router, and a turn is spawned as a detached task so the loop never blocks. Writes come from other tasks: the turn and its TTS receiver send on the socket directly. So it isn't a reader and a writer in a TaskGroup; the reader is the handler and the writers are the turn tasks. I did use a TaskGroup in my first live voice loop in May, running the model stream and the TTS feed side by side so one failing cancelled the other. The v2 socket itself is my teammate Ananda's direct-dispatch design from July: every command runs on the worker holding the socket, so Omni's audio, transcript and tool frames go straight to that local socket with no Redis hop; through October some older app notifications still fanned out through Redis Pub/Sub. Backpressure is the weak spot. Every send is awaited inline: on the direct path a slow phone stalls its own turn's TTS receiver, and on the Pub/Sub path one listener served every socket on the instance, so one slow client could delay others. The fix is a bounded queue per socket with its own writer task. In November the team rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis and deployed two API regions; that's their change, and what I describe here is what I built and ran through October.

    The channel got there fast, in one week of February. On the 23rd I built notifications as SSE with a Redis Stream per user, replayable with Last-Event-ID. The next day I dropped the stream for plain Pub/Sub, because Mongo is the source of truth and the app reloads over REST on reconnect, so a replay log wasn't worth keeping. On the 27th I moved the same channel to a WebSocket, and a teammate added binary audio frames the next day, which SSE could only carry as base64 text. When the backend was restructured in March, Ananda built the WebSocket router it lives in today, and in May I added the upward direction, commands and mic audio, which live voice needed and SSE simply can't do. So LLM text and audio always streamed as small frames the moment they existed; only the pipe underneath changed.

    Versus WebRTC, TCP costs us loss tolerance: one dropped packet stalls everything behind it, there's no adaptive jitter buffer, and we ship raw PCM, about 256 kbps up and 384 down, where Opus would be a fraction. What we got: one channel for audio, captions, commands and the streamed screen, no media server or TURN, and the echo cancellation people choose WebRTC for came from iOS's .voiceChat session anyway. For a beta that was right. What would change it is measured stalls on bad mobile networks, or moving to a speech-to-speech model. In September we looked at exactly that. I wrote an early proposal, WebRTC from the phone straight to OpenAI's GPT-Live model with our socket kept for the screen and tools, and built a throwaway harness to try the realtime model; a teammate then wrote the integration plan the team agreed on. None of it shipped, and both docs are archived now. The cheaper first step is Opus on the same socket.

    bullets.md § aira-api_s5 (real-time channel: SSE → Redis Streams → WebSocket) · developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/WebSockets_API · DDIA ch4 "Message passing data flow" (pdf p154–157)
  • The pipeline: VAD → STT → LLM → TTS, streamed end to end; the latency budget per stage
    Answer

    VAD decides someone is talking, STT turns audio into words, endpointing decides they've finished, and TTS turns the reply into audio. In Omni there's no energy VAD on the phone any more; it streams raw PCM up, and Cartesia Ink-2 behind our server does transcription and end-of-turn detection. Speech counts as started at the first transcribed word, which works as our VAD and filters coughs and doors. TTS is Cartesia over one persistent socket per worker, and the phone just plays frames. Time to first audio matters more than total time because silence is what the user feels; once she's talking, the rest can stream.

    One turn: PCM goes up to the user's STT socket and partials come back as captions. On the final transcript the phone sends omni.send_user_query with its screen state. That hop through the phone is deliberate: the phone decides whether the words are a new question or an interruption. The server checks the session belongs to the user, journals the opener (through October, to a Redis turn log), and loads context in one asyncio.gather: scratchpad, latest screen, the session's monologue, checkpoint summaries plus every turn since the last checkpoint, connector state, running agents and pending actions. They're independent reads, so you pay for the slowest, not the sum. A fast gate then decides if we need memory or a UI move, and can play a short acknowledgement clip. The planner and retrieval only run if the gate asks. Then the main model streams and audio frames come back down.

    Tokens reach TTS before the text is done. Each delta passes a filter that splits speech from the screen program, and the spoken part goes straight into a Cartesia context. I built the first version in February: the LLM ran as its own task pushing deltas into an asyncio.Queue that fed Cartesia's WebSocket. The model then answered in structured JSON, so I wrote an incremental extractor that watches for the field markers, keeps a 30-character tail so a marker split across chunks is still found, and decodes escapes.

    As a budget I think in stages: Cartesia's end of turn, then the gate capped at 1.5 s, the planner at 4 s and retrieval at 3 s, both skipped unless the gate asks, then the model's first token, then Cartesia's first chunk on a socket that's already open. Every cap has a fallback, so a stalled provider degrades the answer, it never holds the turn. To get something audible fast, the gate's acknowledgement clip covers the gap, the TTS socket is warm, and text is pushed as it arrives; after that, the model's first token dominates. I can't give you real p50 or p95 time to first audio for Omni; it isn't instrumented end to end, and I won't claim under a second.

    In live mode I did instrument speech: STT connect, first send and first transcript, plus TTS first audio per turn, aggregated per provider into p50 to p99 on a panel I added to a teammate's retention dashboard. That was removed with our analytics system on July 10, and I don't have the numbers to quote. One thing I know moved them: stopping used to cancel the Deepgram receive task, which dropped final transcripts and skewed the figures. I changed it to send CloseStream and wait a bounded 2 s instead of cancelling, because cancelling lost the user's last words, and the bound stops a dead socket hanging teardown. Today each turn row stores milliseconds for the gate, planner, retrieval, every model call with its first-token time, and every tool, which is how I find the slowest stage. For real TTFA I'd stamp final transcript, first audio frame sent and first frame played under one turn id.

    The hardest latency bug was in Omni in September: questions looked stuck after a barge-in. The phone's cancel was tied to the final transcript, but by then the onset teardown had already stopped the player, so the "is she speaking" check read false and no cancel went out. The backend kept synthesizing an answer nobody heard, and the new question was parked behind that turn. I moved the cancel to speech onset. Today a teammate's hold-and-judge step sits in front of that cut in one-to-one mode, but the cut itself still works that way.

    bullets.md § aira-api_s6 (streaming speech-to-text) + § aira-api_s7 (Omni) · AIE ch9 "Inference Performance Metrics" (pdf p436–439; TTFT/TPOT) · developers.deepgram.com/docs
  • Barge-in (~200 ms): detecting user speech mid-reply, cancelling LLM + TTS, flushing audio buffers
    Answer

    Barge-in is the user talking over the assistant and the assistant stopping. The "about 200 ms" on my resume is detection time from the live-mode detector I shipped in June: each iOS mic chunk is about 100 ms, I take its RMS level in dBFS, and two chunks in a row above −45 dBFS while AiRA is playing count as speech. Two chunks is 200 ms. That's detection, not end to end, and it comes from the chunk math, not a measured distribution. In that version the detector stopped the player on the device directly, so silence followed quickly, but I never measured speech to silence. To prove it with data I'd log per turn the phone's onset time, cancel sent, the server's cancel received and the phone's last frame played, and show the distribution.

    Full duplex came from moving the iOS audio session to .voiceChat, which turns on VoiceProcessingIO echo cancellation. Before that, a software self-hearing gate dropped mic audio while AiRA played plus a 500 ms grace, because her own voice leaked into the mic and came back as a user turn. That gate is exactly what made interrupting her impossible, so once echo cancellation removed the leak it had to go. With echo cancellation on, the floor during her speech sat around −55 dBFS and a real voice at −40 to −20, so −45 sat between. Today the gate only comes back on an audio route without echo cancellation.

    In Omni the signal changed, because an energy threshold fires on a slammed door. Speech now starts at the first transcribed word, the server's omni.transcript_start, so coughs, doors and her own voice produce no event. Slower than 200 ms, far fewer false barge-ins; that's the trade I chose, words over energy. Then it went through versions. From early September the server cancelled the turn itself on that first word. Mid-September I fixed the phone side: its cancel went out only after the final transcript, by then the player was torn down, the check read false, and the backend kept synthesizing unheard audio. I moved it to onset: check whether her reply is playing, send the cancel on the wire, then clear the queue. I also made crowd mode, several people talking, withhold barge-in while she's audible, because other voices would cut her constantly; the screen's wait flags are still released so it never hangs, and today her name is the only thing that cuts in.

    Today it's the client's call. Since late September a teammate's hold-and-judge flow sits in front of that cut in one-to-one mode: at onset the phone freezes her audio locally, and on the final transcript a quick model call decides, within 1.6 s, whether you're asking something new, correcting her, saying stop, or just saying "mm-hm". A new ask or a stop takes my cut path and sends omni.cancel_turn; a correction just drops her queued audio and sends your words, which the server folds into the running turn; a backchannel resumes her. If she has nothing left to say, onset cuts straight away. And on October 20th I removed the server's own stop, so speech never cancels a turn server-side; the phone knows what's actually playing. So she goes quiet at onset, and the wire cancel follows when the judge says cut. Speech to silence is STT time to the first word plus one hop, not measured end to end.

    On the server, cancel_turn does three stops: no more text to TTS, cancel the TTS receiver before closing the audio window, because Cartesia has seconds already synthesized that would keep reaching the phone, then end the model stream at its next event. Each audio frame carries exactly its words, so captions sync and the server knows what it shipped. The interrupted turn is sealed to those words, and the rest goes into the next turn marked never heard, with a reason: held, interrupted or overtaken; a teammate built part of that with me. Shipped isn't proof of heard, since the phone may hold a little more, but it's close.

    Speech arriving mid-answer is parked and folded in at the next tool gap. In the live-mode era that queue was a Redis buffer drained with get-and-delete, so two workers never claimed the same utterance; in Omni it's in process. The races are a cancel during planning, which is why the turn registers as active before any planning I/O; a cancelled turn's own question, which goes back in front of anything newer; a system message, which must not count as the user; and a crash after sealing, handled in the version I ran through October because recovery checks whether the row exists. Parked questions live in memory, so a crash loses them. For "talks over me" reports I'd want onset times, judge verdicts and their latency, cancel sent and received, last frame played, and false barge-in rates per listen mode.

    bullets.md § aira-client (iOS barge-in detector) + § aira-api_s7 (Omni, barge-in) · Pipecat docs "Interruptions" (docs.pipecat.ai)
  • Replacing 3 pipelines / ~17,000 lines with one — what was duplicated and how you unified it
    Answer

    Omni is one turn loop for voice and text. It streams speech in and out over the WebSocket, runs a tool loop with background agents, paints the screen with a streamed UI program, and seals every turn durably. It replaced three pipelines: live voice mode, chat and onboarding, plus the old WebSocket v1 service and its inbound event bus. The deletion commit removed about 17,800 lines and added about 500. So the "17,000 lines" on my resume is lines deleted in one commit, not some net estimate.

    What was duplicated: each pipeline had its own model loop, its own prompts, its own turn storage and its own streaming. Live mode alone had two versions of its pipeline and entity plus its own card tools, and there was a separate Anthropic-specific model entity. So a fix to interruption or streaming had to land three times. Omni has one mic entity owning the STT socket, one stream entity for text and TTS, one pipeline, one tool registry where each tool is declared once, and one turn store. Voice versus text is a flag on the turn, not a separate pipeline.

    For the migration, I started on August 17 with a deliberately standalone pipeline, so it could evolve without breaking shipped surfaces; the next day it became Omni. I wrote the protocol docs for the app team, they integrated the Omni screen on the 20th, and I deleted the old surfaces on the 22nd. In the merge with master I kept the deletions over teammates' edits to those files. We were in beta and Omni uses new collections, so there was nothing to backfill. Honestly, buy-in came from one screen doing what three did, not a formal process. At larger scale I'd keep the old path behind a flag for a release.

    Interruptions went through versions. From early September to October 20th the server did barge-in itself: the first transcribed word cancelled the turn, which is what my resume calls server-side barge-in. Then I removed that, so the phone decides, because it knows what's actually playing, and a teammate's hold-and-judge step decides whether speech is a real interruption. The server became the executor: stop text to TTS, cancel the TTS receiver so already-synthesized audio never plays, end the model stream. The interrupted answer is cut to the words heard and the rest carried into the next turn as never heard. Through October every turn was journalled to a Redis write-ahead log, and at boot leftovers were sealed as failed rows. That assumed one instance, which was our deliberate setup then. In November the team rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis and deployed two API regions; that's their change, and what I describe here is what I built and ran through October.

    Per-turn ids started before Omni. In March each pass of the agent loop got its own turn id, threaded through the orchestrators, tool calls and every notification, because a reply took several passes and the app couldn't tell which bubble a thinking, text or audio event belonged to. In Omni the id is minted when the turn opens; through October it was the Redis log key, the Mongo row id and the tId on every audio frame, and cue requests carry the turn id the phone was playing under, since replies and cues share one socket.

    career/profile/resume.tex (AiRA voice bullet) · DHANESH_AIRA_CONTRIBUTIONS.md "Feature index" → "Omni realtime assistant" · DDIA ch1 "Simplicity: managing complexity" (pdf p40–41)
  • Backpressure: what happens when TTS is slower than the LLM, or the client's network is slow
    Answer

    Short version: on a WebSocket, backpressure is TCP flow control. If the phone reads slower than I send, the kernel send buffer fills, then the server's write buffer, and from then on the send call simply waits. If your code doesn't await that, or pushes into an unbounded queue, memory grows instead. So the question is where you hold data, how much, and when you drop it.

    Here's the voice path today. The LLM streams text deltas, they go through a Lang filter and a mood filter, and each piece is pushed straight into a Cartesia TTS context with tts_context.push. There's one persistent TTS WebSocket per worker, with every turn multiplexed on it. I never throttle the LLM. If TTS is slower, text queues inside Cartesia's context, which is fine because text is tiny. A receiver task per turn reads audio chunks back, packs each into a frame with a 4-byte length, a small JSON meta holding exactly the words that chunk speaks plus their start times, and the 24 kHz PCM, then awaits the send. Since my teammate Ananda's v2 direct-dispatch socket in July, those voice frames go straight to the socket on the same process, not through Redis.

    So the backpressure point is that awaited send. If the phone's network is slow, the send waits, the receiver stops pulling, and chunks pile up in the Cartesia SDK's per-context queue, which is unbounded and in memory. Nothing is dropped on the server. The big buffer is actually on the phone: the speakerQueue plays frames at 1×, keeps them for mute and resume, and parks while paused. Cartesia is usually seconds ahead of playback, which is exactly why stop order matters.

    I only drop when intent changes, never because audio is late. A gap in the middle of a sentence sounds broken; late is better than garbled. On barge-in the phone cancels the reply on the wire at speech onset, clears its queue, and the server's stop_voice cancels the TTS receiver before it closes the audio window, so the seconds Cartesia already synthesized never reach the phone. The reply is sealed to the words the server actually shipped. If TTS fails, the rest of the turn goes out as visible text instead of stalling, and a 5-second idle timeout on the receiver makes sure omni.audio_end is never held hostage by a hung stream.

    This evolved. In February the channel was SSE, first over a Redis Stream per user, then plain Pub/Sub, and each SSE connection had a bounded asyncio.Queue of 100 that evicted the oldest item when full. That was fine while a dropped item could be recovered from the Stream; once Pub/Sub carried the whole payload, eviction lost real events.

    The weakness I'd fix next: every send is still awaited inline. In the direct path a slow phone only stalls its own turn, but the shared notify listener, which through October carried things like canvas and session events over Redis Pub/Sub, awaited each socket send in one loop, so one slow client could delay others on that instance. The fix is a bounded queue plus a writer task per socket. For audio, if the backlog passes a few seconds, stop voice and fall back to text, the same path as a TTS failure. For state frames, keep only the latest. Honestly, we never measured that backlog, so step one is a per-socket gauge of queued audio seconds.

    DDIA ch11 "Messaging systems" (pdf p449–451; drop, buffer or backpressure) · bullets.md § aira-api_s6 (inbound WebSocket event bus)
  • Reconnection and session resume; heartbeats
    Answer

    Heartbeats exist to keep middleboxes from killing quiet connections and to notice dead peers. Reconnection stays cheap because nothing important lives only in the socket: state was in Mongo and Redis in the system I ran through October, so a reconnect is a reload, not a recovery.

    The 15-second : ping was from my first version in February 2026, SSE with a Redis Stream per user. The generator waited on its queue with a 15-second timeout, and on timeout it wrote : ping followed by a blank line. A line starting with a colon is an SSE comment, so the client ignores it, but proxies and load balancers see bytes flowing and don't close the connection as idle. It also let the loop notice a vanished client. I sent X-Accel-Buffering: no and Cache-Control: no-cache so proxies wouldn't buffer the stream. Four days later I moved the channel to a WebSocket, and today uvicorn sends protocol pings every 20 seconds and closes after 60 seconds without a pong. On the phone, the reconnect retries once immediately, then uses full-jitter exponential backoff from 1 up to 30 seconds, at most 10 attempts, with a guard so only one reconnect loop runs.

    The subscriber fix was mine, in June. Before it, any error killed the Redis pub/sub subscriber task for good, and nothing noticed. Cross-worker events like stop-generation broadcasts would silently stop. I turned it into a reconnect loop with exponential backoff, 1 second doubling to 30, reset after a successful subscribe. CancelledError still propagates so shutdown stays clean. All Redis clients got TCP keepalive and 30-second health checks, and the dedicated pub/sub client got no read timeout, because a quiet channel isn't a failure. That was still cachedb_adp through October. In November the team rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis and deployed two API regions; that's their change, and what I describe here is what I built and ran through October.

    Close code 1006: my teammate added Slack alerts on abnormal WebSocket closes, and 1006 started paging on ordinary disconnects. 1006 is never actually sent. It's what the server records when TCP drops without a close frame, which is exactly what a phone does when the app is killed, backgrounded or switches networks. So I added it to the normal set next to 1000, 1001 and 1005. It was safe because it says nothing about our bugs, handler failures still alerted under their own key, codes like 1011 still alerted, and no state depended on a clean close. The honest gap: a mass drop is now silent. The right signal is a disconnect rate with a spike alert, and the v2 socket has no close alerting at all, so that's what I'd add.

    Was dropping durable replay a mistake? For February's notifications, no. Mongo is the source of truth and the app reloads over REST. For voice it's a real trade-off. Lose signal for 10 seconds mid-reply: if TCP survives, frames arrive late in a burst and the phone pauses, then carries on. If the socket dies, the phone waits up to 20 seconds for that turn and then gives up on it, but the server keeps running it, because only a cancel from the app ends a turn. Frames with no socket are dropped. The phone reconnects, the new socket registers under the same user so the rest of the turn flows to it, and the app rejoins the session and reloads what's stored. What's lost is the audio spoken during the gap. Next step: a per-session sequence number on frames and a short replay buffer for the running turn.

    bullets.md § aira-api_s6 (Deepgram reconnect, keepalive) + § aira-api_s5 (Redis Streams replay) · DBI ch9 "Heartbeats and Pings" (pdf p316–319) · redis.io/docs/latest/develop/data-types/streams/
  • Scaling WebSockets horizontally (sticky sessions, per-connection state, where state lives)
    Answer

    WebSockets are hard to balance because an HTTP request is short and can land anywhere, while a socket is pinned to one process for minutes or hours with state attached. The load balancer only decides at connect time, so load skews toward old instances, and every deploy or scale-in has to break connections.

    A sticky session means the load balancer sends the same client back to the same backend, usually by cookie or IP. An open socket is already sticky; stickiness matters on reconnect, when the new socket has to land where the state is. We didn't need it, because through October AiRA ran one backend instance by decision. Earlier, with several workers, I built cross-worker paths instead: pub/sub to reach the socket's worker, broadcast cancels, a Redis list work queue. Then my teammate Ananda's v2 socket, in July, handled every command directly on the worker holding the socket. Faster and simpler, but single-instance.

    What one worker held through October that no other could see: the user's socket, their realtime Cartesia STT connection and mic state, the running sessions, each turn's live stream state, the TTS receiver task, queued questions, a per-session speech gate, the one shared Cartesia TTS socket, app foreground state, and a per-user data-key cache with a 15-minute TTL. Per user meant socket, mic and STT, data keys, and agent slot counters in Redis. Per session meant the OmniSessions row with last_seq, checkpoints, and the omni:uidelta hash in Redis with a 24-hour TTL. Per turn meant in-memory stream state, the Redis write-ahead log, and the OmniTurns row it seals into.

    On deploy, through October, we built into a preview slot on Azure App Service, health-checked both slots on /health, then swapped. HTTP saw no downtime, but sockets on the old process weren't migrated. They dropped, phones retried at once and then with jittered backoff, and reloaded from Mongo. A turn running on the old process was lost, and at boot recover() sealed leftover logs as failed. That assumed nothing else was running, which was shaky if both slots shared Redis. In November the team rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis and deployed two API regions; that's their change, and what I describe here is what I built and ran through October. A proper drain: on SIGTERM stop accepting sockets, let running turns finish up to a deadline, close with 1012, and let clients reconnect.

    Sockets per worker: we never load-tested that, so I won't guess a number; our only k6 script hit a REST endpoint. Idle sockets are cheap with uvloop. For active voice, the single event loop's CPU runs out first: 16 kHz PCM up, about 256 kbps, and 24 kHz down, about 384 kbps, each frame parsed, packed and sent in Python, plus vendor concurrency limits. To measure, I'd replay recorded audio at 1×, ramp users, and find the knee in event-loop lag and chunk-to-send p95.

    In front: api.airaai.in points at Azure Front Door, which fronts the App Service container. For a small team that's managed TLS, an edge and WebSocket support without running nginx, plus App Service slot swaps. Uvicorn's 20-second pings help keep idle sockets alive through it.

    For 100k users: a thin gateway tier that only holds sockets and STT streams; stateless turn workers; a user-to-gateway map in Redis Cluster, which we ran through October, sharded by user with hash tags, like the slots:{user} keys we used; and per-gateway channels instead of one shared notify every instance reads. Turns get a lease and heartbeat, so a dead worker's turn is resumed from the write-ahead log.

    nginx.org/en/docs/http/websocket.html · DDIA ch6 "Request routing" (pdf p227–229) · redis.io docs "Redis Pub/Sub" (fan-out across instances)
  • Realtime channel history: SSE with Redis Streams replay → Pub/Sub → WebSocket, and what each step gained and lost
    Answer

    The realtime channel is everything live between the phone and the backend. Today: reply text, thinking, binary audio frames with their words, audio start, end and cut events, screen programs, titles, task events, approval cards and film scenes. Upstream it carries mic audio and commands. One line each: long polling is the client asking and the server holding the request until there's data; SSE is one long HTTP response, server to client only, with built-in reconnect; WebSocket is a full-duplex connection, text and binary both ways.

    Version one was mine, February 23rd, 2026: SSE with a Redis Stream per user, notifications:{user_id}. Publishing did XADD, so the stream id became the SSE id:, then XTRIM MAXLEN ~1000 as a separate call, then a Pub/Sub message with only the user id as a wake-up. Each SSE connection had a small queue; on a wake it ran XREAD from its last id. On reconnect the client sent Last-Event-ID, and I also kept a cursor key, notifications:cursor:{user_id}, as a fallback when the header was missing. A drain loop read 100 at a time until empty, then went live. The trim was approximate so Redis drops whole nodes cheaply. Gaps: it could have been one XADD with MAXLEN ~, and with no TTL idle users' streams lived forever.

    Version two came the next day: plain Pub/Sub carrying the whole payload. I gained one Redis call instead of several per event (the XADD, the trim, the wake-up, then an XREAD and a cursor write per connection), less code, and no per-user memory. I lost replay: anything published while you're disconnected is gone. Acceptable, because Mongo is the source of truth and the app reloads over REST on reconnect. What broke: SSE was one-way and text-only. The concrete problem with Streams: events by then included whole TTS replies as base64 audio, so each stream could hold up to a thousand audio blobs, with no TTL, and stale audio isn't worth replaying anyway.

    Version three came on the 27th, also mine: the same channel moved to a WebSocket, a set of sockets per user, and the auth dependencies retyped so the same JWT check works on the upgrade. A teammate added binary audio frames the next day, which SSE could only carry as base64 text. The commit doesn't record a single reason, but what made it stick was voice: the phone has to stream mic audio and commands up, and SSE only goes down. When the backend was restructured in March, my teammate Ananda built the WebSocket router it lives in now; in May I built the inbound event bus on it, with direct, broadcast and queue routes; in July his v2 socket moved to direct dispatch, and Omni runs on that.

    Streams versus Kafka: for Streams, we already ran Redis, millions of tiny per-user streams are natural there, it's sub-millisecond, and XREAD by id gives replay. Kafka gives disk durability, replication and long retention, but you can't have a topic per user, so you partition by user and filter, latency is higher, and it's heavy to run. For a few minutes of per-user replay, Streams wins; Kafka wins if the events are also your analytics record.

    Exactly-once-ish today means at-least-once plus an idempotent client. Every frame gets a per-session sequence number; the running turn's frames go into a short, capped, TTL'd buffer; the client sends its last seq on reconnect; the server subscribes live first, replays what's newer, and the client dedupes on session, turn and seq.

    Missed Pub/Sub messages, in the setup we ran through October: if a worker restarts, its sockets drop too, so the phone reconnects and reloads from Mongo, which repairs it. The silent case is the subscriber reconnecting with backoff while sockets stay up. I'd detect it with seq gaps on the client, or have the subscriber send a resync frame to its local sockets after every reconnect. In November the team rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis and deployed two API regions; that's their change, and what I describe here is what I built and ran through October.

  • Cross-worker turn coordination: inbound command queue, stop broadcast with in-memory cancel set, per-session lock, turn buffer, voice slot
    Answer

    Before Omni, a turn could run on any API worker, so I built a few small Redis pieces to keep turns ordered, stoppable and single-voiced across workers. Through October the voice path ran on the one worker that held the socket, so most of that moved back in-process.

    The inbound event bus came in May. Until then the WebSocket only pushed down to the phone. I built the upward side: every text frame is a Pydantic discriminated union, stamped with the server-side user id and routed by function name. Heavy work like an LLM turn was pushed with RPUSH onto a Redis list, ws_inbound, and every worker ran a BLPOP consumer, so the queue was FIFO, each turn landed on exactly one worker and turns were load-balanced for free. Session commands, like starting a live session or connecting speech-to-text, ran direct on the worker that got the frame, because that worker owns the socket and the STT connection, and it saves a Redis hop.

    Stop generating was a broadcast. The streaming turn could be on a different worker than the socket, so cancel_stream went out on a Redis channel and every worker added user:session to an in-memory set. The streaming loop checked that set on every streamed event, stopped, told the app, then broadcast a clear. I checked memory, not Redis, because asking Redis per token puts a network round trip on every token for an event that almost never happens. And a cancel arriving after the turn ended would sit in the set and kill the next answer, so stale markers are cleared when a new turn starts.

    Ordering was a per-session Redis lock, SET NX with a 600-second TTL, used by live, activity and chat. A message arriving while the lock was held was appended to a Redis turn buffer and the app was told it was queued. The lock holder drained before and after each turn and merged everything queued into one follow-up turn, so messages never interleave, and three quick messages get one answer, not three. The buffer is one Redis string per session, a JSON line per message, and the drain is GETDEL. Get-and-delete is an atomic claim: if two workers try to drain around the lock hand-off, only one gets the items, so no utterance is answered twice.

    The voice slot is older, from February. Two overlapping turns in one chat could both stream TTS, and the user heard two voices at once. I added a voice_stream_owner field in the chat's Redis hash, taken under the user's Redis lock, with a backoff wait for the next turn. A few days later I made it release the slot when TTS fails, so one bad call can't block later replies.

    Through October we ran one backend instance by decision, and in July my teammate Ananda's v2 socket started dispatching every command directly. So the queue and broadcast were gone from the voice path: the session lock was an in-process set, a message arriving mid-turn was folded into the running turn at its next tool gap, stop was a direct call, and SpeechGate kept one audio producer per session. Simpler and faster. The cost was that a second instance meant bringing back routing, sticky by user, and that's the first thing I'd design. In November the team rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis and deployed two API regions; that's their change, and what I describe here is what I built and ran through October.

  • Crash safety: Omni turn write-ahead log, recover() at boot, lock TTL expiry, fencing tokens
    Answer

    The write-ahead log, as I built and ran it through October, is a per-turn Redis record of everything a turn did, so a crash never leaves a turn silently missing.

    I built it as TurnBuffer. When a turn opens it writes an opener line: user, session, model, flags and the request. Then it appends a line for every request that joins mid-turn, every model response, and every tool result the moment that tool lands. Every append is best-effort, so a Redis hiccup can never slow or kill the live turn. And tool results are capped at 600 bytes, because this is crash evidence, not the record. The moment the Mongo OmniTurns row seals, the key is deleted, so in steady state Redis holds nothing. A 24-hour TTL refreshed on each append is only a garbage backstop.

    If the server restarts mid-turn, the user hears the reply stop and the socket drop, but the turn never vanishes; how the app paints it is the client's call. At boot recover() scans every leftover buffer. If the row already exists it just deletes the key; otherwise it rebuilds the turn in order and seals it as a failed row. Any tool call that never got a result gets a synthetic error saying the server restarted and the outcome is unknown, so the next model call sees a valid transcript and doesn't assume an email went out. At the same boot step the agent manager resets slots:* counters and re-adopts running tasks.

    The big assumption is a single instance: at boot nothing else can be mid-turn, so every buffer is dead. That was true for us by decision through October. In November the team rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis and deployed two API regions; that's their change, and what I describe here is what I built and ran through October. One edge I'd check is a deploy slot swap, where the new slot boots while the old one still serves; if they share Redis, recover() could seal a live turn. The fix is an instance id plus a heartbeat per buffer.

    On locks: the older chat, live and activity pipelines used SET NX with a 600-second TTL. If the holder crashed, the session was stuck for up to ten minutes; messages queued but nothing drained them. The better shape is a short lease renewed by a heartbeat while the turn runs. That lock had a second flaw too: the value was a constant and release was a plain DEL, so a holder that outlived its TTL could delete someone else's lock. That was my shortcut; I'd done it properly in 2025 in TEMPEX, with a random token and a Lua compare-and-delete, and then wrote the simpler SET NX helper for crons that the live lock reused.

    Is TTL locking safe under GC pauses or clock jumps? No. A process can stall past its TTL and keep writing while it thinks it holds the lock. Owner tokens only protect release; by October the codebase had token-checked leases, which a teammate added.
