    Destructive actions went from prompt rules to code. In 2025 the tool descriptions had "USE WHEN" and "WARNING" lines, and the playbook demanded confirmation before overwrite, clear or delete. Delete email was a permanent Gmail delete, and its description told the agent to search first and confirm. That's only as safe as the model's obedience. Today every tool has a tier checked at boot. Sending, replying and trashing mail, calendar changes and every sharing change are ask-tier: the call is held, the user approves a card, and it runs once. Delete became trash, which is recoverable, and in the version I built, the trash card re-reads the sender and subject fresh from Gmail, so the user sees the real mail, not what the model thinks it is. One gap I'd close: updating and clearing sheet values are still automatic today, on the reasoning that Sheets keeps version history so a write can be put back. I'd still make clearing ask-tier, because nobody notices a cleared range until it matters.

  • TEMPEX: merging bursts of short messages into one reply, cancelling stale runs, steering a running task
    Answer

    TEMPEX, short for "Temporal Mutual Exclusion & Preemption Exchange", is something I built in November 2025 because WhatsApp users type in bursts: "hi", "can you", "check my mail". Each message started its own LLM run, so they got three overlapping, half-cancelled answers. TEMPEX merges a burst into one reply and cancels the run that the newer message made stale.

    Version one was a Redis hash per user and chat with a status, collecting or replying, the merged text and the chunks already sent, all changed under a lock: SET key token NX PX ttl, released by a Lua compare-and-delete so only the owner can release it. If a reply was already streaming, the sent part went into the next prompt so the model continued instead of repeating itself. Version two in December added a trace id per request, an ordered stream queue where only the head may speak, a cancel list, and Lua scripts for atomic queue edits, plus polling loops while a run waited its turn.

    In February 2026 I cut it down to two fields, pending_query and pending_trace_id. Under the lock, a new message merges any pending text and takes ownership. The run then checks ownership at two checkpoints, after the pre-LLM step and after the main LLM, and cancels itself if a newer message took over. No queues, no Lua and no polling. Fewer moving parts meant fewer race windows and less Redis traffic, and it was easier to reason about, which mattered more than the extra features.

    For "actually make it 6pm" during a running task, version two routed the message: if it answered an in-flight task, it was appended to that task's execution log in Mongo and the new run stopped, and the orchestrator re-read the log on every iteration, so the running task saw the correction at its next step. TEMPEX is history: it went in the March 2026 restructure, with the WhatsApp chat pipeline it served. What replaced it, in Omni as I built it, is a turn buffer that folds messages arriving mid-answer into the running turn, and background tasks get corrections through their inbox.

  • Background-agent runtime: per-user 3-slot queue, Mongo state machine, inbox, sleep/wake, boot recovery, upkeep sweeps
    Answer

    I built the durable background-agent runtime in October 2026 because some jobs don't fit in a chat turn. Ordering on Swiggy, researching for ten minutes, or waiting a day for the user to approve an email can't block a realtime conversation, and they have to survive a restart.

    One task, start to finish: the main model calls start_task with a kind, a skill, a title and a brief. The manager checks the skill exists, every service it needs is connected, and any parent task belongs to the user, then inserts a queued row in AgentRuns. To admit it, it does INCR on slots:{user}; if that goes over 3 it does DECR and the task stays queued; if the Mongo claim fails it decrements again so the counter can't drift. The agent loop then runs step by step, saving a checkpoint each step. It reports with share_progress, which becomes an [AGENT_PROGRESS] turn, and finishes with a done tool whose schema is the skill's findings model. On the way out the app gets a task event and a card, the user gets a push, and an [AGENT_DONE] turn hands the findings to the main model, fenced as outside data, so Aira tells the user in the conversation. The slot frees and the next queued task starts.

    The row is the state machine: queued, running, sleeping, paused, then ready, failed or stopped. Every write is guarded, so it only moves from an expected status and a late writer can't reopen a finished task. The inbox holds what comes in for the task: steering, answers, credentials, approvals, wake-ups. It's separate from the conversation because those are inputs to the task's own context, not chat history, and the inbox is only drained while the task is running, so a paused task keeps its messages. Slots lived in Redis because a counter needs a fast atomic INCR and is disposable, rebuilt at boot; the braces in the key are a cluster hash tag. Run state lived in Mongo because it must be durable, indexed and queryable.

    Boot recovery deletes the slot counters, re-adopts every running row that has a checkpoint, fails rows with none as "server restarted mid-run", and only then lets the sweep run. The risk of resuming twice is a side effect that happened but wasn't journalled. So any tool call left without a result is answered "interrupted by a restart; read back before repeating any change", and an approved action that was mid-run is failed, never retried. Two instances would both re-adopt the same rows, which is why it assumed a single instance, as AiRA ran through October. In November the team's rewrite, Ananda's work, moved run ownership and slot gating onto Cosmos DB leases without Redis and deployed two regions, so on master that assumption no longer holds and leases do the fencing.

    The minute sweep, bounded to 8 at a time, fires due wakes and Chrome releases, expires deadlines, fixes slot-counter drift, re-fires done turns that were never announced after 60 seconds, expires approvals, and prunes checkpoints older than 90 days. Each part targets a specific way a task gets stuck. I can't point you to a production log of bugs it caught; at our beta scale it's a safety net I designed, not one I've watched fire.

    Approvals a day later: an ask-tier call is held as a pending row with a card, and lives eight hours by default unless the skill sets its own lifetime. If the user answers in time, the row flips from pending to consumed in one atomic update and the call runs exactly once, and orders are re-read first in case the cart changed. If it expired, the waiting task is woken with "expired". Questions the agent asks get 24 hours, and an unanswered one ends the task as ready with "no answer to" in its open items.

    On Temporal: durable timers, signals instead of my inbox, retries and history would come for free, and a lot of my sweep code would disappear. Harder: LLM and browser calls must become activities because workflows must be deterministic, long message checkpoints fight history size limits, and we'd run a cluster. To prove crash recovery, I'd write the test I haven't automated yet: start a task with a fake tool that blocks, kill the process mid-call, restart, and assert the row is re-adopted, the tool gets the "interrupted" reply, nothing ran twice, and the slot count matches.

  • Gmail/Calendar agent flow: finding the right thread and contact, time zones, approvals before sending
    Answer

    In the agent runtime I built in October, a request like "reply to Ravi and set up a call next Tuesday" becomes a background task with read tools open and every send or calendar change held for the user's approval. Aira starts a connector task with the inbox skill, which is allowed to borrow the calendar skill. The task searches with from:ravi newer_than:7d and a limit of 10, widening only if nothing comes back, and gets summaries with each mail's message_id, sender, subject and links, never full bodies, and always scrubbed. It calls reply_email with that message id, his address as to and the subject. The call is held as a pending action, a fast model writes the approval card, and the task sleeps. The user approves, maybe after editing, and it runs once. The task reads back in:sent to:ravi newer_than:1d before it calls the reply sent. Then it borrows the calendar skill, lists Tuesday's events, and calls insert_event with Ravi as a guest, which is another card where the user can change the start, the end or the guests. It reads back the event, finishes with findings, and Aira tells the user.

    Finding the right thread and person without inventing addresses is a rule plus a mechanism. The rule: recipients come only from the user's words or the sender of a mail read in this task, and ids come from a summary in this task, never guessed. The mechanism: the reply re-reads the original's Message-ID and References headers to get the real thread, which I first got right in December 2025, so it lands in Ravi's thread and not a new one. If two Ravis match, the agent can ask the user through Aira instead of picking one.

    Time zones: calendar times are RFC 3339 with the user's offset, and the task's first message carries the current time in the user's zone, so 9 pm in India is written as +05:30. "Next Tuesday" is computed by the model from that time, and that's where it can go wrong: "next Tuesday" said on a Monday is ambiguous, a request late at night can be off by a day, and daylight saving or a user travelling shifts it. Our guard is the card: it shows the actual date and time before anything is created, and the read-back confirms it. I'd move relative-date resolution into code and show "Tuesday 18 Nov" on the card.

    What the email agent never does, even with approval: send or reply because a mail asked it to; add a recipient nobody named; open a link unless code re-reads it from the mail, with the host shown on the card; or use security mails like sign-in codes as anything more than "a code arrived". It also never deletes mail permanently, only moves it to trash. Mail text is treated as data. Every mail-derived string goes through a scrubber before a model sees it, because an inbox is the easiest place to plant a prompt injection.

1.5 Lumen — realtime text-to-video (open source)

  • The LLM writes a small JSON script; Lumen renders it on Canvas, frame-accurate and seekable
    Answer

    A film in AiRA is a short narrated, animated explainer that AiRA makes when a question is better shown than told, like "how does your heart pump blood?", and it plays right inside the conversation on the phone. Lumen is the engine that draws it. In one sentence: Lumen is a deterministic TypeScript engine on Canvas 2D that takes a small JSON script written by an LLM and renders it as a seekable, narrated film, where every frame is a pure function of time. I wrote it from scratch and I'm the sole author, 64 commits. The repo is public on my GitHub under Apache 2.0; to be precise, its main branch still shows the summer version, and the current engine, the four packages and the workflows sit on my pushed feature branch, not merged yet.

    Why not just generate real video with a video model? Because a video model paints every pixel. It's slow, priced per second, text comes out mushy, and you can't fix one wrong label without regenerating the clip. When the model writes a script instead, it only makes the judgement calls: which pictures, what each step says, what appears on which word. The engine does the pixels. So it's cheap and fast, text is sharp at any resolution, a broken scene can be repaired as data, and the same script always draws the same frames.

    What's in a script: a header with the title, theme and film-wide colour categories, then scenes. Each scene has objects, like pictures with named hotspots, text, paths, charts or measures, described with closed vocabularies such as sizes, zones and roles instead of pixel numbers. It has its narration, an optional comprehension check, and beats. A beat is a list of actions like show, hide, motion, attention or label, and it can carry say, the narration words it starts on. So timing comes from the voice, not from the model typing seconds.

    "Pure function of time" means render(ctx, t) keeps no state between frames. No clock inside, no positions accumulated frame to frame, and randomness only from a seeded generator, film grain included. Any t always gives the same frame. That's what makes scrubbing, replay and export identical: if you jump to 1:40 you get exactly the frame you'd get by playing up to it, because nothing was accumulated on the way. MP4 export is just the same function called at fixed steps offline.

    Canvas vs SVG vs DOM: the frames are drawn with Canvas 2D, not SVG and not WebGL. With many things moving at once, DOM or SVG means mutating a retained tree and paying style and layout every frame. Canvas is immediate mode: clear and redraw from the function, which fits the pure-time model exactly. It also gives me blend modes and offscreen layer canvases for masks and glows, and the same code runs in Node through @napi-rs/canvas for tests. The downside is no free accessibility or hit-testing, so the app adds captions and a text version. requestAnimationFrame is the browser callback that fires just before the next repaint with a timestamp; the player uses it to work out t and draw. My loop also stops itself at the end of a scene, at a word gate, or when the page is hidden.

    Physics without a physics engine: everything is a closed-form function of t. A fall is y equals y0 plus half g t squared, and the bounce after landing is a sine that decays. An orbit is an angle that grows linearly with t. Spins turn about a pivot with a bounded sweep, a connector pinned to two moving ends is recomputed from those ends each frame, and particles are analytic: position comes from a seed and an age, never simulated. A stepped simulation would break seeking, because frame N would depend on frame N minus one.

    The main cost of the pure design is that every frame is recomputed from scratch. I handle it by pushing all the expensive work to compile time. When a scene is added, validation, layout, the placement solver and path parsing and flattening all run once; render(t) only evaluates cheap formulas over a flat list of draw components. Figure plans are memoised in a WeakMap, decoded pictures sit in one cache so a frame never waits for a decode, and the device pixel ratio is capped at 2 in Lumen's player and in the app's WebView bridge, because 3x phones gain nothing visible for a lot more fill.

    On holding 60 fps on a mid-range phone, I'll be straight: I haven't measured frame times on a cheap Android yet. Those are the levers I built in. To measure it I'd log the gaps between requestAnimationFrame calls inside the WebView, send a frame-time histogram back over the bridge, and run the heaviest scenes on a low-end device, looking at p95 frame time, not the average.

    And what can't Lumen show that a Veo-style model can? Photoreal footage, real people and faces, a camera flying through 3D space, lighting, and soft things that bend and squash, which I wrote down as the honest gap. In Lumen a picture is a still image that moves whole; the backend enforces that. For teaching a mechanism that's usually better, because a label stays sharp and stuck to the right part. For "show me what swimming through a coral reef looks like", a video model wins, and I'd rather find real footage than fake it.

    github.com/Dhanesh-Pottekula/Lumen (README) · bullets.md § lumen_aira_canvas · AIE ch2 "Structured Outputs" (pdf p123–129)
  • Playing while still being generated (streaming JSON parsing, buffering)
    Answer

    It plays while it's still being generated because everything is scene by scene: the backend releases scenes in order as they're ready, the phone appends each one to a live compiler, and the player only ever waits at the frontier.

    Half-streamed JSON is handled on the backend, not in Lumen. The planner streams one JSON object with its keys in a fixed order, title, terms, categories, pictures, steps, so pictures close first and start drawing while the steps are still being written. An incremental reader in film_ent.py tracks quotes, escapes and brace depth, and decodes an entry only once its braces have closed. It handles real edge cases I hit, like a stray ] before , { and a spare brace, and an early version raised an exception on every chunk until I made it check that the entry had closed first. A malformed entry is skipped and named in a refused list instead of failing the film. A scene writer's reply that isn't one JSON object gets one repair turn, so Lumen's compiler.add only ever sees whole scenes.

    Why can an already-added scene never change? Because each scene is laid out from the state the earlier kept scenes left on screen: where pictures sit, how big they are, which labels are up. If scene 2 could change after scene 3 compiled, scene 3's layout would be wrong and the timeline would move under the playhead. So the compiler is append-only. Adding a scene compiles only that scene and appends its time window, and a scene that can't be drawn keeps its index with zero time. I have a test that adds scenes one by one, asserts each scene's timing never changes afterwards, and asserts the whole-film compile gives exactly the same timings as the streamed one.

    Out-of-order scenes on the phone: each one arrives as an omni.film_scene frame with its index. The client store keeps a contiguous list. An index below its length is ignored, so a known index is never replayed. The next index is appended, and any parked successors drain in behind it. A higher index is parked and triggers a resync: after 250 ms the client fetches the film with every scene released so far, retrying every 2 seconds up to five times. I didn't send scenes as Lang deltas, because a Lang statement is replaced whole and the film would be re-sent with every scene.

    Seeking: the player moves by scene, and by time inside a scene, using the engine's own scene windows, and a time is clamped inside the film compiled so far. If you go to a scene that hasn't arrived, the player enters buffering, the only state that shows a loader, and holds the frame until the scene lands; once the backend says the film is ready, a missing scene means you've reached the end. It stays frame-accurate because earlier scene windows are frozen; new scenes only extend the timeline at the end.

    The honest weak spot is time to first scene. In one timed run of film-lab, my local replica of the pipeline, on a heart film, planning streamed for about 130 seconds, the first scene in order was ready at about 159 seconds and the last at about 246. The lab builds everything before playing, so that's not a measurement of AiRA's streaming, but with in-order release it means playback could start about a minute and a half before the film finishes. Still, two and a half minutes to the first scene is the number I'd attack next, by getting the opening step planned and written first.

    bullets.md § lumen_aira_canvas (streaming scene-by-scene compiler) + § aira-api_s5 (half-streamed JSON parsing) · HOL ch6 "Grammar: Constrained Sampling" (pdf p216–220)
  • Cost: ~$1.60 per 3-minute video vs ~$79 with Veo — how you measured it
    Answer

    About $1.60 for a roughly three-minute film, and I want to be precise about what that number is: an estimate from one instrumented run, not production billing.

    Here's the arithmetic. I ran a film, "How does your heart pump blood around your body?", through film-lab, my local replica of AiRA's film pipeline with the same prompts, models and rules, which logs token usage for every call. The film came out 198 seconds long, 7 scenes and 10 pictures. Across the planner, the seven scene writers and three corrective retries it used about 479,000 input tokens and about 18,000 output tokens, reasoning included. At the planner model's price of $2 in and $10 out per million, from third-party price lists, that's about 96 cents plus 18 cents. Ten pictures at 4.5 cents each, the list price for AiRA's Gemini Flash image model at 512 pixels, is 45 cents. Total, about $1.59, so I say about $1.60.

    What's inside and what isn't. The planner is the small part, about 41,000 input tokens. The bulk is the scene writers: each one sees about 43,000 tokens of catalog and rules, and ten writer and retry calls account for about 438,000 of the 479,000. Left out: the eight part-finding vision calls, which weren't logged; narration, which in AiRA is Cartesia speech made per scene, while the lab used a different TTS; image search; and the conversation turn that commissioned the film. The lab also drew with OpenAI's image model, because it had no Gemini key, so the picture line is what AiRA would pay, not what the lab paid. On the other side, I gave no credit for prompt caching. The writers share a cache key and an identical 43,000-token prefix, so the real bill should come in lower. So I say "about" on purpose.

    The $79 for Veo is not a measurement either; it's a list-price comparison. Veo 3.1 was listed at about 40 cents a second when I checked, and 198 seconds times 40 cents is about $79. That's all it is: list-price arithmetic on this film's length. A flat 180 seconds would be $72, and I never generated a Veo version to compare quality or real billing. On top of that, a video model makes short clips, so a three-minute film is many clips stitched together, with consistency problems between them. So roughly fifty times cheaper, at list prices, for this kind of explainer.

    If I were to measure it properly, I'd do it in production, not the lab. Every model call in a film run is already stored as an iteration on the run row with its stage and timing. I'd add token usage and the image and speech calls to that, keep a per-model price table, sum it per film, and report cost per film minute at p50 and p95 across real films, with cached tokens counted at the cached price.

    career/profile/resume.tex (AiRA text-to-video bullet) · AIE ch4 "Cost and Latency" (pdf p201–203) · ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/pricing (Veo price)
  • 17 animation modules across 4 packages — the package boundaries and why
    Answer

    Lumen had 17 animation primitive modules in its library, and it's packaged as 4 npm packages.

    The 17 modules all landed in one commit in July, and every one is a pure function of time or progress. Motion has easing, springs, stagger and a seeded random generator. Strokes and stroke verbs do draw-on along an arc-length window, erase, traced paths, arrowheads and a hand-drawn wobble. Reveal does wipes, iris, blinds and dissolves, with soft edges from an offscreen mask. Focus does dim-everything-except, spotlight, highlight rings and a magnifier. Callout places labels with leader lines in eight directions. Sequence does progressive disclosure and predict-then-reveal. Type motion is kinetic type: counters, typewriter, word reveal. Then particles, which are analytic rather than simulated, a camera with pan and log-interpolated zoom, charts, timelines, shape morphing that resamples and aligns two outlines before interpolating, icons, a small canvas math typesetter I chose over KaTeX to stay offline and deterministic, map projection with borders over time, and the first JSON storyboard format. The library has moved on since: the storyboard format was replaced by the validated JSON compiler, and figures, physics diagrams and cards were added. So 17 is the original primitive set.

    The four packages are split by dependency. @aira/lumen is the core: schema, validation, compiler, canvas renderer and the capability catalog, with no dependency on React, Cartesia, an MP4 muxer or any LLM SDK. @aira/lumen-react is the player component with play, pause, seek, captions and audio sync. @aira/lumen-cartesia is optional narration: speech with word timestamps, a local IndexedDB cache and alignment to scenes. @aira/lumen-mp4 is the optional in-browser exporter. The rule is simple: anything that needs a vendor key, a UI framework or a browser-only API is an optional adapter, and the core stays pure, so it runs in Node, a browser or a phone WebView.

    Why it matters in practice: AiRA ships only the core, bundled into one file inside a WebView, with no React DOM there and narration handled by our own backend, while someone on the web can use React plus Cartesia. Both the player and the exporter call one shared frame function, so playing and exporting can't drift apart. The weak spot is that the four packages are versioned together at 0.1.0-beta.1 and none is on npm yet; the split lives on my pushed feature branch, while the repo's main branch still shows the earlier single-app layout.

    github.com/Dhanesh-Pottekula/Lumen · bullets.md § lumen_aira_canvas (17 modules, four npm packages) · DDIA ch1 "Evolvability: making change easy" (pdf p41–42)
  • Compile pipeline: JSON Schema with AJV, semantic checks, typed path diagnostics, validate-and-repair, dropping undrawable scenes
    Answer

    JSON Schema is a standard way to describe the shape of JSON: types, required fields, enums and ranges. AJV is the fast JavaScript validator that compiles a schema into a validation function. I run it on the 2020-12 draft in strict mode, with all-errors on, so a scene gets every problem at once instead of one at a time.

    The pipeline, per scene: decode; the AJV schema check; semantic checks a schema can't express, like duplicate ids, unknown targets, placement cycles, and lifecycle rules such as acting on something before it's shown; a layout resolver that turns words like sizes and zones into real boxes on a 540 by 960 portrait view; compile into a flat list of draw components with entrances, exits and motions; canonical geometry checks, like refusing an orbit whose first frame would jump from where the object rests; then repair, or refuse the scene. In AiRA it runs in strict mode, one scene at a time, as scenes stream in.

    "Validate and repair" has three outcomes. Fixed: only where exactly one reading exists. A field that can only mean one thing gets corrected, objects are auto-fitted and clamped into the safe frame, overlaps are pushed apart, and a picture that would be too small to read is raised to the smallest size that reads. Trimmed: an unknown word, object or action is dropped with a DROPPED_* warning, along with everything that named it, and a motion that would go nowhere is refused while the rest of the scene plays. Rejected: schema errors, broken references, lifecycle errors and motion geometry that can't be repaired drop the whole scene. The lenient ones are lenient for a practical reason: refusing a scene over one misspelt word blanks part of a film.

    What should never be auto-repaired is anything with more than one reading, or where the repair changes meaning. In the geometry repair step, anything that can't be fixed one unambiguous way is fatal for that scene rather than guessed. The backend follows the same rule: if a picture lost a part, a reference to that part is dropped, never widened to the whole picture, because a label meant for the left ventricle pointing at the whole heart teaches something false. Wrong and confident is worse than missing.

    Why one undrawable scene doesn't break the film: compiling is per scene, a dropped scene keeps its index with zero time on screen, and only kept scenes pass layout state forward, so a dropped one changes nothing for the others. The rest plays.

    Typed, path-addressed diagnostics mean every problem comes back as code, path, message and received value, like UNKNOWN_TARGET at a specific beat and action. With those, code can drop exactly that action, decide blocking or not from the code, and give the scene writer one corrective retry that names each reason and the exact ids it may use. One error string can't be acted on that precisely. And invalid JSON mid-stream never reaches this layer: the backend only decodes entries whose braces have closed, and a writer reply that isn't one JSON object gets one repair turn first.

    The paint recorder catches what validation can't, because validation checks what the model wrote and the recorder checks what the engine actually drew. It records every paint on the layer canvases with the engine function and component that made it, then flags paint nobody asked for, glows in the wrong place, labels far from their thing, collisions with a picture's silhouette, motions that contradict their statement, jumps between scenes and stale leftovers. All of that is valid JSON.

    And the flash limiter: WCAG 2.3.1 says content shouldn't flash more than three times a second, because of seizure risk for people with photosensitive epilepsy. A model told to "emphasise" can stack pulses, so I enforced it in the engine, not the prompt: no burst within a third of a second of the previous one, and glows never fade faster than 0.3 seconds or grow past a size cap. A prompt rule is a suggestion; an engine rule always holds.

  • AiRA image films: streaming planner, picture lanes, vision inspector hotspots, scene writers, word-synced narration
    Answer

    The main model writes one line, Film(title, brief), in its screen program, and everything after that is a background worker that streams scenes to the phone. I built this pipeline in October, after the drawn-SVG version failed; what I describe is my branch through October, and the team's November rewrite carried it over into the new codebase.

    The flow: the phone sees the Film statement and commissions it, and the agent manager starts a film worker. A streaming planner, OpenAI's Sol model at medium reasoning, loads its prompt layers and then writes one JSON object: title, terms, categories, the pictures needed, then the steps, each with its narration and the exact screen. As each picture entry closes, a picture lane starts. It either finds a real photo through image search or draws one with Gemini's Flash image model at 512 pixels on a flat ground, in the style of the film's first drawing. Pillow and numpy cut the subject out, it's uploaded to blob storage, and a vision model finds the named parts. Once a step's pictures are measured, its scene writer runs, with four lanes each for drawing and writing. An assembler, all in code, holds each scene to the engine's rules, keeps continuity between scenes, allows one corrective retry, and publishes scenes strictly in order.

    Labels on parts of a generated picture: first, part names are never sent to the image model, because a map told "serbia" came back with SERBIA written on it. The vision model returns boxes as fractions, missing parts and which way the subject faces. The outlines are traced in code, not by the model: flood fill from the box centre over its commonest colours, then a Moore-neighbour border walk resampled to 64 points. Those become named hotspots and outlines on the picture in Lumen, so a label, an arrow, a dimmed hole or a tint targets heart.left-ventricle and rides with the picture when it moves. In the engine, one placement solver keeps labels off the real drawn outlines, and the path layer gives drawn shapes named corners and sides.

    Word sync: each beat names the narration words it starts on with say. The engine turns that into a word index and emits per-word cue times for each scene. On the phone, each scene's narration plays, the app tracks how many words have actually been heard, and the player gates on that: it holds the picture at the next cue until that word is spoken. If the voice pauses, the picture waits. Earlier versions aligned Cartesia word timestamps to captions with a piecewise time remap; estimated timings drifted, so I moved to gating on heard words.

    The planner's three prompt layers: a generic rules file that's always loaded, with the priority right kind of visual at believable sizes first, right pictures second, motion last. Exactly one of four narratives: explain, how-to, story or weigh. And one to four of 19 visual skills named for the shape of the idea, like mechanism, cause and consequence, change over time, parts and structure, size and amount. The planner picks them with a single load call before it writes anything. These replaced nine subject skills like biology and physics, because the subject doesn't tell you how to show something; the shape of the idea does.

    Why model-drawn SVG failed, which I wrote up in a 418-line research doc: a model writing SVG can't see its own output, coordinates eat its token budget, it doesn't know paint order so shapes hide each other, and curves are the worst. With only nine ready-made drawings, the model fell back to hand-writing whole SVGs; in one gravity film, six of twelve objects were hand-drawn. Circles survived; an onion, a hand and a knife didn't. So now the image model draws, a vision model measures, the engine owns positions, and the LLM only makes calls about meaning. The cost is more model calls and latency, and pictures that move only whole. The narration's emotion tags came from my teammate Ananda's TTS work, which I merged into the planner.

  • Keeping prompts in sync with the engine and judging quality: generated catalog, drift checks, placement solver, film report
    Answer

    Two different answers here: for films the prompt is generated from the engine and the build fails on drift; for the screen UI language the generator lives in the app, and keeping it in step across repos is still partly manual.

    Films first. Lumen's schema is the single source of truth. A catalog module builds the scene writer's catalog from it, one entry per kind, verb, placement and composition, reading types, limits and enum values straight from the schema, and it refuses to render if anything lacks a description. When I rebuild the engine, one bundler script builds it for the app and writes that catalog into the backend as the film prompt, so the engine the phone runs and the catalog the writers read come out of the same run. On the way it strikes out withheld vocabulary, then checks that no withheld name survived, and throws, writing nothing, if one did. It also throws if fewer entries were struck than expected, which catches the schema changing shape. To be exact about "can't drift": that holds for the generated catalog. It's a script I run, not a CI gate, and the hand-written planner and step prompts around it are prose that can still mention things. When I rebuilt it for film-lab in October, the regenerated catalog came out byte-identical to the API's copy.

    Why fail the build? Because of what happens when the model keeps using a feature you removed. My lesson was that prohibitions in prose only teach the vocabulary. If the catalog lists a word and says "don't use this", the model uses it. So the fix is three layers: strike it from the contract the model reads, have the validator drop it with a warning if it still appears, and fail the build if it survives in the catalog. A real case: once the engine started moving older pictures aside on its own, the aside verb was withheld from the model.

    The placement solver: one solver places a box above, below, beside, near, on or inside a referent, avoiding the real drawn outlines and strokes, not bounding boxes, staying inside the frame and taking the nearest free spot first. Labels go on the shape if they fit, else beside it, else into a tidy pointer column like a key. The same solver picks motion end points and where a character's speech line goes. Everything is sized for a 540 by 960 portrait phone view, with a minimum text size of about 12 points on a phone and a 4.5 to 1 contrast floor.

    Readable on a phone: a film report script renders every beat of real films at phone scale and measures text size, contrast of the text's ink against the worst background pixel behind it, since WCAG judges the least contrast, plus crowding, sync and pacing. It can compare two engine builds. Next to it, the paint recorder checks what was actually drawn.

    Does a film actually teach? Honestly, we haven't measured that with users. Today's proxies are a plan lint that stores about 25 counts per plan, like terms never spoken or words on screen the voice never says, plus the film report and the paint checks. I'd measure first-try answers on each scene's comprehension check, the scene where people drop off, rewinds per scene as a confusion signal, and follow-up questions that re-ask the same thing. At scale, without watching every film: run the automatic checks on every film, send Lumen's deterministic keyframes to a vision judge with a rubric calibrated against a small human-rated set, have people review the lowest scorers, and track all of it per prompt version.

    On the UI side it's weaker. In September I wrote a TypeScript-compiler script in the app that reads each component's signature and props and fails on a mismatch, and my teammate Sahad later extended it to check the parser's own table. But the catalog the backend actually teaches from, ui_catalog.md, is a prose file several of us hand-edit in the API repo, so the two can drift. I'd close that with an API CI check that compares its signature lines against the app's component table.

  • Packaging, export and release: one-IIFE WebView bundle, WebCodecs MP4 export, CI checks, provenance-signed npm, golden frames
    Answer

    The engine reaches the React Native app as one minified file inside a WebView. A bundler builds the core package with esbuild into a single IIFE that exposes a global Lumen, and writes it into the app as a string constant. The app loads it into a WebView shell whose content security policy allows no network at all, images only as data URLs and inline script only, and talks to it through a small bridge: set the header, append scenes, hand over pictures, sync a scene, report heard words. The engine never fetches anything, so the app prefetches pictures and passes them in as data URLs. One honest catch: the policy allows unsafe-eval, because AJV compiles its validators with new Function; precompiling them at build time would remove that. The backend never runs the engine at all; only the generated catalog crosses into the API.

    MP4 export is in the open-source playground, not the phone app. It renders every frame offline at a fixed frame rate with the same frame function the player uses, encodes video with WebCodecs' VideoEncoder as H.264 and the narration with AudioEncoder as AAC, and muxes them with mp4-muxer. It runs faster than real time, it's deterministic rather than a screen capture, and picture and voice stay in sync because they come from one timeline. The limit is browser support for WebCodecs encoding.

    Before a release, CI runs typecheck, build, a public-API check that compares exports against a snapshot you have to update on purpose, a package-content audit so only allowed files go into the tarball, an install smoke test from a locally packed build, npm audit, and gitleaks for committed secrets. Two honest gaps: the engine's own test scripts aren't in CI, I run them locally; and the workflows live on my pushed feature branch, not on main yet, so I'd call CI wired rather than proven.

    Provenance: the release workflow publishes with npm publish --provenance from GitHub Actions using an OIDC token, so npm attaches a Sigstore-signed statement that this tarball was built from this repo, this commit and this workflow. The attack it stops is someone with a stolen npm token publishing a tampered version from their own machine: it won't carry valid provenance, and users can check with npm audit signatures. It doesn't stop malicious code committed to the repo or a compromised dependency. To be clear, the workflow is wired but nothing has been published; npm has no @aira/lumen package today. It only runs by manual dispatch from main, and the AiRA work is on a feature branch.

    Testing a rendering engine: determinism is what makes it testable. Today the tests are Node scripts that render with @napi-rs/canvas. One compiles probe lessons and checks layout and visibility. A path suite of about 2,500 lines checks properties, like a label placed next to a part landing next to that part, and that compiling scene by scene gives exactly the same timings and layout as compiling the whole film. The film report measures contrast on real rendered pixels and can compare two builds, and the paint recorder checks every draw call. What I don't have yet is golden frames: PNG snapshots at fixed times, compared with a small tolerance. The engine already picks deterministic keyframes, and the seeded randomness makes snapshots stable, so that's the next thing I'd add, along with fuzzing scenes from the schema.

1.6 Jigsaw — hiring platform

  • AI candidate matching (10,000+ recommended): embedding/ranking pipeline, how a match score is built
    Answer

    At Jigsaw I built and scaled two AI pipelines, candidate recommendation and job extraction. Over my time there, August 2024 to July 2025, the recommender recommended 10,000+ candidates and the extractor processed 2,000+ jobs. I'd say plainly that those are recommendations made, not hires. Jigsaw's product covered the whole hire: hiring companies and their recruiters posting jobs and running interviews, video assessments and chat, and candidates applying or being referred. [FILL: one line on who Jigsaw's customers were and roughly how big the candidate pool was.] Recommendation means a job goes in and a ranked shortlist of candidates from our pool comes out. [FILL: what the recruiter actually saw per candidate: a score, a reason, or just the ranked order.]

    An embedding is a model turning text into a vector so that things that mean the same land close together. That's why it fits matching. A job asking for a frontend engineer with React and a profile that says built UIs in Next.js share few exact keywords, but their vectors sit near each other, and keyword search alone would miss that. The trade-off is that an embedding is fuzzy: it knows topic well and hard facts like years of experience or city badly.

    How the score was built: [FILL: the embedding model, where the vectors lived, and whether the score was pure similarity, keyword rules, an LLM, or a blend, with the weights if there were any]. The shape I'd defend on a whiteboard is filter first, then rank. The new job is parsed into fields by the extraction pipeline. Hard constraints like location, experience band and notice period are filters, not similarity, and they belong inside the vector query as metadata filters, so you never rank someone who can't take the job and never filter after a top-k and end up with three results. Then you embed the job's requirement text, compare it with the stored candidate vectors, blend that similarity with explicit signals such as must-have skills present and experience fit, attach a short why, matched skills and missing ones, and treat recruiter shortlist or reject as feedback.

    Batch versus on request: [FILL: whether recommendations were computed in a batch job or on request, and why]. The trade-off I'd explain is that candidate pools change slowly and jobs arrive a few at a time, so computing the shortlist when a job is created or changed and caching it keeps the recruiter's screen instant and the model cost bounded. On request you only read the cached list and apply fresh filters, like dropping someone who was just hired.

    How do I know it was good? I don't have a precision number to quote, so I won't make one up. [FILL: how quality was actually judged at Jigsaw: recruiter feedback, spot checks against manual shortlists, or nothing formal.] The way to prove it is a labelled set of past jobs with the candidates recruiters actually shortlisted, then precision at 5 and at 10 against it; live, the recruiter accept rate on recommendations; and a side-by-side against a manual shortlist for the same job. Bias I'd check the same way. Strip names, photos, gender and obvious proxies from what gets embedded, compare selection rates across groups on that labelled set, and run counterfactuals: swap the name or college on one profile and the score shouldn't move.

    If a recruiter says the top 5 are always wrong for senior roles, I'd pull those jobs and look at the parts of the score. The usual cause is that embeddings match on skills, not seniority, so a junior with the same stack scores as high. The fixes are seniority as a hard filter or a strong feature, a check that the extractor actually reads the experience range from senior posts, and a small labelled senior set kept as a regression test.

    Freshness: when a profile changes, I'd put an event on a queue and have a worker re-embed it and upsert by candidate id with a content hash, so a retry or a duplicate event is a no-op and an unchanged profile isn't re-embedded, and I'd mark any cached shortlist that includes that candidate as stale. Switching embedding models is a migration, not an update, because vectors from two models can't be compared. Build a new index next to the old one, backfill in batches, write new updates to both, shadow-compare top-k overlap and accept rates on a sample of jobs, then flip reads behind a flag and keep the old index until you're sure.

    A re-ranker goes after retrieval. Take the top 50 to 100 from the vector search and rescore each job and candidate pair with a cross-encoder or an LLM judge. A cross-encoder is one forward pass per pair, so cost grows with k. An LLM judge takes seconds and real money per job. Both belong in the job-created batch path, not on a click.

    With today's models I'd use hybrid retrieval, keyword plus dense, then an LLM re-ranker that has to quote the lines of the resume behind its verdict. For trust, I'd show the why next to every score: matched must-haves, missing ones, the experience fit. I'd show bands like strong, possible, stretch instead of a fake-precise 87 percent, and let recruiters correct a match so it becomes training and eval data.

    career/profile/resume.tex (Jigsaw) · HOL ch2 "Embeddings for Recommendation Systems" (pdf p89–93) · HOL ch8 "Dense Retrieval" (pdf p250–262)
  • Job extraction (2,000+ jobs, 400+ hours saved): extracting structured fields from job posts with LLMs
    Answer

    Job extraction takes a job post as it arrives and turns it into the structured record the rest of the system runs on, because matching filters and ranks on those fields. At Jigsaw it processed 2,000+ jobs. [FILL: the fields you actually extracted, for example title, location and remote or not, experience range, must-have and nice-to-have skills, employment type, salary.] The rule I'd hold to for any of them is that salary and years are only filled when the post states them.

    How to make an LLM reliable here: [FILL: the model and the validation approach you actually used, and what happened when an output failed]. The pattern I'd describe, and would build again, is to not trust the model's text. Ask for output against a JSON schema and validate against the same schema in code. If validation fails, retry once with the validation error in the prompt. If it still fails, keep what parsed, set the rest to null, and send the job to a human review queue instead of guessing. For invented values the rule is grounding: a salary or a number of years is only accepted if that number actually appears in the source text, otherwise it's null. A model filling an empty salary field is the classic failure, and a cheap substring check catches most of it.

    Normalising inputs: [FILL: which formats you actually received, PDFs, HTML pages or plain text, and how you turned them into text]. The general approach is to get everything to clean text first: the text layer out of PDFs, HTML stripped of navigation and boilerplate down to the main content, whitespace collapsed, length capped. After that one prompt and one schema serve every source, and a hash of the normalised text gives you exact de-duplication for free. Near duplicates, the same job reposted, match on title, company and location or on embedding similarity above a threshold. Candidate profiles de-duplicate on email, phone or LinkedIn URL. [FILL: how duplicates were actually handled at Jigsaw, if they were.]

    The 400+ hours on my resume is an estimate, not a timesheet, and the resume counts it as manual screening saved across the pipelines. It's the manual minutes a person would spend per item times the volume. [FILL: the per-item minutes you assumed, and which work is counted: job entry, candidate screening, or both.] For scale, if it were all job extraction, 400 hours over 2,000 jobs is 12 minutes a job, which is why I'd state the real assumption out loud rather than let the number stand alone.

    Cost: I don't have a per-extraction figure to quote. [FILL: the per-extraction cost or monthly LLM spend, if you know it.] The levers are clear though, and they're how I'd make it 10 times cheaper. Use a small model for the easy majority and escalate to a bigger one only when validation fails. Trim the input to the main content. Cache by content hash so a reposted job is never extracted twice. Use a provider's batch API when nothing is waiting on the answer. Pull easy fields like 3 to 5 years with a regex before the model sees the text.

    Personal data: job posts are mostly public, but resumes are personal data, so only the fields extraction needs should leave the building. Email, phone and address get masked before the call and joined back by id afterwards, and the provider has to be on terms that don't train on or keep the data. [FILL: exactly what you sent to the provider at Jigsaw and what you masked.]

    If I designed it fresh: a versioned schema; normalise, then extract, then validate, retry and ground; a review queue for failures; and a gold set of a few hundred hand-labelled posts with field-level accuracy, run on every prompt or model change, plus a weekly spot check of live output.

    career/profile/resume.tex (Jigsaw) · AIE ch2 "Structured Outputs" (pdf p123–129) · HOL ch6 "Output Verification" (pdf p213–220)
  • Reports 12 min → 30 s: what was slow (N+1? no index? in-app aggregation?) and the fix
    Answer

    A Jigsaw report went from 12 minutes to 30 seconds, about 24 times faster, and the lesson was measure first, then fix the real cause. [FILL: what the report contained and who read it: which entities, which period, which users.] Twelve minutes hurts because someone sits waiting for it, and a request that long also runs past normal HTTP and proxy timeouts.

    Two terms first. An N+1 query is when code fetches a list with one query and then runs one more query per row, so 1,000 rows means 1,001 round-trips. An explain plan is the database telling you how it will run a query: index scan or full scan, rows examined, join strategy and where the time goes.

    Finding the time: [FILL: what you measured first, step timings, query logs, EXPLAIN or counting queries]. The order I'd go in is to time each step of the report, count the queries one run makes from the query log, and run EXPLAIN on the heavy ones, which splits the 12 minutes into round-trips, slow single queries and time spent in the app. The cause and the fix: [FILL: the real one, per-row lookups batched into one query, a missing index added (name the columns and their order), aggregation moved into the database (which GROUP BY or pipeline), or a mix, with the round-trips before versus after and whether rows were streamed or loaded]. That's the before-and-after I'd draw on a whiteboard.

    Why moving aggregation into the database wins over doing it in Node: the database aggregates where the data lives, uses its indexes, and sends back a few summary rows instead of every raw row over the network to be looped over in JavaScript. One GROUP BY, or an aggregation pipeline, replaces thousands of round-trips. App code is still right when the logic is genuinely hard to express in a query or needs data from another system. Indexes go equality columns first, then the range column, for example company id, then created_at, so the index seeks to one tenant and scans only the date range. You check it with EXPLAIN: the plan should show an index scan and rows examined close to rows returned.

    Proving the fast report gives the same numbers as the slow one: run both on the same real data for several date ranges and diff the output field by field, including empty ranges and edge days. Any difference is a bug in one of them, not rounding. [FILL: how you actually checked it.] For the 30 seconds, [FILL: whether it was one run or a median of repeated runs on production-size data]. I don't have a load-test number for ten people running it at once, so I won't claim one. On memory, a big export should stream: a cursor or paginated reads piped into the CSV or PDF writer, never everything loaded first.

    Thirty seconds is still too long for a synchronous request. I'd make it a background job: return 202 with a job id and a status URL, and poll or notify when it's ready. That also handles two people asking for the same report. Key the job by its parameters, so a second request joins the job already running instead of starting another, and cache the result under that key until the data underneath changes.

    Where the remaining 30 seconds goes: [FILL: what it's spent on today]. If it's a live scan of raw rows, getting under 2 seconds, or sub-second, means not recomputing history. Keep summary tables or materialised views per day or hour, update them incrementally as records change or with a nightly job, and compute only today's slice live. The trade-off is freshness and an extra write path you must keep correct, against reports that answer from a few hundred summary rows.

    Heavy reports on the primary slow the app for everyone. The options in order: run them as background jobs off-peak, move them to a read replica, or move them to a warehouse once reporting becomes its own product. At 10 or 100 times the data, a live scan grows linearly with rows, so that's the first thing to break. With rollups the cost grows with the number of buckets, not rows, which is why precomputing is the long-term fix.

    To stop the regression coming back: a test that counts queries per report run so an N+1 can't sneak back in, a perf test on seeded production-size data in CI, and a slow-query alert in production. If I did it again, I'd want the query-count guard and the background-job shape from day one rather than after it hurt.

    DDIA ch3 "Aggregation: Data cubes and materialized views" (pdf p120–122) · postgresql.org/docs/current/using-explain.html · use-the-index-luke.com
  • NestJS backend: referrals, assessments, UniPile LinkedIn outreach; modules, guards, RBAC
    Answer

    At Jigsaw I built a NestJS backend for referrals, assessments and LinkedIn outreach through UniPile. NestJS is an opinionated framework on top of Express or Fastify. Express gives you routing and middleware and leaves structure to you; Nest adds modules, decorators and dependency injection. A module groups a feature's controllers and providers, controllers map routes, and providers are injectable services, so a service declares what it needs instead of importing singletons.

    Request order in Nest is middleware, then guards, then interceptors on the way in, then pipes, then the handler, then interceptors on the way out, with exception filters catching errors. Guards answer whether this caller may do this at all, which is where role checks live. Pipes validate and transform input. Interceptors wrap the call for logging, timing or response shaping. DTO validation belongs in a global ValidationPipe with class-validator: whitelist strips unknown fields, and forbidNonWhitelisted turns them into a 400, so a client can't slip in a field like role or companyId.

    [FILL: the actual module split, for example referrals, assessments, outreach, auth and users, and the actual roles, for example admin, recruiter, hiring manager, candidate.] In Nest, role-based access is normally a Roles decorator plus a RolesGuard that reads the required roles from route metadata and compares them with the role in the JWT. [FILL: confirm that's how yours worked, or say what differed.] The guard only says a recruiter may call this route. Whether this recruiter may touch this candidate is an ownership check in the service. For tenant isolation, the rule I hold to is that the company id comes from the token, never from the request body, every query is scoped by it through a scoped repository, and tests try another company's ids and expect 404 rather than 403, so you don't even leak that the record exists.

    [FILL: the referral flow, who refers, what's stored, which statuses it moves through.] [FILL: how assessments worked, question types, timer, scoring, who saw the result, and how video was recorded, stored and played back.] Two things I'd insist on whatever the details. The timer is enforced on the server, by storing the start time and deadline and rejecting a late submit, because a client timer is only a suggestion. And video goes straight from the browser to object storage on a short-lived signed URL, so the API never streams video, with playback through another signed URL and transcoding if the format needs it.

    Chat: [FILL: the transport, socket.io, raw WebSockets or a hosted service, and how messages and read receipts were stored]. The shape I'd describe is a room per conversation, messages stored before they're broadcast, and read receipts as a last-read message id per participant rather than a flag per message. At 10 times the users you run several Node instances, add the socket.io Redis adapter so an emit on one instance reaches sockets on the others, use sticky sessions if the polling fallback is on, and keep presence in Redis with a TTL.

    UniPile is one API over LinkedIn and email messaging through the user's own connected account. The reason to use it is that LinkedIn's own API keeps messaging and most profile data for approved partners. I ran into that on a small personal spike in November 2024, an Express app on my own GitHub, not Jigsaw code. It exchanged the authorization code at LinkedIn's token endpoint and called the OpenID userinfo endpoint; the education and skills calls I tried next are restricted for ordinary apps, so they stayed commented out. Honestly, it never stored or refreshed per-user tokens, and the client secret sat in a committed .env with an access token hard-coded in index.js; both need rotating and purging. The right design keeps the secret in a secret manager and stores each user's tokens encrypted at rest, keyed to the user, refreshing before expiry where the provider issues refresh tokens and asking the user to reconnect where it doesn't.

    Outreach: [FILL: how sends were scheduled, caps, delays, a queue, and how UniPile webhooks were handled]. The risk is getting the account flagged, so the design I'd defend is a queue with daily caps per account, randomised delays, working hours only, and the sequence stopping the moment someone replies. If a batch crashes halfway, every message has its own status row, pending, sent or failed, with an idempotency key of campaign plus recipient, so a restart sends only what's still pending and never sends twice. Webhooks like reply received or connection accepted get verified, de-duplicated by event id, acknowledged with a fast 200, then processed async to update status and stop follow-ups.

    Time to hire down 60 percent is a product number, not something my code measured on its own. [FILL: how it was measured, for example days from job open to offer, before versus after, over what period, and which features drove it.] The features on that resume line are interviews, video assessments, real-time chat and role-based access. On where Nest ended and the AI pipelines began: [FILL: whether they ran inside Nest or as a separate service, and how they talked].

    At StreetDine, where I can show it from code, roles were RESTAURANT, STAFF and RESTAURANTADMIN: Rohit's backend had a JWT middleware per role and separate staff endpoints with ownership checks, and my staff app gated screens on the client. On both, I'd now keep guards for coarse roles, a small policy layer in the service for ownership, and tenant scoping built into the repository so it can't be forgotten.

    docs.nestjs.com/guards · docs.nestjs.com/modules · career/profile/resume.tex (Jigsaw)
  • TypeScript migration strategy (incremental, allowJs, strict flags), SEO +30%
    Answer

    I migrated the Jigsaw codebase to TypeScript. [FILL: which codebase, the Next.js app, the Node backend or both, and confirm it was incremental with no feature freeze.] The why is simple. Shape errors get caught at compile time instead of in production, refactors become safe because the compiler finds every caller, and the types document the API for the next developer. For a growing team, that last one matters as much as bugs.

    The tsconfig switches: allowJs lets .js and .ts files live in one project and import each other. checkJs type-checks the JavaScript files too, using inference and JSDoc comments, so you get errors in files you haven't converted yet, and JSDoc types are the bridge for those files. strict is the family of flags, noImplicitAny, strictNullChecks, strictFunctionTypes and others. any switches checking off. unknown means I don't know yet, so you must narrow it before using it, which makes unknown the right type at boundaries.

    Big-bang versus incremental: a big-bang rewrite stops feature work and lands one huge risky diff. Incremental keeps shipping and lets the team learn as it goes, at the cost of a long mixed period. The incremental path I'd walk through: tsconfig with allowJs and a CI type-check, tsc with noEmit, so nothing new can break the build. Then convert leaves first, utils, constants and models, and move up the dependency graph to services and then pages or controllers, because each conversion makes everything above it better typed. Every new file is TypeScript, and a file gets converted when someone is already touching it for a feature, so the migration rides along with feature work instead of competing with it.

    To keep new any out: no-explicit-any as a lint error for new code, review, and tracking the any count so it only goes down. Progress is measurable as files converted, the any count, a type-coverage tool's percentage, and which strict flags are on. If strict suddenly shows 2,000 errors, don't fix them in one go. Take a baseline, turn strict on per folder, allow a small expect-error budget with a ticket each, and ratchet the budget down every sprint.

    Types lie at the boundaries, and that's where runtime still breaks even when everything type-checks. A request body, a JSON.parse, a third-party response or a database document is whatever the outside sends, and an as cast just silences the compiler. The fix is validation at the edge, DTOs with class-validator in Nest or a schema parse, so the inside of the system trusts validated types. A big legacy object whose shape changes across the code becomes a discriminated union on a kind field, or starts loose and gets narrowed module by module. For a Mongoose or Sequelize model layer, write an interface per document and pass it into the model generics, or infer the type from the schema, so queries return typed documents. Frontend and backend stay in agreement through a shared types package, or better, types generated from the backend's DTOs or OpenAPI spec. [FILL: one real bug TypeScript caught after the migration, and one runtime break that still type-checked.]

    Adoption is about review and conventions more than training: a short written guide, the first few conversions done together, and review comments that point at the rule. [FILL: how you actually got the team on board, and how you justified a sprint the migration slowed.] [FILL: any numbers on bug rates or onboarding time; if none were measured, say so.]

    SEO: the Core Web Vitals are LCP, how fast the main content paints, under 2.5 seconds is good; INP, how fast the page responds to input, under 200 ms; and CLS, how much the layout jumps, under 0.1. For SEO, SSR and SSG beat client-side rendering because the crawler gets real HTML with content and meta tags instead of an empty shell waiting on JavaScript. The 30 percent: [FILL: the metric, for example Search Console clicks or impressions or a Lighthouse SEO score, the period, and what changed, SSR or SSG pages, meta tags, sitemap, structured data, page speed]. If I can't name the metric and the period, I shouldn't quote the number.

    If I did it again, I'd turn on strictNullChecks early, because null bugs are the most common ones, and generate API types from day one instead of hand-writing them on both sides.

    typescriptlang.org/docs/handbook/migrating-from-javascript.html · typescriptlang.org/tsconfig#strict · developers.google.com/search/docs/fundamentals/seo-starter-guide
  • Mentoring 3 developers — code review practices
    Answer

    I mentored three developers at Jigsaw. [FILL: their level, and one real example: what a mentee struggled with and what changed.] [FILL: what a normal week looked like, for example reviews on every pull request, a weekly one-to-one, pairing on the hard tickets.]

    In a review, I look at the contract first. Does it do what the ticket says, what happens on the edges, empty input, failure, retries, and does it touch a boundary like auth, tenant data or money. Then design: is this the right place for the logic, will the next person understand it. Naming and style come last, and as much of that as possible should be a lint rule, not a comment. Comments should explain why, and I try to make every one teach a reason rather than just ask for a change. With a newer developer I also ask them to walk me through why each change is there. If they can't explain a line, that's the conversation to have, not the diff.

    If a mentee keeps shipping the same kind of bug, fixing it in review each time isn't working. I'd sit with them on the next one and find out whether it's a gap in understanding or a missing guardrail. Then I'd make that class of bug hard to write: a test, a lint rule, or a helper that does it right by default. And I'd write the rule down. That's how we work at AiRA today, where I lead a four-person team: the review rules live in AGENTS.md with numbered codes, so a review comment can point at a rule instead of a personal preference.

    google.github.io/eng-practices/review/ · career/profile/resume.tex (Jigsaw)

1.7 StreetDine — restaurant software

  • Live order board: realtime updates (sockets), order state machine (new → accepted → ready → served → closed)
    Answer

    StreetDine was a restaurant ordering startup where I was a founding member alongside Rohit Raj, who did the architecture and most backend domains, and Mukesh Negi, who did most of the visual UI. I was full-time from January 2024, and after I joined Jigsaw in August 2024 I kept contributing part-time until April 2025, which is why the dates overlap. The partner web app is what restaurants ran service on: owners, counter staff, waiters on their phones, and a kitchen screen. My core work there was the live order board and the order lifecycle behind it; 380 of that repo's 732 commits are mine.

    Each order is a card, and the main button changes with its state. The five actions are accept, as Accept Paid Order or Accept Payment Later, Mark Order Ready, served, Accept Cash and Close Order. Around those sit assign waiter, change table, add items, cancel an item and print. Dine-in and take-away differ in serving: a dine-in item can be marked served once the order is accepted or ready, because a waiter brings things to the table as they come, while a take-away item is only handed over once the order is READY. Accept-cash-before only appears for a dine-in, unpaid, pending order, and never for staff. So the button is a function of status, dine-in or take-away, the paid flag and the role. A KOT is a kitchen order ticket, the slip that tells the kitchen what to cook.

    The statuses were PENDING, ACCEPTED, PREPARING, PREPARED, READY and SERVED, and closing sets is_order_open to false and frees the table. Illegal moves are things like going backwards, closing a pending order, or serving a take-away before it's ready. Here's the honest part: those rules lived in my frontend helpers, not on the server. Rohit's status handler checked who you were, the assigned staff member or the owning restaurant, then wrote whatever status the body sent. A button is a suggestion; it has to be a transition table on the server plus a conditional update on the current status.

    Add more items is modelled as order_version: each batch of new items gets the next version, the kitchen screen groups items by batch, and each batch prints its own KOT, so the kitchen sees only what's new. Cancelling one item leaves it on the order, struck through; my isAllItemsServed counts cancelled items as done, and the amount helpers exclude them from the bill. Waiters, the STAFF role, see new orders plus their own, and can't assign staff, change a pending order's table, take payment up front, or see menu, staff, reports or settings; owners see everything and assign waiters.

    Realtime was socket.io with one room per restaurant. Staff join their associated_restaurant, and the kitchen screen is the same app, so it sits in the same room. Rohit wrote the socket client and the server; I wired it into every order view, restaurant and staff. A mutation emits an event carrying just the restaurant and the order id, and every open screen in that room refetches its list. I chose refetch over applying the payload because the database is the truth and you never apply an event to the wrong state; the cost is an extra request per device per event. I added an in-flight guard, isGetOrderLoading, against refetch storms, but it was a plain variable, not a ref, and later I took it out of the check, so today every event refetches. A ref-based flag with one trailing refetch, or a short debounce, is the real fix. The device that made the change also refreshes itself locally, so it never waits on the round trip.

    Only the clicked button spins, because loading flags are keyed by order or item id through meta.arg. Long lists got slow, so I made each card render lazily with IntersectionObserver. Push and sockets both refresh because they cover different cases: OneSignal push reaches a backgrounded app, and the socket keeps open screens in sync without a notification.

    If the DB changed but the board didn't, start with the fact that the server is only a relay. It rebroadcasts what a client emits after its own mutation, so a change made any other way, like an order created by the payment webhook, emits nothing on the socket and only arrives through push. Next, reconnects: socket.io reconnects the transport by itself, but my connect_error and disconnect handlers only log, and the room join happens on profile load, so after a reconnect the socket isn't back in the restaurant room until the page reloads. That's a real gap; rejoin in a connect handler. So the socket should be the signal and the database the truth. Change streams fix the root cause: MongoDB pushes every write on the orders collection to the server, which emits to the restaurant room whoever wrote it, with a resume token for reconnects.

    The same roughly 15 order handlers ended up copy-pasted across about 8 hooks. That's largely on me: after Rohit's restructure each view got its own hook, and I added each action to each one as features landed. I'd refactor into one shared order-actions hook that takes the order and a refresh callback and maps the role to the right endpoint. For an audit trail I'd $push a status-history entry, from, to, who and when, in the same atomic update as the status change. Full event sourcing gives replay and history, but costs projections and migrations a restaurant doesn't need. At 100 times the scale: server-emitted events from change streams, the Redis adapter across socket nodes, auth on the socket handshake and room joins, which today aren't checked, server-side transitions, and indexes on restaurant and status.

    DHANESH_PERSONAL_AND_PRIOR_WORK.md "1. Live order dashboard and the order state machine" + "10. Real-time order sync with socket.io" · socket.io/docs/v4/ · DDIA ch11 "Event sourcing" (pdf p464–466)
  • Reporting APIs: one shared aggregation pipeline replacing 4 copies
    Answer

    The reporting API is my strongest StreetDine backend work: 689 of the 785 lines in the dashboard controller are mine by blame. It's on MongoDB with Mongoose. Owners got sales, orders, staff, tables and payments, each bucketed by hour, weekday, month or year, or a custom day range, plus trending categories and items, dine-in versus take-away, top staff, busiest tables, cash versus UPI, and the last 10 transactions.

    An aggregation pipeline is a list of stages each document flows through. I used $match, $lookup, $unwind, $project, $group, $sort and $limit. $lookup is a join to another collection, per document. It's fine when the foreign field is indexed, like _id, but it costs a lookup for every document that reaches it, and $unwind after it multiplies the rows. That's why $match goes first: it can use an index and cut the set before anything expensive runs. A report on restaurant plus a createdAt window wants a compound index on restaurant then createdAt, equality first, then range. Honestly, the order and payment models declared no index beyond _id. I ran explain("executionStats") while building the pipelines, IXSCAN versus COLLSCAN and documents examined versus returned, then took it out. Adding that index is the first thing I'd do.

    The shared pipeline came from a refactor I did in April 2024. Each report had four near-identical copies, one per range. I replaced them with one base pipeline: $match on restaurant and the window, $lookup the joined collection, $unwind, and a $project that adds the time-zone offset to make a local timestamp. Then a switch (range) pushes the range's stages: today groups by $hour, weekly by $dayOfWeek, monthly by year and month, yearly by $year, and custom by day, month and year. The commit shows 715 lines deleted and 129 added, but I'd be precise if asked to show the diff: that commit also moved the other reports off that branch, and they came back on their own branches the same day. The dedup itself took the sales report from about 320 lines to about 215. One route per report then dispatches on body.type, graph, chart or trending, so one endpoint serves every view of that report.

    The revenue source changed twice. First it was the order's own amount. In June 2024 I moved to quantity times variant price over the order items, so it reflected what was actually ordered. In December 2024 I rebuilt it on the payments joined to each order, so reports count money actually collected, which is what an owner closes the till against. Money is stored in paise, as integers, because floats can't represent 0.1 rupee exactly and errors add up across thousands of orders. The backend does the paise maths and the app divides by 100 for display.

    Time zones were hard-coded IST at first. Then the client sent its IANA zone and the server worked out the offset. That fix has a real bug: the offset is computed once, for now. Across a daylight-saving change, buckets on the other side shift by an hour. India has no DST, so it never showed, but it's wrong. The right fix is passing the timezone into the date operators, since $hour and friends accept one, and computing day boundaries in the restaurant's zone instead of the server's.

    Two more bugs I'd own up to in review. The staff, table, trending and payments pipelines don't $match on the restaurant, so they aggregate across every tenant: a data leak and wrong totals. Every query has to be scoped. And bad range input returns 500 instead of 400. That matters because 500 says we broke, it pages the wrong people and invites retries, while 400 tells the client to fix its request. A smaller one: after $unwind on payments, an order paid in two parts is counted twice in the count.

    Every owner opens reports at closing time, so the load spikes all at once. I'd pre-aggregate into an hourly rollup collection keyed by restaurant and hour, updated with $inc when an order closes or a payment lands, or by a cron with $merge. Reports then sum a few dozen rollup rows instead of scanning orders. To test pipelines: seeded fixtures with orders at known times, expected buckets asserted exactly, plus an empty range, orders either side of midnight in the restaurant's zone, a DST date, and a second restaurant whose data must never appear.

    DHANESH_PERSONAL_AND_PRIOR_WORK.md "8. Reports and analytics" · mongodb.com/docs/manual/aggregation/ · DDIA ch3 "Aggregation: Data cubes and materialized views" (pdf p120–122)
  • Concurrency: two waiters updating one order
    Answer

    Today both waiters would get a success and the last write wins. That's a lost update, and the fix is a conditional update where the loser gets a 409. In the StreetDine backend the staff accept handler, Rohit's on main, reads the order, checks whether assigned is set, and then does an unconditional findByIdAndUpdate. Two requests at the same moment both read unassigned, both pass the check, and both write. Each waiter sees Order accepted and walks to the table, and the order ends up assigned to whoever wrote second. The general status handler has the same read-then-write shape. My own change on a branch, assign the staff member when they accept an unassigned order, kept that shape too, so I'm not claiming I'd solved it.

    The fix is to put the check inside the write: findOneAndUpdate filtered on the order id, the restaurant, assigned: null and status: PENDING, setting assigned and ACCEPTED in one atomic operation. A single-document update is atomic in MongoDB, so exactly one request matches. The other gets null back and returns 409 Conflict with the current order, and the app refetches and shows who took it. Today that case returns a 500 with Staff already assigned, which reads like a server failure.

    For general edits to an order I'd add a version number, put it in the filter of every update and $inc it, so a stale screen can't overwrite a newer change. Mongoose's optimisticConcurrency option does the same thing, but only for save(). The socket refresh tells the other waiter quickly, but it can't stop the race; only the conditional write can.

    DDIA ch7 "Preventing lost updates" (pdf p255–259) · DBI ch5 "Optimistic Concurrency Control" (pdf p174–176)
  • Payments: Razorpay order → checkout → webhook, signature over the raw body, item snapshots, cash and partial payments, idempotency
    Answer

    Razorpay works like this. Our server creates a Razorpay order for the amount, the browser opens Razorpay Checkout with that order id, the user pays, and Razorpay tells our server through a signed webhook. The server is what decides the order is paid. A webhook is the provider calling our endpoint when something happens, so we learn about the payment even if the user's tab is gone. You never trust the browser's success callback, because anyone can call it, fake it, or close the tab before it runs.
