    I built the customer-side checkout end to end. Proceed Payment re-posts the cart with the order options, take-away, vehicle number for dine-in-car, cooking instructions. Then Pay online calls POST /payments/create-online-payment/:cartId; Rohit's backend creates the Razorpay order and returns its id and amount. I load checkout.js on demand, open Checkout with that order id, and restrict methods to card and UPI by switching off netbanking, wallets, EMI and pay-later. The case for that restriction is that card and UPI confirm straight away, while the others add redirects and slower, messier failure modes.

    On success, the handler doesn't create anything. It redirects to the cart page with the Razorpay order id, shows Validating payment, and polls the cart every 3 seconds until the backend webhook has turned the cart into an order, then routes to the order. My first version, in June 2024, posted the order id, payment id and signature for a server-side check instead. The signature is HMAC-SHA256 of order_id, a pipe, then payment_id, keyed with the secret, compared with the signature Razorpay returns. I switched to webhook plus polling so a closed tab or a forged callback can't create a paid order. My HMAC check and my webhook rewrite stayed on branches; the webhook on main is Rohit's.

    A webhook signature has to be checked over the raw body. Razorpay signs the exact bytes it sent. Re-serialising the parsed object with JSON.stringify can change key order, spacing or number formatting, and then a genuine webhook fails or the check means less than you think. Both the main webhook and my branch version did that after express.json(). The fix is express.raw on that one route, or capturing the raw buffer in the json parser's verify hook.

    The order itself comes from my service, createOrderFromCartOnPaymentSuccess. It snapshots every cart line into an OrderItem: name, category, veg flag, discount, image, variant name and price, the take-away flag, order_version 1, because a later menu or price edit must never change a past order or its bill. It takes the next per-restaurant token with an atomic $inc upsert, links the payment and closes the cart. Cash went through three designs: a CASH payment for the total, then a pending payment on an unpaid order, and finally an unpaid order with no payment row, which won because the money isn't collected until staff collect it. When staff accept cash, the due is the sum of non-cancelled items after discount, in paise, minus payments already on the order, so partial and repeat payments add up.

    A bug I fixed: the Razorpay key was read from a plain process.env variable, and Next.js only exposes variables prefixed NEXT_PUBLIC_ to the browser, so checkout had no key. It moved to NEXT_PUBLIC_RAZORPAY_API_KEY, and I replaced hard-coded demo prefill details with the logged-in user's.

    The gaps, honestly. The webhook isn't idempotent today. A duplicate delivery saves a second payment and creates a second order with a new token, because nothing checks whether the cart already has an order. The fix is a unique index on the Razorpay payment id, a check that the cart is still active inside the same step, and answering a duplicate with 200 so Razorpay stops retrying. If the webhook never arrives, there's no timeout on the polling. I'd stop after a minute or two, show we're confirming your payment, don't pay again, and run a reconciliation cron that asks Razorpay's API about any payment request without a result. If payment succeeds but order creation fails, the payment row is already saved, which is right, and the handler returns 500 so Razorpay retries; with idempotency in place that recovers cleanly, plus an alert, and a refund if the order can never be made.

    Refunds for items cancelled after payment: the partner app already works out refund owed when cancelled items drop the due below what's paid. The backend side I'd add is a partial refund through Razorpay's refund API on that payment id, for that amount in paise, recorded as a refund row against the order so reports and the bill both see it.

  • PWA and staff app: service worker caching, push worker scope, KOT printing, role guards, Netlify static export
    Answer

    A PWA is a website that installs and behaves like an app. To be installable it needs HTTPS, a web manifest with a name, icons, a start URL and a standalone display mode, and on most browsers a service worker. A service worker is a script the browser runs apart from the page. It can intercept network requests, serve from a cache, receive push messages and run sync events when no tab is open, none of which a page can do. Cache-first is for things that rarely change, like icons and fonts. Network-first is for data that must be fresh, like orders, with the cache only as a fallback. A Trusted Web Activity wraps a PWA in an Android app for the Play Store, full screen in Chrome, verified through assetlinks.json.

    At StreetDine the PWA came in layers. In March 2024 I set up the first version, next-pwa with Workbox, the manifest and icon sets. In October 2024 Rohit re-set it up with next-pwa 5.6, the static export and assetlinks.json for the Android TWA. In December 2024 I wrote the hand-made service worker. Offline really meant the app shell and icons, not orders. It precached the icon set and served it cache-first. There was no offline order queue. Background Sync replays a queued action when the network returns, and Periodic Background Sync wakes the worker on a schedule. I registered a sync-orders tag and a periodic sync with a 24-hour minimum interval, with feature detection, but both handlers only post SYNC_ORDERS to open tabs so they refetch. No writes are queued.

    The push worker needed its own scope because only one service worker controls a scope. Register OneSignal's worker and the PWA's at / and they replace each other, so I moved OneSignal to /onesignal-push/. Two problems I'd fix. next-pwa's sw.js and my service-worker.js were still both at /. And my activate step deleted every cache, not just old versions. Name caches with a version and delete only the ones that don't match, otherwise every update throws away caches you still need.

    The staff app was a separate STAFF-role surface: its own dashboard, My Orders, a staff POS, and a parallel staff API slice. The client hides screens, but the real enforcement is on the server, with a staff JWT middleware, separate staff endpoints and ownership checks in the handlers. The weak spot was the client guard. After a Next middleware guard lived for five days in February 2024, role checks ended up as hooks that wait setTimeout(500) and then redirect. That's a race: on a slow load auth isn't ready at 500 ms and you bounce a logged-in user, or on a fast one you flash a screen you shouldn't. The fix is one route guard that waits on the auth loading state, not a timer, or middleware on a cookie.

    Printing used react-to-print with a hidden print component. A shared printData(order, version) prints either an invoice or a KOT, and the KOT filters items to that order_version, so each add-items batch prints only the new items for the kitchen. If the restaurant's kot_order_print setting is on, creating an order or adding items auto-prints.

    Netlify static export: output: export pre-renders pages, but a dynamic route like an order id has no HTML file for ids that didn't exist at build time, so a refresh or a deep link 404s. The fix is rewrites in a _redirects file that send those paths to the shell page, and the client reads the id. The trap, and it was my commit: in the partner app I named the file _rereedirects, and Netlify silently ignores a misspelt file. The customer app had it right.

    Street-vendor mode is a one-tap dashboard for a single-person stall. One call creates the order, records a CASH payment, marks it served, paid and closed, and takes a token, and the app prints the bill. It's a separate endpoint because it's one atomic business step with its own rule, only street-food restaurants may use it, and chaining four normal calls from a phone could fail halfway and leave a half-made order. I built the frontend in May and August 2024. The backend endpoint is mine too, but it stayed on a branch and was never merged.

    For real offline for waiters, I'd keep an IndexedDB outbox of actions, each with a client-generated id as an idempotency key, replay them in order when the network returns, have the server de-duplicate on that id and reject illegal transitions, and show pending state on the card. On iOS that's harder. Web push only arrived in iOS 16.4 and only for home-screen apps, there's no Background Sync, and Safari can evict storage for sites you haven't used in a while. Our iOS support was icons and a manifest, nothing more. With no tests and no CI anywhere, I'd add CI first, lint plus the static-export build, which would have caught broken routes. Then backend tests on the money and state logic, since that's where bugs cost real money: amount due in paise, order transitions, webhook idempotency and report buckets.

1.8 Side project — Perplexity Mini

  • Redis queue → Playwright crawler → Qdrant → Llama 3.1 behind nginx
    Answer

    Perplexity Mini is a small answer engine I built alone, September 19 to October 6, 2025, over a web index that refreshes itself nightly. A Redis queue feeds a Playwright crawler that opens 10 pages at a time, pages become 384-dimension vectors in Qdrant, the same vector DB we use at AiRA, and Llama 3.1 8B on Ollama behind nginx answers.

    POST /api/urls takes {url, depth} and does an LPUSH of that JSON onto the Redis list urls_queue. A Redis list works as a queue because LPUSH adds at one end and BRPOP pops from the other, blocking until there is an item, so the worker never polls. A blocking pop holds its connection, so the worker gets its own subscriber connection, separate from the publisher. Jobs deeper than 1 are dropped. A p-queue with concurrency 10 opens a page per URL on one shared headless Chromium (domcontentloaded, 60 second timeout) and takes the title, document.body.innerText and every a[href]. I upsert a Mongo Url row, cut 500-character chunks with 50 of overlap, extract topics, embed, and upsert chunks into Qdrant with url_id, text, index, topic_ids and updated_at. If the depth was 0, every outlink goes back on the queue at depth 1. That's one hop, because outlinks multiply fast and the second hop drifts off topic.

    Playwright drives a real browser, which beats requests plus BeautifulSoup on pages that build content with JavaScript; the cost is that every page is a heavy, slow tab. Cleaning was basic: innerText gives rendered text but keeps menus and cookie banners, so I'd add a readability-style extractor. Backpressure is weak: the BRPOP loop calls the handler without awaiting it, so Redis drains as fast as it can pop, jobs pile up in memory, and only the p-queue bounds open pages. And BRPOP removes the job with no ack, so if the process dies, that URL and everything waiting in the p-queue is gone. The fix is LMOVE into a processing list, removed on success, with a reaper for stale entries, or Redis Streams with a consumer group and XACK. Redis still earns its place: it decouples the API from slow crawling, absorbs bursts, and lets the crawler feed links back in.

    Topics are extracted per page: the chunks go to DeepSeek V3.1 through OpenRouter, which returns 3 to 5 broad lowercase topics with 2 to 5 tags each as schema-constrained JSON. Then a merge-upsert into a Qdrant topics collection: existing topics keep their id and get the union of tags, new ones get a UUID, and each is embedded on its tags. The embedder is a FastAPI sidecar on Sentence Transformers paraphrase-MiniLM-L12-v2: 384 dimensions, cosine distance, small, free and fast on CPU, at the price of retrieval quality. 500 characters suits that model's short input, and the overlap keeps a sentence cut at a boundary whole in one chunk; splitting by characters rather than sentences is a weakness. Mongo keeps URL, title, links, depth, status and sourceTopic.

    Refresh is three node-cron jobs. At midnight, ask the Perplexity Sonar API for 10 current URLs per known topic, inserted at depth 0. At 06:00, re-enqueue every URL with depth up to 1. At 05:00 and 17:00, delete URL rows found more than 2 days ago. It never detects change: it re-embeds everything with random point ids, so a recrawl adds duplicates. The fix is a content hash per page to skip unchanged ones, ids derived from URL plus chunk index, and a delete by url_id when a page changes or disappears. The cleanup once cascaded to Qdrant like that, but I removed the cascade, so today Mongo rows go and their vectors stay: answers can cite deleted pages and the index only grows. Same family: status is set to visiting and never to visited, so I can't tell done from crashed and nothing can retry. I'd add visited and failed states with attempts and a lease time.

    Ollama runs open models locally; I chose it as free, private and unlimited, at the cost of weaker 8B answers and latency I never measured. nginx is the reverse proxy: an upstream with least_conn over two Ollama instances, 3600 second proxy timeouts and a 500 MB body limit. I didn't set TLS, rate limiting or proxy_buffering off. Citations, honestly, didn't exist: the context was chunk text joined together with no URLs. I'd number each chunk with its URL, ask for [n] citations, and send the source list as an SSE event.

    The Compose file runs mongo, qdrant, redis and nginx. The three data stores have named volumes, nginx mounts its config read-only, there are no healthchecks, and all four publish ports: 27017, 6333, 6379 and 8080 to 80. The Node app and the embedder ran outside it. Only nginx should be published; the rest belongs on the internal Compose network, because an open, unauthenticated Redis or Mongo is a classic breach. I never built a crawler image; I'd start from the official Playwright image, install only Chromium, and raise --shm-size, because Chromium crashes on Docker's small default.

    Politeness: there's no robots.txt check or per-domain limit today; I'd add cached robots.txt, per-domain rate limits, backoff on 429 and 503, and an honest user agent. Dedupe is only the unique URL; I'd add canonical URLs, a content hash, and SimHash or MinHash. Prompt injection: a page saying "ignore previous instructions" lands straight in the context, and "use only the context" doesn't help, because that text is the context. I'd fence context as quoted data, flag instruction-like text at ingestion, and show citations. With no tools, the worst case is a wrong answer.

    DHANESH_PERSONAL_AND_PRIOR_WORK.md summary "Perplexity Mini" · github.com/Dhanesh-Pottekula/perplexity_mini · LEH ch4 "The vanilla RAG framework" (pdf p130–136)
  • Why each piece; what you'd change for production
    Answer

    Every piece was the simplest thing that let me learn the whole loop alone, and for production I'd change the queue, the serving and the retrieval first. On the queue: a Redis list is a pop with no ack, and I already had Redis. Redis Streams add consumer groups, acks, a pending list and replay. BullMQ is the Node library on Redis that gives you retries, delays, rate limits and concurrency out of the box, so for a Node crawler it is the practical next step. Kafka is a partitioned durable log for very high throughput and many independent consumers; it's overkill until several systems read the same events. I'd switch the moment a lost URL matters, which in production is day one.

    The embedding service is Python because Sentence Transformers and the ML ecosystem are Python, while the crawler and API are I/O-heavy and fine in Node. It also started life as a full Python pipeline; I later moved topic extraction to Node and left Python with embeddings only. The cost is one more service and an HTTP hop per batch. Local Llama 3.1 8B on Ollama was a choice for zero cost, privacy, no rate limits and learning. The trade-off is quality (an 8B model synthesises grounded answers worse than an API model) and speed on my own hardware, which I never benchmarked. At 1,000 users I'd move answers to an API model or to a GPU server with continuous batching such as vLLM, because Ollama on one box serves very few generations at once.

    Two Ollama servers behind nginx least_conn: answer lengths vary a lot. Round-robin can stack a long generation behind another long one, while least_conn sends each request to the server with fewer open connections. To be honest, both instances ran on one LAN machine on two ports, so it spread load across two model processes, not two machines. Compose ran mongo, qdrant, redis and nginx; the Node app and the embedder ran outside. Data stores are trivial to containerise, the app was still changing hourly under tsx and PM2, and Playwright in Docker needs extra care. I deployed without Kubernetes in the sense that it only ever ran on my machines; there are Minikube notes in the README but no manifests. What broke was found by reading back, not in production. A fresh install fails because the topics collection is no longer created after my move to Node. The two-stage retrieval silently became a flat search. The cleanup job leaves orphan vectors.

    Could it crawl 4,000 pages a minute? No, and that number is off my resume because nothing measured it. 4,000 a minute is about 67 pages a second. I have 10 browser pages at once, each allowed up to 60 seconds, plus one LLM topic call per page through OpenRouter and an embedding call. Even if a page took a single second, which I never measured, 10 slots give about 600 pages a minute before the LLM step, and the LLM step would need about 67 calls a second. To get near it: plain HTTP fetch first and a browser only for JavaScript pages, several crawler processes, batch or drop the per-page topic call, and per-domain limits so speed does not become abuse.

    How many users could it serve? I never load-tested it, so I reason about it. What breaks first is Ollama: each answer holds the model for the whole generation, with a prompt of up to 100 chunks, so a handful of concurrent users queue up. Next is crawler throughput, since the crawler shares the same Node process as the API. 10,000 users a day is only about 0.1 requests a second on average, maybe 1 to 2 at peak, so the fix is not exotic. Split API and crawler workers, use a queue with acks, put serving on a GPU or an API, trim context to 5 to 10 reranked chunks, cache embeddings and repeat answers, stream real tokens, publish only nginx with auth and rate limits, and run managed Qdrant and Mongo with backups and metrics.

    Retrieval was dense only. Queries with exact tokens fail on dense-only search: version numbers, product codes, rare names. A paraphrase model blurs them toward similar-sounding text. I didn't keep a log of failed queries, so I can't quote one. I'd add a sparse BM25-style vector in Qdrant next to the dense one and fuse with reciprocal rank fusion. Evaluation, honestly, was me eyeballing answers. Properly: a golden set of questions with their known source pages, recall at k for retrieval, faithfulness and answer relevance with an LLM judge spot-checked by hand, run on every change.

    If it became agentic, the index turns into a cache. I already had a search endpoint that fans queries out in parallel to Perplexity Sonar and normalises results to {title, url, snippet}, capped at 25 per query. The model would decide to search, fetch the top pages live, answer with citations, and write useful pages back into Qdrant. The costs are latency and per-query API spend, plus a step budget, a loop limit and stronger prompt-injection defences, because live pages are untrusted input.

    LEH ch10 "Monolithic versus microservices architecture in model serving" (pdf p392–397) · HOL ch8 "Example: RAG with Local Models" (pdf p274–277) · qdrant.tech/documentation/
  • Retrieval and answers: 384-d embeddings, Qdrant search, Llama 3.1 on two Ollama servers behind nginx least_conn, SSE, grounding
    Answer

    RAG means you retrieve relevant text first and make the model answer from it. The loop: embed the question, search a vector index, put the top chunks in the prompt, generate, and ideally cite. In Perplexity Mini the vectors are 384-dimensional from Sentence Transformers paraphrase-MiniLM-L12-v2, stored in Qdrant with cosine distance. Cosine compares direction, not length, which is what sentence-embedding models are trained for. On normalised vectors cosine and dot product give the same ranking, and Euclidean ranks the same too. Answers come from Llama 3.1 8B on two Ollama servers behind nginx, and least_conn sends each request to the server with the fewest open connections.

    One question end to end: POST /api/chat embeds the query through the Python embedder. It searches the topics collection (top 100, score at least 0.5), gathers those topics' url_ids into a Qdrant match.any filter, then searches the chunk collection (top 100, score at least 0.1) under that filter. It joins the chunk texts into a context and prompts Llama. The system prompt says to use only the context, prefer the newest information, and say "I don't know" if it is not there. The reply goes back over SSE: data: frames, then event: end with [DONE], and writing stops if the client disconnects. nginx had 3600 second proxy timeouts because a local 8B model with a huge prompt can easily pass nginx's default 60 second read timeout when nothing comes back until generation ends. An hour is far too generous; with real streaming a few minutes is plenty.

    SSE is a plain HTTP response kept open with text/event-stream: the server writes data: lines and the browser's EventSource reconnects on its own. An answer only flows one way, so SSE is simpler than a WebSocket and passes through ordinary proxies. But I have to be straight: it was not real streaming. Ollama was called with stream: false, and I cut the finished reply into 100-character pieces with an async generator. Time to first token equals the full generation time. The fix is stream: true, piping tokens as they arrive, and proxy_buffering off in nginx so the proxy doesn't hold them back.

    Against making things up I had only the prompt rule (use only the context, say you don't know) and score thresholds. The chunk threshold of 0.1 is effectively none, and there were no citations, so a user couldn't check anything. For failures, the Ollama chat call went through exponential backoff with a 1 second base and a 30 second cap, 3 retries by default. The Ollama service also capped structured calls with a p-queue of 5, but topic extraction had moved to OpenRouter, which had neither retries nor a cap, so overload protection was thin.

    The bug I'm least proud of: after I moved topic extraction from Python to Node, the topic upsert stopped writing url_ids onto topic points. The filter always came out undefined, and two-stage retrieval silently became a flat top-100 search. Nothing failed, so nothing told me. I'd catch it with a small retrieval eval (known question, expected page, recall at k), by logging the filter size per query and warning when it's empty, and with an integration test that topics carry url_ids after ingestion.

    Top 100 chunks at a 0.1 score into an 8B model is wrong twice. Most of that is noise that dilutes the answer. And 100 chunks of 500 characters is roughly 50,000 characters. Ollama's default context window is a few thousand tokens unless you raise num_ctx, so most of it gets cut off silently. Better: retrieve maybe 50, rerank with a cross-encoder, keep 5 to 10, drop near-duplicate chunks from the same page, and size the context on purpose.

    Evaluation, properly: a golden set of questions with known source pages and reference answers. Measure context recall and precision for retrieval, and faithfulness and relevance for answers with an LLM judge, spot-checked by hand. Run it on every change, which is exactly what would have caught the refactor. Hybrid search: keep the dense vector, add a sparse BM25 or SPLADE vector in Qdrant, query both, and merge with reciprocal rank fusion so exact names and numbers still match.

    Last lesson: a fresh install fails because nothing creates the topics collection anymore. The Python startup that made it was deleted when I moved to Node, and Node only creates the chunk collections. The Qdrant wrapper logged the failed upsert and returned false, so it failed quietly. The lesson is idempotent startup that creates everything it uses, fails loudly, and is tested from an empty environment. At AiRA we create our Qdrant payload indexes at boot in the entity that owns them, safe to re-run, and an error there is suppressed instead of failing startup.

1.9 AiRA — sign-in, connectors and secrets (the important flows)

Each flow is drawn step by step, with its code files, in ~/Desktop/airaai/aira_temp/docs/AIRA_FLOWS_INTERVIEW_PREP.md (shown below as FLOWS §N). Study order: tools → Key Vault → session → Google → Apple → email_token → connectors → Gmail push → PKCE → vault → WebSocket → files. Short on time: do the crypto tools, Key Vault, Google native, Gmail push and the vault.

  • The three crypto tools: tokenize (HMAC-SHA256, one-way, to search), encrypt_transient (AES-256-GCM, two-way, to store), signed JWT (sessions, short codes) — and why a plain SHA-256 of an email is not enough
    Answer

    We use three different tools, and the skill is knowing which job each one does. tokenize is HMAC-SHA256 with a secret key: one-way and deterministic, so the same email always gives the same token, which means I can put a unique index on it and search by it. encrypt_transient is AES-256-GCM with a fresh random 12-byte nonce on every call: two-way, but the same email encrypts differently every time, so you can store it and read it back, but you can't search on it. And the session is a signed JWT: anyone can read it, nobody can change it without our key. All three live in crypto_adp.py. My teammate wrote that base; I added the bound variants later for the credential vault.

    So a user's email is stored twice in Mongo: an email_token to find them and an encrypted_email to read the real address when we actually need it. Google and Apple refresh tokens and connector access tokens are stored encrypted the same way. The plain email never sits in the database.

    Why not a plain SHA-256 of the email? Because emails are guessable. Someone with a leaked table can hash a list of a few million common addresses and join on it, and they'd have most of our users in minutes. HMAC needs our secret, which lives in Key Vault, so a database dump alone doesn't let you test guesses. We also lower-case and strip before tokenizing, otherwise A@Gmail.com with a trailing space and a@gmail.com would become two accounts.

    For Qdrant I want to be precise, because it changed. In our 2025 memory system, memory payloads in Qdrant were stored as encrypted envelopes, with HMAC tokens for lookups, and my part there was making the read side decrypt concurrently, into deep copies, across six collections. In the current memory code, Qdrant payloads are stored as plain JSON and isolated by a user_id filter on every query, and that same filter is what account deletion uses to purge them. The strong per-user encryption today is the credential vault, where every user has their own data key wrapped by Key Vault.

    Two weak spots I'd name. tokenize and the session JWT are keyed by the same tenant_secret; I'd split them so one key does one job and a rotation of one doesn't touch the other. And if I wanted memory text protected against a Qdrant leak again, I'd bring back field encryption on the text fields and keep only the filter fields in the clear, accepting that you can't filter or search on encrypted text.

    FLOWS §1 · aira-api/app/adapters/crypto_adp.py · datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc2104 (HMAC) · csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/38/d/final (AES-GCM)
  • Startup + Azure Key Vault secrets: DefaultAzureCredential (no stored password), every secret fetched in parallel, validated by a pydantic-settings Secrets model of SecretStr, encrypted local-only cache, the app refuses to boot without them
    Answer

    All our secrets live in Azure Key Vault, and the app holds no password to reach it. At boot, the FastAPI lifespan builds the adapter registry, and the Azure adapter uses DefaultAzureCredential, which resolves to the App Service managed identity in the cloud and to az login on a laptop. It lists every enabled secret, fetches them all at once with asyncio.gather, and validates the bundle with a pydantic-settings Secrets model. Each field is a SecretStr with an alias like docdb-uri, because Key Vault names use dashes, and SecretStr prints as stars in logs and reprs, so you have to call .get_secret_value() on purpose. The vault URL has the word environment swapped for the environment name, so local and production read different vaults through one code path. My teammate built this loader; I extended it.

    Two things I added. Optional integrations like Browserbase, Stripe Link and Instacart are typed SecretStr | None, and each feature stays off until its secret exists in Key Vault, so the server answers 'not available here' instead of crashing. And the same vault holds a key, not a secret: aira-vault-kek, an RSA key used only to wrap and unwrap per-user data keys for the credential vault. That key never leaves Key Vault.

    If Key Vault is down at startup, the fetch or the validation fails and the app refuses to boot. That's deliberate, because half-configured is worse than down. And since deploys go to a preview slot that's health-checked before the swap, a version that can't load its secrets never takes traffic; the running version keeps the secrets it loaded at its own boot. Locally only, the bundle is cached in an AES-GCM encrypted file with mode 600 so restarts are fast. Honestly, that cache key is derived from config values, so it's a convenience, not real protection, and production never uses it. Rotating a secret means changing it in Key Vault and restarting.

    LLM provider keys sit in Key Vault like everything else, one vault per environment. To limit damage from a leak, I'd want separate keys per environment, provider-side spend caps and usage alerts, and keys scoped to a single project where the provider supports it, so rotation is a Key Vault edit plus a restart. I haven't verified that spend caps are set on every provider, so I won't claim it. And I'll be straight that we've committed keys to git in the past, and there are still a couple of temporary fallback defaults in the settings model that should be removed and rotated.

    On AKS with no secret at all, I'd use workload identity. The pod's Kubernetes service account is federated with a user-assigned managed identity: Azure trusts the cluster's OIDC issuer for that namespace and service account, the pod gets a projected token, and DefaultAzureCredential picks it up with no code change. Then Key Vault RBAC gives that identity Key Vault Secrets User for secrets and Key Vault Crypto User for wrap and unwrap on the KEK, nothing more. We already use the same federated idea in CI: GitHub Actions logs into Azure with OIDC, with no stored password.

    FLOWS §2 · app/main.py lifespan · app/adapters/azure_adp.py fetch_secrets · learn.microsoft.com/en-us/python/api/overview/azure/identity-readme · learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/key-vault/general/overview
  • Session token: one JWT {sub, iat} used as an httpOnly cookie (web) or Authorization: Bearer (phone, WebSocket); expiry = iat + configured hours; logout unbinds the device's push token
    Answer

    Every login, whatever the provider, ends the same way: we issue our own JWT with just sub, the user id, and iat, signed with our secret. The web gets it as an HttpOnly cookie, the phone gets it as access_token and sends Authorization: Bearer, and the WebSocket handshake goes through the same dependency, which reads the cookie first and then the Bearer header. Expiry isn't an exp claim. It's iat plus a configured number of hours, 168 today, so changing the config shortens or extends every session already issued. Logout deletes the cookie, and if the app sends its device id, we unbind that device's push token so a logged-out phone stops getting notifications.

    Moving the token into a cookie was my change, in August 2025. Before that, after Google login the API redirected to the web app with the token in a ?token= query string. A token in a URL ends up in browser history, in server and proxy logs, and in the Referer header if the page loads anything third-party. And anything JavaScript can read, an XSS can steal. So I made the Google callback set an HttpOnly cookie, and did the same for the email-code login, which then returned JSON and let the frontend decide where to go.

    Secure means the browser only sends the cookie over HTTPS. SameSite decides whether it rides on cross-site requests: Lax sends it on top-level navigations but not on cross-site POSTs or fetches, and None sends it everywhere, but browsers only accept None together with Secure. In August I made HttpOnly and Secure production-only with SameSite=Lax. In September, when I rewrote the Google callback, the cookie outside local became Secure, SameSite=None on the .airaai.in domain; as I remember it, that was because the web frontend called the API cross-site with credentials. Locally, frontend and API were both plain http on localhost, same site, so we didn't need None, and a Secure-only cookie isn't reliable over plain http. That's why the flags were production-only. I also made every set and delete call use the same attributes, because a cookie set with one domain can't be cleared with another. Today the cookie is Secure outside local and SameSite=Lax, since our current web pages sit on our own domain next to the API.

    The weak spots, honestly. The token is stateless, so logout can't kill a copy of it until it expires. And the mobile auth-code exchange should check the purpose claim; today it decodes any token we signed. I'd add a token version on the user row for revocation and a purpose check on every decode.

    FLOWS §3 · app/entities/dependency_ent.py · app/entities/auth_ent.py get_user_id · cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Session_Management_Cheat_Sheet.html · datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc7519
  • Google native sign-in (phone): verify the id_token ourselves (RS256 via JWKS, aud, iss, email_verified), find by google_sub → else link by email_token atomically → else create; 409 for external emails Google can't vouch for; 2-try loop on DuplicateKeyError
    Answer

    On the phone, Google's SDK proves who the user is, and our server checks that proof itself before it trusts anything. My teammate built the native flow in October; before that the phone used our browser OAuth flow with a deep-link auth code. I know it end to end, so let me walk it.

    The user taps Continue with Google. The SDK is configured with offlineAccess: false, so we ask only for identity, no Gmail or Drive access; those are separate connectors with their own consent. A returning user gets a 'Continue as' prompt and a silent sign-in with no Google screen, and an in-progress flag stops a double tap. Google hands back an ID token, a signed JWT, and the phone posts it with the timezone to POST /v1/auth/google, where the request model forbids extra fields.

    The server verifies it, and that's the answer to 'why verify when the phone already did': anyone can POST a made-up token to our API, so the phone's word means nothing. We read the kid from the header, fetch Google's public key for it, and check the RS256 signature. Then aud must be our client id, so a token Google minted for some other app can't log into ours; iss must be Google; aud, exp, iat, iss and sub must be present and unexpired; and email_verified must be a real boolean true. Any failure is a 401; if Google's keys are unreachable it's a 503. For Apple it's the same idea plus a nonce check. In the browser flow the difference is that the server exchanges the authorization code itself, server to server with the client secret, and then verifies the ID token from that response.

    Then we find the account. First by google_sub, Google's permanent id, because emails can change and the sub never does. If there's no match, by email_token, the HMAC of the email, which links an older account that signed up through the browser. That link is one atomic find_one_and_update filtered on google_sub still being null, so two simultaneous logins can't both link. If nobody matches, we insert a new user. Unique indexes on email_token and on google_sub back that up, and if a parallel request wins the race we catch DuplicateKeyError and go round once more, at most two tries. A new user's scratchpad is created as a background task so login isn't slowed down.

    Linking by email is only safe when the provider really owns the address. For a gmail.com or Google Workspace address, Google is authoritative. For a personal domain on a Google account, Google can't prove the person still owns that domain, so we return 409 and the app switches to the browser sign-in automatically. In general, one account per email is enforced by the unique index, and we only merge when the identity provider vouches for the address. In our older email-code login, the one-time code proved the address, and it used the same email_token lookup, so it landed on the same account.

    Finally we drop Google's token and issue our own JWT, valid seven days. The phone stores it in the Keychain as this-device-only, loads the profile with a 4-second cap so a slow network never blocks sign-in, marks the user signed in, and fires a login event that opens the WebSocket with Bearer. From then on every request carries that header through the API client.

    FLOWS §4 · app/routers/auth/services/auth_s_v1.py google_native_signin · app/entities/user_ent.py resolve_google · developers.google.com/identity/gsi/web/guides/verify-google-id-token · DDIA ch7 "Preventing lost updates" (pdf p255–259)
    • Notes: Mobile Google sign-in, the full flow chart
  • Google browser OAuth: HMAC-signed state + 10-minute iat + one-time Redis nonce (get-and-delete), code exchange, call_type (auth / docs / delete / connector); the phone gets a 2-minute auth code, never the session in a URL
    Answer

    The state parameter exists so that when Google calls our callback, we can prove that this login is one we started, and as I built and ran it through October, it's an HMAC-signed payload plus a one-time Redis nonce. It went through a few versions, and the first one was mine and wrong.

    In January 2026 I built the connectors API, and the connect endpoint sent state=user_id to Google. That's not CSRF protection at all. A raw user id is guessable and unsigned, and the callback trusted it to decide whose account gets the tokens. So an attacker could send someone a consent link carrying the attacker's id, the victim approves with their own Google account, and the victim's Gmail ends up connected to the attacker's AiRA account. In April I moved connectors onto the HMAC-signed state with the user id as its own uid field, and about a week later my teammate added the nonce. The state is s1. plus a base64 payload of iat, a random nonce, the app and call type, the timezone and optionally the uid, plus an HMAC of that payload. The nonce is also written to Redis with a 10-minute expiry.

    On the callback we check three things. The HMAC must match, compared in constant time, so nobody can edit the payload. iat must be under 10 minutes old. And we GETDEL the nonce from Redis, which only succeeds once, so a replayed state is rejected as consumed. That's why it's both: the signature proves we minted it and nobody changed it, but a signed value can be replayed any number of times until it expires, and the Redis nonce makes it single use.

    Adding Google login to FastAPI, the way we did it: a login endpoint builds the state and redirects to Google with response_type=code, the scopes and access_type=offline. The callback verifies the state, exchanges the code server to server with the client secret, verifies the returned ID token's signature, audience, issuer and email_verified, finds or creates the user, and issues our own session JWT. We use the code flow rather than the implicit flow so tokens never pass through the browser. The call_type in the state routes the same callback: auth for login, docs for the API docs, which only our own domain can open, delete for the public account-deletion page, which never creates a user, and connector. I added an upload type for the AiRA Drive web page.

    For the phone, my teammate made sure the session never goes into a URL. The callback redirects to aira://authcallback with a two-minute signed auth code, and the app swaps that code for the real session over a POST. The gap I'd still close: the state isn't bound to the browser that started the flow, so a crafted consent link is still possible as a social-engineering attack. I'd add a short-lived cookie or an app-held verifier and compare it on the callback, the way PKCE does. In November the team, led by my teammate Ananda, rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis and deployed two API regions, so on master that single-use check has to live somewhere other than Redis; what I've described is the version I built and ran.

    FLOWS §5 · app/entities/auth_ent.py get_signed_state / parse_and_verify_state · developers.google.com/identity/protocols/oauth2/web-server · datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc6749#section-10.12 (CSRF and state)
  • Apple sign-in (iPhone): nonce hash check against replay, Apple JWKS cached in Redis with a refetch on unknown kid, spend the one-time code only after verifying, key users by sub (email comes once, may be a private relay), signed-JWT client_secret, revoke the refresh token on account deletion
    Answer

    I built Sign in with Apple end to end, and the short version is: verify Apple's identity token ourselves, check the nonce against replay, spend the one-time code only after that, and key the user by Apple's sub, not by email.

    The iPhone generates a random raw nonce and gives Apple only its SHA-256. Apple returns an identity token, which is a JWT with that hash inside, an authorization code, and the name and email, but only on the very first sign-in. The app posts all of it to POST /v1/auth/apple/signin. To verify, I read the kid from the token header and look up Apple's public keys, which I cache in Redis for an hour. If the kid isn't there, Apple has rotated keys, so I clear the cache and fetch once more. Then RS256 signature, audience equal to our bundle id, issuer equal to https://appleid.apple.com, and sub, iss, aud, exp and nonce all required. Finally I hash the raw nonce we received and compare it to the nonce in the token. A stolen token is useless without the original raw nonce, and that's what the nonce is for.

    Only after that do I exchange the authorization code. Apple's client_secret isn't a fixed string; it's an ES256 JWT I sign on each call, with Apple's 180-day maximum expiry, using a dedicated Sign in with Apple key from Key Vault, kept separate from our push-notification key. I first ran verification and the exchange in parallel with asyncio.gather to save a round trip, then deliberately made them sequential again. Apple's codes are one-time. If the token turned out to be bad, the parallel version had already burned the code on a login we were about to reject, and then we'd never get a refresh token for that sign-in. One round trip is cheaper than that.

    How it differs from Google: Apple needs the nonce check, the email arrives once and may be a private relay address, the client secret is a signed JWT, and Apple requires you to revoke the token when the account is deleted. So we key users by a tokenized sub stored in the same email_token field Google uses, so one lookup works for both providers. We flag private relay addresses, and the name falls back to 'unknown'. In late June I also built Google and Apple linking by tokenized email with an apple_sub lookup for later sign-ins, but a teammate reverted that PR on master on July 1st, and only the guarded migration runner came back, which I re-landed the next day. So today a person who signed up with Google and later uses Apple gets a second account. That's a known gap.

    We keep Apple's refresh token, encrypted, mainly for deletion. When a user deletes their account, I decrypt and revoke it at Apple, which fully unlinks the app from their Apple ID. Then we drop their Browserbase browser profiles, and my teammate's concurrent purge deletes every Mongo collection keyed by the user, including the vault's credentials and keys, the connector tokens and the Gmail watch, plus their Qdrant points and Redis keys. What it doesn't do yet: uploaded blobs are left orphaned, Google tokens are deleted rather than revoked at Google, and a session JWT stays valid until it expires because sessions are stateless. Those are the next three things I'd add.

    FLOWS §6 · app/adapters/apple_adp.py SignInClass · app/routers/auth/services/auth_s_v1.py apple_signin · developer.apple.com/documentation/sign_in_with_apple/sign_in_with_apple_rest_api · developer.apple.com/documentation/sign_in_with_apple/revoke_tokens
  • email_token: HMAC of the lower-cased email (or the Apple sub), unique index for login lookup, also maps a Gmail push's mailbox address back to users — no plain email stored anywhere
    Answer

    email_token is a one-way, repeatable fingerprint: the HMAC-SHA256, with our secret, of the lower-cased, stripped email, or of the Apple sub. Same input, same token every time, and you can't turn it back into the email. That's how we look users up by email without storing the email.

    It's used in three places. First, login: there's a unique index on it, users_email_token_unique, which my teammate added, so two accounts can never share an email, and the user lookup is just an equality query on the token. Second, Apple: Apple may never send an email, so I store the tokenized Apple sub in the same field, behind one tokenize_lookup_key helper, so the same lookup works for both providers. Third, Gmail push, which I built: Google's push only says 'mailbox x changed'. When a user connects Gmail, I store the tokenized mailbox address on their watch row, and on a push I tokenize the address and find every watch for it. Two AiRA users could connect the same mailbox, so it returns all of them. That path never needs a plain email in the database either. The real email exists only as an AES-GCM encrypted_email for when we must read it.

    Linking works on top of that. For Google, we look up by google_sub first, then by email_token to link an older account, atomically and only when Google can vouch for the address. For Apple, my June version looked up by tokenized email on the first sign-in, because that's the only time Apple sends the email, and by apple_sub afterwards. That linking was reverted, so today Apple users are keyed by the tokenized sub alone.

    The index story is a good lesson. I put a sparse unique index on apple_sub, expecting Google users without the field to be skipped. It still collided. Sparse only skips documents where the field is missing, and Pydantic was writing apple_sub: null explicitly on every Google user, so they all had the field with the same null value. The fix was a partial index with a filter of apple_sub of type string, so only real values are indexed. Changing options under the same index name needs a drop and recreate on the live database, which is why I built the guarded admin migration runner. In the end we took the apple_sub index out of startup, and my teammate later used the same partial-index pattern for google_sub.

    The trade-offs: you get exact match only, no prefix or fuzzy search on email. And rotating the HMAC secret means re-tokenizing every user. That's doable because we keep the encrypted email and the raw apple_sub to recompute from, but it's a migration, not a config change.

    FLOWS §7 · app/entities/user_ent.py (index users_email_token_unique) · app/entities/connectors/google_ent.py process_gmail_push
  • Google connectors (Gmail / Calendar / Drive): user id inside the signed state, same callback as login, save only the scopes actually granted, encrypted tokens, fall back to the sign-in refresh token when Google omits it, a 4xx on refresh marks the grant gone, wake tasks waiting on the connector
    Answer

    A connector is a second, separate Google consent, for Gmail, Calendar or Drive, layered on top of login. The design rule is: ask for a scope only when the user wants that feature, and store exactly what they granted.

    Connecting Gmail goes like this. The logged-in app calls GET /v1/connectors/connect/gmail with its timezone. The server builds a signed state with call type connector and the user's id inside it, because Google's redirect back to us carries no cookie or Bearer we could trust. The scopes are 'email profile' plus the Gmail scope, with access_type=offline and prompt=consent. The app opens the URL, the user agrees, and Google calls the same callback we use for login. We verify the state, exchange the code, and verify the returned ID token to learn which Google account was connected. Google returns the granted scope string, and we keep only connectors whose scope is actually in it, since a user can untick boxes. Each granted connector is upserted into ConnectorScopes with the access and refresh tokens AES-GCM encrypted, one row per user and connector. Then, in parallel, we wake any background task that was sleeping on 'I need Gmail' and start the Gmail push watch, and deep-link back with aira://connectorcallback?status=success&connectors=gmail. On first scrape: our earlier version read up to 1,000 emails through the batch API and map-reduced them into a profile. The current flow doesn't bulk-scrape on connect; agents read mail on demand and new mail arrives through push.

    The refresh token problem bit us. Google only returns a refresh token on the first consent for a given client and user. On later consents it's simply missing, and the early code ended up with no refresh token for the connector, so the access token died after an hour and every Gmail tool call failed. The fixes were mine. Keep the stored refresh token when Google omits a new one. When the connector callback has none, fall back to the refresh token saved at sign-in. And send prompt=consent on connector URLs, which makes Google issue a fresh refresh token. Honestly, prompt=consent is the real fix; the fallback is a safety net, because a refresh token belongs to the grant it came from.

    Refreshing is refresh_scope: decrypt the refresh token, ask Google for a new access token, store it encrypted. If Google answers with a 4xx, the user has revoked access, so we mark the grant gone and the app shows the connector as disconnected instead of retrying forever. A 5xx just propagates without marking anything. If there's no refresh token at all, we raise 're-consent required'. Switching a connector off keeps the tokens; disconnecting stops the Gmail watch, cancels its renewal job, deletes the row and ends the tasks that needed it.

    Incremental authorization means requesting scopes over time instead of all at login. My January version used Google's tokeninfo endpoint to read granted scopes and stored an encrypted user_scopes row per scope, with retry decorators that refreshed the right scope's token on a 401. Today it's the per-connector ConnectorScopes row, built on my teammate's base. We don't merge grants with include_granted_scopes, so each connector stands on its own and can be revoked on its own.

    FLOWS §8 · app/entities/connectors/connector_ent.py upsert_scope / refresh_scope · developers.google.com/identity/protocols/oauth2/web-server (refresh tokens, incremental auth)
  • Gmail push: watch → Pub/Sub → our endpoint; verify the OIDC push JWT (audience + our service account), always 204, then pull the History API in a background task; Redis lease + "again" flag (one reader, no lost mail), compare-and-set history_id (idempotent), renew the watch a day early through the IRIS scheduler
    Answer

    Gmail push is 'push to notify, pull to read', and I built it. Google tells us a mailbox changed; we fetch the changes ourselves.

    It starts at connect. I call Gmail's profile and users.watch together, pointing the watch at our Pub/Sub topic, and store a watch row with the user, the tokenized mailbox address, the current history id and the expiry. Watches last about seven days, so the IRIS scheduler renews each one a day early. Renewal only re-arms the watch and updates its expiry; it never touches the history id, which only the reader moves. A failed renewal retries in an hour, and a revoked grant stops renewing.

    When mail arrives, Gmail publishes to Pub/Sub, and Pub/Sub POSTs to /v1/connectors/gmail/notify with a Google-signed OIDC token in the Authorization header. That's how we secure it: I verify the JWT's signature, that the audience is our configured audience, and that it was issued for our own push service account with a verified email. Never trust the body alone; anyone can POST 'you have new mail'. The body is just base64 of the mailbox address and a history id. I queue a background task and always return 204, even for bad input, because an error makes Pub/Sub redeliver and you get a retry storm, and answering fast keeps Pub/Sub's deadline from ever waiting on Gmail.

    In the background, I find watches whose tokenized address matches and whose history id is lower than the pushed one. That filter alone drops duplicate and out-of-order pushes. For each user, I read the history API since the bookmark, following pages; if the history is too old Gmail returns 404, and I restart from the profile's current history id. I keep INBOX additions, fetch the newest ten in parallel, parse them, and hand one note to the mail trigger, which starts a system turn so Aira can tell the user. Then I move the bookmark only if it still holds the value I read, a compare-and-set, so two readers can never both advance it.

    If ten pushes arrive at once, each one sets an 'again' flag in Redis for that user and tries to take a lease. Only one gets it, so there's one reader per user. The reader loops while the flag is up: take the flag with get-and-delete, read, renew the lease. After releasing, it checks the flag once more, because a push could land between its last take and the release, and it would otherwise be lost. So no double reads and no lost mail, and the history-id filter plus the compare-and-set make the whole thing idempotent.

    Historically, in late 2025, my first Gmail push sent LLM-summarized digests to WhatsApp. This version wakes the assistant instead. Weak spots: it's at-least-once, so a crash after the note is handed off but before the bookmark moves can repeat a note, and returning 204 on a bad token means a misconfigured audience fails silently except for a warning log. I'd alert on that. That's how it ran through October. In November the team, led by my teammate Ananda, rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis and deployed two API regions, so there the lease and the flag would have to move to another store; the history-id compare-and-set carries over unchanged.

    FLOWS §9 · app/entities/connectors/google_ent.py · developers.google.com/gmail/api/guides/push · developers.google.com/gmail/api/guides/sync · cloud.google.com/pubsub/docs/authenticate-push-subscriptions · DDIA ch11 "Fault tolerance" (pdf p482–484; idempotence)
  • Swiggy / Stripe Link connect with PKCE: verifier kept on the server, S256 challenge sent out, one-time random state in a Redis hash, dynamic client registration cached, callback URL built from the configured base URL (never the Host header)
    Answer

    PKCE is how a client with no secret proves that the one redeeming an authorization code is the one that asked for it, and I used it for both Swiggy and Stripe Link. I generate a random verifier and keep it on the server, send only its SHA-256, base64url encoded, as the S256 challenge, and send the original verifier when I exchange the code. It stops authorization-code interception: a code stolen from a redirect, a log or a malicious app on the same URL scheme is useless without the verifier, which never left our server.

    For Swiggy, I wrote the MCP client on the official SDK over streamable HTTP, against three servers: food, Instamart and Dineout. Swiggy uses dynamic client registration, so we register as a public client once per redirect URI and cache it in Redis. On connect I create a 32-byte random state and a Redis hash holding the user, the service and the verifier, with a 10-minute expiry, and the callback reads and deletes it in one Lua script, because Redis Cluster pipelines aren't transactional, so it's strictly one-time. The callback URL is built from our configured public base URL, never the Host header, because that client registration is shared by every user and a spoofed Host must never reach it. On top sit 51 hand-declared tools, each with a Pydantic args model that maps our snake_case to Swiggy's camelCase and forbids extra fields, plus an approval tier.

    Four tools are never retried: place_food_order, checkout, book_table and check_payment_status. If a placement call times out, the order may already exist, and a retry could double-order or double-charge. So the agent is told the order may or may not exist and to read orders back first. Everything else retries up to five times inside a 30-second budget with jittered backoff. And the approval card for an order is built from fresh reads of the cart and address, not from the model's arguments.

    Being the client side was harder than I expected. The SDK hides HTTP status codes, so a 401, a 429 and a 503 all came back as the same generic error. I added a response hook that records the status, Retry-After and the body, and a classifier that turns them into typed errors. The SDK's task groups must exit in the task that entered them, so each live session is owned by its own task and closed by an event. There's one session per user per server, closed after 10 idle minutes, reopened on an expired-session 404. Server sampling is refused and elicitations are surfaced to the agent.

    Stripe Link is how a browser task pays without the model ever seeing a card number. The tool creates one spend request with the idempotency key run_id:tool_call_id and a ledger row. The user approves in Link, like entering a UPI PIN, and we poll every two seconds; if Link asks for 3-D Secure, the user gets a bank-verify card and a second window. On approval I fetch the one-time card, fill it into the checkout through the browser agent's secret fill, which keeps values out of the model's context, drop it from memory, and return only the brand and last four. Even our validation errors are raised without their cause, because that error could quote a card number.

    Why build Link and Instacart when our users are in India? Honestly, partly building ahead. India has no agent card rail: RBI requires a bank OTP on every online card payment, Stripe Issuing isn't offered there, and Link excludes India, so Indian users pay in the live view. Swiggy is the India rail. Link and Instacart are gated by the country the app reports and stay off until their keys exist. Instacart's self-serve door is two tools with a partner key. They were cheap, and they reuse the same ledger, approval and handoff pieces as everything else.

    FLOWS §10 · app/entities/connectors/connector_ent.py open_state / take_oauth_state · datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc7636
  • User vault (envelope encryption): per-user DEK sealed by the Key Vault KEK (RSA-OAEP-256, never leaves Key Vault), values AES-GCM with AAD user:handle:field, 15-minute DEK cache with one shared unwrap per user, race-safe key minting, background re-wrap on KEK rotation (8 at a time), crypto-shred on "forget all"
    Answer

    The vault is where the browser agent keeps a user's saved website logins, and I built it on envelope encryption. Each user has their own 32-byte data key. Every value is sealed with AES-256-GCM under that key. And the data key itself is stored only wrapped, by a key-encryption key in Azure Key Vault called aira-vault-kek, an RSA key, using RSA-OAEP-256. The KEK never leaves Key Vault. Mongo holds two things: UserKeys rows, with the user, a version, the wrapped data key and the KEK version that wrapped it, and Credentials rows, one per user per site, with the handle login:<site>, the input fields, one ciphertext per field and the key version. So the data key lives only in wrapped form, plus briefly in process memory.

    Why not encrypt every value inside Key Vault directly? Each one would be a network round trip, it costs per operation, and Key Vault throttles. With envelope encryption, I call Key Vault once per user to unwrap, and everything else is local AES. It also means one compromised data key exposes only one user.

    Why AES-256-GCM: it's authenticated, so any tampering fails decryption instead of returning garbage. Each value is stored as base64url of the 12-byte nonce, the ciphertext and the 16-byte tag together. The AAD isn't stored, it's recomputed: user_id:handle:field. AAD is data that's authenticated but not encrypted. Binding to it means a ciphertext copied into another user's row, another site's row, or swapped from the password field into the username field fails to open. Nonces are 12 random bytes from secrets on every seal. I'll be precise: with random nonces the guarantee is probabilistic, not absolute. What makes it safe is that each per-user key seals only a handful of values, far below the roughly four billion messages per key that the guidance allows for random 96-bit nonces.

    Unwrapping is a network call, so the plain data key is cached in memory for 15 minutes. If 20 requests for the same user miss at once, one unwrap task runs and the rest await it through asyncio.shield, so one caller cancelling doesn't kill it for the others. There's an epoch counter: revoking bumps it, so an unwrap already in flight can't re-cache a key for a user we just forgot. The risk is that a plain key sits in process memory for up to 15 minutes, visible to a memory dump or a debugger. To shrink that window we forget it on revoke, on account deletion, and when the browser task that needed it ends; what's left is the 15 minutes while a task is running or after an API save. Minting is race-safe: a unique index on user and version, and if two saves both try to create version one, the loser gets DuplicateKeyError, re-reads and uses the winner's key.

    Rotation is where envelope encryption pays off. Key Vault keeps KEK versions. New wraps always use the current version and record it, and unwraps always use the exact version that wrapped, so old keys keep opening. At startup, a background pass finds active data keys on an older KEK version and re-wraps them, eight at a time behind a semaphore. Each write is a compare-and-set, so a key retired meanwhile stays retired. The data is never re-encrypted; only the 32-byte keys are. For zero downtime: create a new KEK version, let the pass re-wrap, watch the logged count of stale keys reach zero, and only then disable the old version. Disable it early and those users read as 'vault unavailable'.

    Across many instances, every instance would run that pass at boot, so you'd pay duplicate Key Vault calls and risk throttling. Correctness holds, because the compare-and-set makes the second writer a no-op. Through October we ran one instance by decision; with more, I'd put the pass behind a Redis lock or run it as a one-off job. Since the team's November rewrite runs two API regions without Redis, the one-off job is what I'd pick now. And since it only runs at boot, a rotation waits for the next deploy.

    Crypto-shredding means deleting the key instead of chasing every copy of the data. 'Revoke all' deletes every credential row, retires the active key by blanking wrapped_dek and setting retired_at, forgets the cached key and deletes the browser profiles. It refuses with 'in use' if a live task is holding a site. The proof for the user is the retired key row with its timestamp, and any leftover ciphertext can't open. One honest caveat: a database backup taken earlier still holds the wrapped key, and the KEK is shared and still alive, so someone with unwrap rights and an old backup could open it. True shredding across backups needs a per-user KEK you delete, or backup retention shorter than the promise.

    If a developer with production read access dumps the vault tables, they get wrapped keys and AAD-bound ciphertext. Neither opens without unwrapKey on the KEK, which only the app's managed identity holds. They do see which sites a user saved logins for, because handles are plaintext. The emails and OAuth tokens elsewhere are encrypted under the app's data-encryption key, which is a Key Vault secret, not something in the database. The real threat left is anyone who can run code as the app identity.

    Audit is the gap. There's no per-decrypt ledger today; the credential row stamps when it was last used and how the last fill went, and logs carry only ids. I'd add an append-only ledger of user, handle, run and time, readable by the user in the app and by a small on-call group. One-time codes typed on the phone go to Redis as otp:<run_id>, encrypted with the app key, for five minutes. Reading one leaves it in place so a failed fill can retry, and it's deleted only once it has been typed successfully.

    FLOWS §11 · app/entities/vault_ent.py · app/adapters/azure_adp.py wrap_key / unwrap_key · cloud.google.com/kms/docs/envelope-encryption · learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/key-vault/keys/about-keys
  • WebSocket auth + Redis pub/sub fan-out: authenticate on the handshake, register the socket on this worker, publish to the notify channel so any worker can reach a socket that lives on another; cleanup in finally
    Answer

    The phone holds one authenticated WebSocket, /v2/ws, and the rule is: authenticate on the handshake, register the socket on the worker that owns it, and let any other worker reach it through Redis pub/sub.

    Auth uses the same dependency as our HTTP routes. A FastAPI dependency typed as Request doesn't get injected on a WebSocket route, so the fix is to type it as Starlette's HTTPConnection, the common base of Request and WebSocket, which has headers and cookies. I did that first in February when I moved notifications to a WebSocket. Our March codebase reset dropped it, so I re-applied it in April, and a few days later I added the Authorization: Bearer path for the phone. The dependency reads the session cookie first and then Authorization: Bearer. Native mobile WebSocket clients can't set cookies, and browser WebSocket APIs can't set headers, so accepting both covers both. After accept, the socket goes into this process's map from user id to a set of sockets. A set, because one user can have a phone and a tab open, and both should get every event. A dead socket is dropped on its first failed send, and cleanup runs in finally with independent steps under asyncio.gather.

    The path there was mine, in steps, in February 2026. First, SSE with a Redis Stream per user: XADD, then an approximate XTRIM to about a thousand entries, and replay on reconnect using Last-Event-ID and an XREAD drain loop. Streams were in the middle for replay: a durable per-user log, so a client that dropped could catch up. The next day I replaced Streams with Pub/Sub, because Mongo was already the source of truth and the app reloads state over REST on reconnect, so the stream was a second copy with memory to trim and no expiry. That version had one subscriber per instance and a routing map from user to a set of bounded asyncio queues feeding the SSE responses. Three days later I moved it to a WebSocket, because the phone has to send audio and commands up, not just receive. A teammate added binary audio frames the next day, and my teammate Ananda later rebuilt the router in our restructured codebase and built the v2 socket with direct dispatch in July.

    Cross-worker delivery, as we ran it through October: anything produced off the socket's process, an agent finishing, new Gmail, a cron, is published on the Redis channel notify with the user id. Every instance subscribes at startup, checks its own map, and only the owner sends. The subscriber needs its own Redis connection, because a connection in subscribe mode can only run subscribe-family commands and sits in a blocking read. Ours has no read timeout, so an idle listen isn't treated as a failure, while the normal client uses 5-second timeouts. I also made it self-healing: reconnect with backoff from one second doubling to 30.

    One nuance about voice. Since that v2 direct dispatch, the realtime turn runs on the process that holds the socket, so its audio frames and screen updates go through a direct local path that never touches Redis. Redis pub/sub is for everything produced elsewhere. And through October we ran a single instance by decision. In November the team, led by my teammate Ananda, rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis and deployed two API regions, so this fan-out is the design I built and ran, not what master does now.

    Scaling horizontally: pub/sub broadcasts every message to every instance, which is fine up to a few dozen instances. Beyond that I'd keep a presence map from user to instance and publish to a per-instance channel, or shard by user hash. It's also fire-and-forget: if nobody is listening, the message is gone. That's acceptable only because state lives in Mongo. A known gap is that the listener awaits each send inline, so one slow client can delay others on that instance. The SSE version actually had bounded queues per connection; I'd bring that back as a per-socket queue with a writer task.

    Token expiry mid-connection, honestly: we check the token only at the handshake. The 20-second ping with a 60-second timeout is liveness, not auth, so a socket can outlive its token. I'd record the token's expiry at accept, close with an app-specific close code when it passes, between turns so it never cuts a sentence, and let the app refresh and reconnect.

    FLOWS §12 · app/routers/websocket/services/websocket_s_v2.py · app/entities/notifications_ent.py · redis.io/docs/latest/develop/interact/pubsub/
  • File upload with SAS URLs: short-lived, write-only signed URL; bytes go phone → Blob Storage; the API only confirms and enriches
    Answer

    The bytes never touch our API. The phone or browser uploads straight to Azure Blob Storage with a short-lived signed URL scoped to one blob, and the API only hands out permission and checks the result afterwards.

    The flow has three calls. First, POST /files/upload-url with the size and MIME type. The server rejects anything over 50 MB or outside the MIME allowlist, builds the blob key itself from the user id and a fresh file id, so the client can't choose a path, and returns a SAS URL valid for three minutes. It's a user-delegation SAS, signed with a temporary key fetched through our managed identity, not with the storage account key. Second, the client PUTs the bytes directly to Blob. Third, POST /files/{id}/process: the server confirms the blob exists, re-checks the real size, because a SAS can't enforce the size the client declared, has Gemini write a title and description, and saves the record. Why: a 50 MB upload through FastAPI would tie up memory and bandwidth on the same workers serving realtime voice.

    The upload stack itself is my teammate's. My parts: the AiRA Drive web page, where users upload PDFs and images straight to Blob with client-side checks and a per-file status tray. Download links are signed only after an ownership check, and a batch download keeps only the ids the user owns. And file search, including semantic search over attachments in Qdrant, exposed to the model as a tool.

    For storage in general at AiRA: user uploads go through that SAS path, under a key the server builds from the user id and file id. Things the server generates, voice audio, generated images and film pictures, are uploaded by the server itself and served back through signed read URLs.

    Weak spots I'd fix. The SAS permission set is read, add, create and write; for an upload I'd narrow it to create and write. And on account deletion, blobs are left orphaned and unreachable rather than deleted, so I'd add a blob delete or a lifecycle rule to the purge.

    FLOWS §13 · app/adapters/azure_adp.py get_upload_url · learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/storage/common/storage-sas-overview
  • Security hygiene: leaked keys in git history, per-route ownership checks, guarded admin migration endpoints
    Answer

    Three habits here: secrets never in code, every query filtered by owner, and production changes going through a guarded path instead of a laptop. I'll be honest that we learned all three by getting them wrong first.

    Leaked keys: yes, and several were mine. In September 2025 I hard-coded an Exa key in source and moved it to settings the next day. In December 2025 I put Telegram, Razorpay, Periskope and WhatsApp tokens as overrides in the settings file, and in January local WhatsApp gateway credentials, which I took out later that month. Teammates committed others. What I learned is that removing a key from the file doesn't remove it from history; once it's pushed, treat it as exposed and rotate it. The fix that stuck was structural. Every secret is a SecretStr field loaded from Key Vault at boot, and an optional integration simply stays off until its secret exists. What prevents it now, honestly, is that plus review. There's no automated scanner in this repo's CI yet, and there are still two temporary fallback defaults in the settings model that need to go. In my own open-source engine's CI I run gitleaks, and I'd add the same here as a pre-commit hook and a CI gate, plus push protection on the repo.

    Ownership checks: I've shipped this bug myself. The speaker rename and delete endpoints I built in May 2026 took a speaker id without scoping it to the user, and a teammate added the owner scoping. Earlier, my first conflict-list endpoint had a count that wasn't filtered by user. The fix for the class of bug is a rule in our AGENTS.md, which my teammate wrote: every client-supplied id is cross-validated against the authenticated user and the parent resource before any read, write, signing or delete. In code that means user_id goes inside the query filter itself, not a separate check before it, and a miss returns 404, so you can't even probe whether an id exists. The vault, for example, refuses a task id that isn't yours with 'run not owned', which the API turns into a plain 404. What's still missing is enforcement. I'd add a data-access layer where every user-owned query requires a user_id, and a test per route that calls it with another user's id and expects a 404.

    Admin migrations: I built the runner when the apple_sub index had to be dropped and recreated on the live database. POST /v1/admin/migrations/run only accepts allowlisted admins, built on my teammate's allowlist, comparing the tokenized email in constant time. It's a dry run by default that writes a JSON report, and it only applies when you type the phrase 'RUN MIGRATION'. Scripts are chosen by filename only, validated against path traversal, run as an asyncio subprocess, and the report path is accepted only inside the reports folder. Every run goes into a MigrationRuns ledger. The index script refused to run if Apple users already existed. One honest blemish: after a revert and re-land, the 'drop' script ended up containing the recreate step, so its name no longer matches what it does. And the fuller framework, with dependency ordering, a lock and rollback, stayed on a branch.

Learn: your own repos and ~/Desktop/airaai/aira_temp/contribution-audit/bullets.md, docs/DHANESH_*.md (code-verified numbers) · resume career/profile/resume.tex · LinkedIn copy career/profile/linkedin-live-snapshot.md. Practise by recording yourself explaining each one in 5 minutes.

1.10 AiRA — product, architecture and stack

  • The pitch, your role and the timeline: product phases, what you built first, proudest and hardest systems, competitors
    Answer

    AiRA is a voice-first AI assistant: you talk to it like a person, it remembers you across sessions, it paints its answer on the screen while it speaks, and it can run real tasks in the background, like research, your Gmail and Calendar, or a cloud browser, with your approval. The user wants one assistant that knows them and gets things done. It's in beta on iOS and Android, and I don't have a user number I'd stand behind, so I won't guess.

    I'm founding engineer and team lead: I lead a four-person frontend and backend team, own system design and PR reviews, and work directly with the CEO. In practice there was rarely a spec: I'd take a product idea, write the design doc, build the backend and often the client half. I also wrote several of the team's AGENTS.md coding rules and about 34 design docs. I don't log time, so no percentages for a typical week; git shows I ship on both repos, 89 backend and 32 app commits in September 2026, plus reviews.

    The product went through five phases. I joined in late July 2025 on the Next.js web chat, building auth and the streaming client, then moved to the backend in August to build memory: Redis working memory, a Memgraph graph and Qdrant retrieval. From autumn 2025 to January 2026 it was a WhatsApp assistant: I integrated three WhatsApp providers, then self-hosted WAHA, plus Gmail and Calendar agents and IRIS reminders. In February 2026 we moved to our own mobile app, and in May I built live voice mode on Deepgram, then Cartesia. In August I built Omni, which replaced live mode, chat and onboarding, and I deleted about 17.8k lines in one commit, which my resume rounds to 17,000. In October it became a persistent agent with durable background tasks. The direction was the CEO's call; the technical push was that WhatsApp gave us no voice or screen of our own, and three pipelines did one job. My latest commits, end of October, are Gmail push, choice handling in approvals and browser permission modes. Since November the team works on Ananda's backend rewrite on master; what I describe is what I built and ran through October.

    The system I'm proudest of is Lumen, the text-to-video engine I wrote alone. The model writes a JSON script, Lumen validates and repairs it scene by scene, and every frame is a pure function of time, so one bad scene is dropped instead of blanking the film. The one I'd delete is the Memgraph graph memory I co-built with a teammate; the traversals had to avoid the user's own node, which links to everything. I switched its traversal off myself in October 2025 in favour of simple scratchpads, and the March 2026 reset removed it. A second database for an unproven idea.

    The hardest problem was interruptions in Omni: keeping the stored conversation true to what the user actually heard. The model stream, the TTS receiver and phone playback run at once, and a cancel can land anywhere. So each audio frame carries exactly the words it speaks, and the server records the words it really shipped.
