When a reply is stopped, stop_voice cancels the TTS receiver before it closes the audio window, so audio Cartesia already made never plays, and the turn is sealed to the heard words. Since October 20 whether to interrupt is the app's call, a teammate's hold-and-judge flow; the server stops cleanly and records honestly. An answer held or cut off is carried into the next turn as "never heard", with the reason: held, interrupted or overtaken. That took iterations from August 22 to September 17; Shashwat worked on the "unheard output" part with me.

    On commits, the audit counts 1,528 of mine in aira-api: 398 merges and 1,086 unique non-merge patches. By subject prefix it's roughly 445 feat, about 100 fix and about 370 refactor, though my subjects were often wrong. The merges come from the v3 rewrite, when three of us rebuilt in parallel and I kept pulling teammates' branches in, and from restacking my work into stacked PR branches in August.

    I haven't measured voice versus typed usage. Every Omni turn row records audio and mode, so it's one aggregation away. We also didn't have one agreed success metric for most of my time. There was an internal retention dashboard, and at the end of October I proposed a north star, current-user return on value days, in our core-loop doc. It isn't shipped, so I haven't seen it move.

    How so much in 15 months? Honestly, not alone. Memory, the Jev gate and the LongMemEval result were shared with teammates. I reused one spine: a typed tool framework and later one tool registry, so each new agent was mostly declarations. And I build with an AI coding assistant, which several of my commits credit. Was the deleted work wasted? Mostly no. Live mode taught us the STT, barge-in and turn-buffer pieces Omni reused, and activities became guided runs. The social Ask and Posts feed was the real waste, and I removed it myself in August. From AiRA I'd reuse the gated tool registry, the durable run-row state machine, the write-ahead turn log and Lumen.

    ChatGPT voice, Gemini Live and Pi have better models and far more scale, so I wouldn't claim we beat them at talking; what differs is the product around the voice. It streams a UI the app renders natively while it speaks, keeps long-term memory, and runs durable tasks that act behind approvals and a vault, so passwords never reach the model. Any of them could build that; our edge is focus, not a moat.

  • Architecture in boxes: one FastAPI monolith, which datastore holds what, request path vs background, what fails when a store is down
    Answer

    One FastAPI monolith, aira-api, plus two side services. The phone hits api.airaai.in, which goes through Azure Front Door to an App Service container running uvicorn, over REST plus one authenticated WebSocket, /v2/ws. The turn loop, agent manager and crons run in that process. Mongo, on the Cosmos Mongo API, is the source of truth: users, sessions, turns, agent runs, tasks, approvals and vault rows. Redis is hot state: locks, slot counters, the per-turn log, screen deltas, one-time codes and the pub/sub notify channel the older features use. Omni's own frames, audio included, skip Redis: since a teammate's v2 direct dispatch in July they go straight to the socket this process holds. Qdrant holds memory and attachment vectors, Blob holds files, Key Vault holds secrets and the vault's wrapping key, and Memgraph held the graph until March 2026. Outward: six LLM providers, Cartesia, search APIs, Google, Swiggy, Stripe Link and Browserbase. The two side services were aira-api-bg, a 12-hourly consolidation of the shared knowledge store that I co-built with a teammate, and aira_relay, my WhatsApp MCP server. One framing note: this is what I built and ran through October. In November Ananda's first-principles rewrite merged to master with no Redis, native Cosmos DB, no APScheduler and two API regions; that's the team's later change, not my work.

    v3, or MACH, is the layout the team reset to in mid-March 2026: controller for HTTP only, service for the use case, entity for business logic, DB models, prompts and tools, and adapter as the only layer touching an SDK or a secret. It's hexagonal without ports, because we have one implementation per vendor and adapters already return our own neutral types. Part of the pull was one-way imports, since the old layout had circular-import trouble. The reset was a teammate's; my part was the big merges and later the coding rules.

    Request path versus background: if the user is waiting to hear or see it in this turn, it stays in the path, under a timeout. That's the ownership check, context loads in one asyncio.gather, the Jev gate at 1.5 s, the planner at 4 s, memory at 3 s, then the model streaming into TTS. Everything else is detached, long work becomes a durable agent row, and periodic work is a cron.

    Redis down on my version: boot pings it, so a restart fails. At runtime voice keeps working, because Omni frames don't touch Redis. What breaks is quieter: turn-log appends are best-effort, so turns finish but lose their crash evidence; cron firings can't take their lock; slot counters stop admitting background tasks; screen deltas and vault one-time codes fail; and older features on notify, like live session titles, go silent until the subscriber reconnects with backoff. For Qdrant, in 2025 I made the API boot with vector features off, but that guard didn't survive v3; from today's code I'd expect boot to fail, untested. At runtime a slow Qdrant costs only the memories through the 3 s timeout, but a fast hard error isn't caught there and would fail the turn. That's a gap. Mongo down means nothing works; the phone gets omni.process_failed.

    Redis and Mongo hold the same data for different jobs: Redis is the hot copy for a read every turn, Mongo the durable one. Short-term memory in 2025 was a Redis list with a 30-minute TTL on logout, PERSIST on login, and read-through rehydration from Mongo on a miss. The turn log is the same: Redis appends during the turn, one sealed Mongo row at the end.

    The single point of failure is the single instance, a deliberate choice that boot recovery even assumes, plus Mongo. The per-turn bottleneck is model latency: TurnUsage records milliseconds for gate, planner, retrieval, first token and total, and the model stages are where a turn waits; I never timed store calls separately. We never load-tested, so beyond that it's a guess. My suspects are the one shared Cartesia TTS socket per worker and sends awaited inline: on the Omni path a slow phone only slows its own turn, but the shared notify listener awaits each send in one loop, so one slow client there delays others.

    Redrawn, I'd keep the direct socket path and add a bounded writer queue per socket, make recovery safe for more than one instance from day one, drop Memgraph, and give agent workers their own pool. For 20 engineers the seams already exist: voice gateway, turn orchestration, agent runtime, connectors, memory, identity. I'd split the voice gateway and agent runtime first, because they scale differently.

  • Stack choices: FastAPI, MongoDB, Qdrant, Memgraph, Redis, Azure, APScheduler, OpenRouter, Browserbase — and the trade-offs
    Answer

    Quick answers: the backend is FastAPI. The graph database was Memgraph and the vector database is Qdrant. Omni uses Cartesia for both STT and TTS, and Deepgram was the STT vendor before it. The browser stack is Browserbase plus Stagehand. Much of the base stack predates me: FastAPI from May 2025, Redis from July, Qdrant added by a teammate in early August. Memgraph I co-own: I wrote its first schemas on August 9, 2025, the same day a teammate swapped the Neo4j setup for Memgraph. So I'll say which choices I'd defend and which I'd change.

    Why Python and FastAPI rather than NestJS, which I used at Jigsaw? The work is I/O-bound: model calls, databases and sockets, so native async fits. The AI SDKs are Python-first, and we used numpy, Pillow and HDBSCAN. Pydantic gives us request validation and LLM schemas from the same models. The cost is CPU work under the GIL, which we push into asyncio.to_thread. Why MongoDB over Postgres? Our shapes are nested and keep changing: turns with typed parts, run rows with checkpoints, waits and an inbox. With a Pydantic model as the schema, a document fits. Honestly, Postgres would have given us joins, transactions and pgvector in one box, and I'd consider it seriously today.

    On Qdrant versus pgvector, Pinecone or Weaviate: we didn't run a bake-off, so I won't pretend we did. What we use are payload filters on user_id with payload indexes, named vectors in the feed prototype, an async client, and the fact that it self-hosts in Docker. Pinecone is managed but you can't self-host it. pgvector would remove a datastore, which is the real trade-off. FastEmbed gives local 384-dimension ONNX embeddings. That means no API call, latency or per-call cost on each memory write or search, and text never leaves our process. The price is weaker embeddings than the big APIs, and CPU on the API box, so it runs in a thread.

    Why a graph at all? The idea was to walk out from the topics in a question to related memory that text similarity misses. Memgraph speaks Cypher over Bolt, so the neo4j driver works unchanged and we could swap to Neo4j, it's in-memory and fast, and every query was scoped by user_id. It was our weakest bet. I switched graph traversal off myself in October 2025, and the March 2026 reset removed it.

    Why Redis for queues, locks, pub/sub and short-term memory instead of Kafka or RabbitMQ? We already ran it, our volumes are small, and anything that needs durability lives in a Mongo row, not in a queue. I actually tried durable first. In February 2026 I built SSE with a Redis Stream per user and replay. The next day I replaced it with plain pub/sub, because Mongo was already the source of truth and the app reloads over REST on reconnect. Kafka would buy replay and partitions we didn't need.

    Why Azure over AWS or GCP? That decision predates me, and I won't invent the business reason. What we lean on is Key Vault with managed identity for all secrets, Key Vault RSA wrapping for the vault's per-user keys, Entra auth for Redis, Blob with SAS links, and App Service slot swaps. Why APScheduler inside the API rather than Celery beat or a cloud scheduler? We ran one instance with a handful of async jobs, so a separate beat process was overhead. In August 2026 I made it multi-instance safe with a Redis SET NX EX 50 lock per firing. IRIS settles due rows in one bulk_write before running them, so a reminder fires at most once even if the process dies. The cost is that the scheduler dies with the API, but the sweep catches up because it queries everything due up to now. Ananda's November rewrite on master has since removed APScheduler.

    OpenRouter sat in front through 2025 until the v3 reset. It gave us one key and many models, so each persona could route to its own model. A teammate put OpenRouter first in the chain, then OpenAI, then Gemini, and the same day I restructured the error handling and added JSON repair. It cost us an extra hop, and we had less control over provider features like Anthropic cache markers and later native async tools. In v3 we moved to direct SDKs behind our own facade.

    We went with Browserbase and Stagehand after I ran a measured feasibility study: 21 sessions, 8 tests. Parallel tabs in one Chrome worked, and secret fill through Stagehand variables kept passwords out of the model. A Browserbase Context kept the cart across release and a crash. We also get a captcha solver, a live view so the user can take over, and we pay per browser-minute instead of for idle VMs. It also showed the costs. Stagehand's action cache hit 0 of 103 actions on Amazon, because the cache service returned 413. Stagehand can't re-attach after a restart. Each action took a 7.2 s median, and the finder's tokens dominate. Our own Chrome fleet would mean owning anti-bot, captchas, sessions and scaling, too much for a small team.

    Mongo has no joins, and that hurt in the admin dashboard in January 2026. The first version ran about ten count queries per user. I replaced it with one aggregation on users: $lookup into seven collections and $filter to count tasks by status, so it's one round trip. Was five datastores over-engineering? At peak we ran Mongo, Redis, Qdrant, Memgraph and Blob. Memgraph was over-engineering: it should go first, and it did. Redis earned its place then; the team's November rewrite on master, Ananda's, removed it and moved to native Cosmos DB. I'd keep Mongo, a vector store and Blob.

  • Incidents and debugging stories: a cached coroutine, sync clients blocking the event loop, Redis OOM, braces in f-string prompts
    Answer

    The worst bug I shipped myself was a synchronous client inside async code. On September 18, 2025, restructuring the LLM fallback code, I switched the OpenRouter client from AsyncOpenAI to the synchronous OpenAI class inside async methods, and my Exa search client the next day had the same pattern. A sync call inside a coroutine blocks the event loop for the whole model call, so nothing else on that worker runs: other users' streams freeze and unrelated endpoints wait on someone else's LLM call. There's no error; it just looks like "slow under load". My teammate Ananda moved it back to AsyncOpenAI on October 1, and another teammate replaced Exa with Perplexity on September 27. To find it: asyncio debug mode logs slow callbacks, or fire two concurrent requests and watch them take twice as long.

    The personality cache is a cousin. My teammate Ananda had put functools.lru_cache on an async function, which caches the coroutine object, not the result, so the second call with the same id gets an already-awaited coroutine and raises. On October 21, 2025 I replaced it with an asyncio.Lock-guarded OrderedDict LRU of resolved values; the weak spot I left is size 1 with no invalidation.

    Redis out of memory is my postmortem, because part of the cause was mine. Detection: the team hit Redis OOM in early July 2026; teammates made their own Redis changes that week, and I looked in my own code. My root cause: for speaker identification, live mode appended every mic chunk to a per-session key in every mode, with no TTL, and only passive mode ever popped it. At 16 kHz 16-bit that's about 1.9 MB a minute per session, forever. Fix, on July 2, in the fix/redis_oom PR, which held only my commit: buffer only in passive mode, pipeline APPEND with EXPIRE NX 60 so a buffer lives at most a minute, and delete it on reconnect, stop and release. Prevention: the same day I built a Redis memory monitor, a locked minute cron snapshotting INFO memory and DBSIZE, plus an on-demand family scan capped at 100 keys per pattern with MEMORY USAGE, and the next day I removed speaker ID, dropping about 47 packages including torch. I don't have a user-impact number, and I can't say my keys were the only cause. Now I'd require a TTL on every key family.

    Braces, in September 2025: the storage prompts were Python f-strings with JSON examples inside, and an unescaped { there is parsed as an expression, so the prompt raised a format error. I escaped the examples, and I also doubled braces in the user's text, which honestly was wrong: a value interpolated into an f-string isn't parsed again, so that only showed the model doubled braces. User braces only bite if the finished string is formatted a second time. The real lesson is keeping template and data apart, which our later {{variable}} renderer does. WhatsApp, November 2025: my Meta Cloud API send URL put the recipient's number where our business phone_number_id goes, so sends failed; I fixed it the same day.

    Consolidation, October 2025: the prompt builder read payload["summary"], but the records store text in content, so every cluster reached the model empty, and the next step overwrites the old records with the output. The commit doesn't say how I spotted it; the guard I'd add is refusing an LLM call with empty inputs. list_events: the code compared the HTTP status to the fastapi.status module itself, exc.status == status, never true, so a 401 never triggered the token refresh. I fixed it in October 2026 to check HTTP_401_UNAUTHORIZED.

    CPU work: the Resemblyzer voice encoder ran in asyncio.to_thread until I removed it with torch, and the film pipeline's Pillow and numpy cut-outs and FastEmbed run in threads too. That keeps the loop free, and those libraries release the GIL for most of their heavy work. I never measured the latency effect.

    Commit messages that don't match the diff are fair criticism: "Refactor code structure" was 21k lines of agent runtime, and the speaker-ID deletion was also called a refactor. It costs reviewers, blame, bisect and release notes, and my own audit had to read every diff. Now it's one change per commit with an honest subject.

  • Hindsight and scale: what you'd change, what breaks first at 10× / 100×, sweeps and shared graphs at scale, debt left behind
    Answer

    Three things I'd do differently. One: design for more than one instance from day one, with sticky socket routing and a bounded writer queue per socket. Audio already goes straight to the socket on our version, but sends are awaited inline and recovery assumes one process. Two: fewer stores, so no Memgraph, and probably Postgres with pgvector or Mongo plus Qdrant and nothing else. Three: tests and evals as part of done from day one: state-machine invariants, replays of real turns, a tool-choice set. For context, this is about the system I built and ran through October; in November Ananda's rewrite on master removed Redis, moved to native Cosmos DB and deployed two regions, which is the team's answer to some of this.

    At 10× the single instance breaks first, and adding a second breaks recovery, because boot seals every leftover turn buffer as failed, including the other process's live turns. Next is the voice path on each box: one shared Cartesia TTS socket per worker multiplexes every speaking user, downstream audio is about 384 kbps per speaking stream, and sends are awaited inline. Omni frames go direct, so Redis isn't in that path; only the older features fan out over one notify channel that every instance receives. Mongo and Qdrant are fine at 10× on the indexes we have. LLM spend grows linearly; it's the biggest bill, not the first break. At 100× I'd shard everything by user_id, since every query is already user-scoped: Mongo shard key on user, Redis, which already ran in cluster mode, keyed by the user hash tag that slots:{user_id} already uses, and sticky socket routing so events go straight to the owning instance.

    The minute sweeps read due rows on an index: IRIS settles them in one bulk_write, and the agent sweep runs four stages bounded by a semaphore of 8 under a 50-second lock. It stops working when one instance can't finish a minute's due set within the minute. Then I'd partition the sweep by user hash, or push timers into a delay queue keyed by due time, so work arrives instead of being scanned.

    Memgraph is in-memory, so one shared graph has to fit one machine's RAM. User-scoped indexes, depth shrinking from 6 to 3 and a 200-node cap keep each walk bounded, but a million users' writes land on one box. I'd split graphs per tenant or move edges into an adjacency table. Honestly, we never got there; we removed it.

    Durable agents at 3 slots times 1M users: slots are a cap, not a load, and most rows sleep. The infra is a separate worker pool claiming queued rows, timers in a delay queue, and Browserbase and provider rate limits. On cost, my only measured basis is the feasibility study: 1.5 to 4 minutes and about 100k to 200k finder tokens plus 14k to 50k worker tokens per add-to-cart on gpt-4.1-mini. I haven't multiplied that out, so no dollar figure.

    Debt I knowingly left: the single-instance assumption, inline socket sends, best-effort turn-log appends, no automated turn-loop tests, no check that the prompt cache hits, and losing in-flight async tool calls on restart. For a beta, iteration speed mattered more, and each one is written down. The feature I'd never have built had we talked to users first is the social side: the Ask opinions and the news-to-debate Posts feed I built from May to July 2026, which I removed myself in August. I didn't run user research then, and that's the lesson.

1.11 AiRA — tools, agents and LLM plumbing

  • Typed tools and agent loops: Pydantic schemas, hidden credential injection, progressive disclosure, APEX, registry gates, async tools
    Answer

    Tool calling: you give the model tools, each a name, description and JSON schema, and instead of text it can reply with a structured call, a name plus JSON arguments. It never runs anything. Your code validates the arguments, runs the function, and sends the result back as a tool message with the same call id, and the model continues. An agent loop repeats that until the model answers without calling a tool, or until you stop it with a step cap, a timeout, stuck detection, a budget, or an explicit done tool.

    I built our typed tool framework in November 2025. Each tool is a FunctionSpec with an argument model, a return model and an async function that takes one validated object. The schema the model sees comes from model_json_schema(mode="serialization"), run through a cycle-safe pass that inlines every $ref and $defs, and every argument has a description. The executor validates the model's raw arguments with Pydantic before calling anything. Credentials never appear. access_token and user_id are Field(exclude=True), so they're absent from the schema. The pipeline decrypts the user's Google token, and the loop injects it and the user id just before execution. Later versions inject them from the function's annotations, with the token decrypted per connector.

    Progressive disclosure fixes prompt bloat. Every tool's full schema in every prompt is big and slow, and choice gets worse. In December 2025 the orchestrator saw only agent names and descriptions, and a GET_TOOLS_INFO step loaded one agent's schemas. In April 2026 that became get_agent_info and execute_agent_tool over 8 agents. In August 2026 the unified registry made it general. Deferred tools sit in a catalog baked into the system prompt at startup, tool_search loads their schemas, and execute_tool runs them. tool_search tries a lexical match first, then falls back to cosine similarity over embeddings precomputed at startup and returns the top three, so "scheduling" finds the IRIS tools.

    APEX, in April 2026, was a separate agent because long jobs like canvas plus note plus email need many tool calls in a row, and we wanted them off the chat turn, reporting each step to the app. It streamed Claude tool use and ran all of a step's calls in parallel, matched back by tool_use_id. It had three meta-tools, get_agent_info, execute_tool and get_skill, 20 iterations, a 180 s per-tool timeout and a 90 s stream idle timeout, with live state in Redis and checkpoints in Mongo. The circuit breaker stopped the run after 5 consecutive turns in which every tool failed. On April 30 I flattened it into the main loop. Two agents meant hand-offs, a second state in every prompt and duplicated tokens; now one model had get_agent_info and execute_agent_tool plus get_skill, the loop went from 8 to 20 iterations, and the user saw each step live.

    The unified registry, from August 2026, has one declaration per tool: schema, handler and harness metadata (which agents, deferred or not, surfaces, required connector, and later an approval tier). Every dispatch runs fixed gates in order: resolve the name, check the calling agent, check the surface, check the connector is linked, validate the schema, then run the handler. The provider never sees the metadata, so tool arrays stay byte-identical, which keeps the prompt cache warm. Boot fails if any tool lacks a valid tier.

    For bad arguments, I tell the model. Crashing kills the turn, and blind retries repeat the same mistake. A tool error with the validation message usually gets fixed on the next step. The registry answers an unknown tool with "search for it first", and it's capped: three invalid done calls fail a background run.

    To stop loops running forever or burning money, there are layers. Iteration caps: the old orchestrator went from 25 to 5, the flattened main loop has 20, APEX had 20. Timeouts: 180 s per tool and 90 s on the stream. Then the circuit breaker. In durable agents there's stuck detection: the same tool with identical arguments three steps in a row that got back nothing new, or five such no-progress steps of any calls, stops the run with "no progress at" that tool. Plus a step budget per skill, 3 slots per user, and tool results capped at 40,000 bytes.

    Async tools: say a tool takes 40 seconds. In September 2026 I upgraded the OpenAI SDK for native async tool calls. The tool returns a pending output, the model tells the user it's on it, and the turn seals early. When the result lands, it re-enters on its original call id as a new system-triggered turn, and the model speaks it. Calls still running are listed in the volatile "now" message, so the model doesn't start them twice, and orphaned calls are repaired. The trade we accepted is that a restart loses in-flight async calls.

    Do I know the model picks the right tool? Not measured. We had tool events and every background model call stored on its run, so we read failures by hand. To measure it, I'd build a golden set of utterances, each labelled with the expected tool or "no tool", and run it nightly against the real tool arrays. I'd track exact-match accuracy and tool_search recall in the top three, plus argument-validation failures per tool in production.

  • Durable task runtime: run-row state machine, waits and inbox, guarded transitions, boot recovery, minute sweep, stuck detection
    Answer

    A durable agent is a background task whose state lives in a database row, not in a Python coroutine. It survives restarts, it can sleep for hours waiting on the user, and it resumes at the next step. A plain asyncio task dies with the process, can't wait days and leaves no record. I built this runtime on October 7 and 8, 2026. The task kinds are research, canvas_doc, connector and browser.

    The main model calls start_task with a kind, skill, title, brief and optional parent run. It checks, in order: browsing is enabled on this server; the skill exists under that kind, or it lists valid names; every service the skill needs is connected, or it tells the model to show a Connect card; and the parent run is one of the user's finished tasks. Then a queued row goes into AgentRuns. Admission is a Redis INCR on slots:{user}; over 3, it decrements and stays queued; otherwise an atomic FIFO claim in Mongo, decrementing again if the claim fails.

    The state machine goes queued, running, then sleeping or paused, back through queued, and ends in ready, failed or stopped. A wait says why it sleeps, like a question, credential, approval, payment or timer, each with a deadline; the inbox holds what wakes it, like a steer, an answer or an approval. Waking moves the sleeper to queued and pushes the inbox entry in the same write, and the inbox drains only while the row is running.

    Every write goes through two guarded store methods. update is a find_one_and_update with filters. transition only moves a row from an expected status. A late writer, say a timeout after the task finished, matches nothing, so a finished run never reopens.

    At boot, recover() deletes every slot counter. It re-adopts each running row that has a checkpoint, takes one slot for it and wakes it with a restart note. A running row with no checkpoint fails as "server restarted mid-run". Then every user's queue is served, and the minute sweep waits until recovery finishes. It assumes a single instance, which is how we ran through October; Ananda's November rewrite on master runs two regions without Redis, so this exact recovery is my version, not what's deployed now.

    The minute sweep runs four stages, each bounded by a semaphore of 8, under the cron lock. First, due IRIS wakes and idle Chrome releases. Second, overdue sleepers. Timer and payment waits wake to re-check, approvals re-deliver their outcome, and question, credential, connect and blocker waits end as ready with a line saying what's missing. Third, slot-counter drift. Fourth, lost "done" turns not announced after 60 seconds, approval expiry, and checkpoint retention at 90 days.

    A task reports back as system turns. [AGENT_PROGRESS] means "so far, still looking". [AGENT_NOTE] carries a question. [AGENT_DONE] is the only one spoken as finished, with the findings fenced as external data. Plus task frames and a push. Stuck detection lives in the shared loop: the same tool with identical arguments three steps in a row that got back nothing new, or five such no-progress steps, stops the run and records "no progress at" that tool.

    Why Mongo and Redis instead of Temporal, Celery or a cloud workflow service? I wrote the trade-off into the plan. Extending the run row needed no new infrastructure. It fits our layering, and the primitives already existed: guarded claims, a checkpoint field, a sweep. Temporal meant a new server and rewriting the agent loop as deterministic workflow code right before a deadline, and Celery doesn't model human waits that last days. The cost is that we own timers, recovery and once-only behaviour, but the state machine sits behind one store, so it can be swapped.

    The ask_main restart case: a restart caught a task right after it asked the main model something. In the October build, on adoption, I scanned the checkpoint for an ask_main call answered "asked" with no user line after it. The task went straight back to sleep on that same ask instead of running and asking twice. After I split asking into ask_main and ask_user on October 19, recovery became simpler: the adopted task wakes with a note saying its last question may not have reached the user, to re-check and ask again only if it still needs to.

  • LLM providers and cost: model per job, fallback chain, prompt caching with stable prefixes, strict structured output, cost per turn
    Answer

    Prompt caching means the provider keeps the processed state of a prompt's beginning. A later request that starts with exactly the same bytes reuses it, so it's cheaper and the first token comes faster. It matches by prefix, so one changed byte early on invalidates everything after it. That's why the order has to be: static tools and system prompt first, then per-user, then per-session append-only, then volatile per-turn content last. The providers AiRA used are OpenAI, Anthropic, Gemini, DeepSeek, Groq and Cerebras, through OpenRouter in 2025 and through direct SDKs since v3, plus the Jev evaluation model on TypeSafe.

    The 2025 fallback chain tried OpenRouter, then OpenAI, then Gemini. A teammate put OpenRouter at the front on September 18, 2025, and the same day I restructured the error handling and added json_repair before Pydantic, which fixes trailing commas, missing quotes or brackets and prose around the JSON. On the version I ran, the facade falls back from Cerebras to Groq for the small GPT-OSS calls.

    On caching, precisely: the Anthropic marker code was Shashwat's and Ananda's, and the OpenAI cache key Omni uses is Shashwat's. My finding was that the new stream() facade never passed the flags, so no markers were being sent. On August 19 I added CacheSpec (system, tools, messages, a boundary index and a 5-minute or 1-hour TTL), carried it through the facade, and made other providers ignore it. I put the breakpoint on the message holding history. Five days later I saw the history was a sliding window, raised from 5 to 10 turns, so its leading bytes changed every turn. I split the fixed preamble out and moved the boundary onto the preamble alone, the last message whose bytes outlive a turn. That's also why memories and the "now" block go last, and why skills are appended after the tail: anywhere above the breakpoint they'd invalidate the session's prefix. Tool arrays must be byte-identical across sessions for the same reason. Tools sit at the front of the prefix, so one reordered field misses for everyone; the registry keeps its metadata off the wire.

    Did I verify hits? Not systematically. Cached tokens are recorded per stage in TurnUsage and per call in Ananda's observability layer, but nothing tracks a hit rate, and I never used it to confirm the breakpoint move, though our own caching doc says to check it first. When I looked by hand in working sessions, ordinary calls on Luna were around 99% cached and a landed async result re-read everything after the call once; that's my observation, not a dashboard number. A tracked hit rate is the first thing I'd add.

    Model per job comes down to the latency budget and the shape of the output. Jev answers typed yes, no or choice questions inside 1.5 s. The retrieval planner and session titles use GPT-OSS 120B on Cerebras, because the planner sits on the critical path, so throughput beats depth. The main turn was Sonnet 4.6, then OpenAI's Luna after my teammate Shashwat moved it on August 28. Memory notes use DeepSeek V4 Flash and Pro, the warm-start speaker uses V4 Flash, the film planner uses Sol, and attachments use Gemini Flash Lite. As for strict structured output rejecting our Pydantic models: strict mode needs every property listed as required and additionalProperties: false, and Pydantic leaves fields with defaults out of required. So strict_schema(require_all=True) closes every object and lists every property. Tool schemas stay lenient, because an optional argument really is optional.

    Cost: I don't have a cost per Omni turn. TurnUsage records tokens and milliseconds per stage, so it can be computed, and Ananda added per-call cost estimates at the end of September and widened them across providers in November. For a browser task, the only measured basis is the feasibility study: 1.5 to 4 minutes, about 100k to 200k finder tokens plus 14k to 50k worker tokens per add-to-cart. The Gmail onboarding scrape, up to 1,000 emails, was never costed. The per-user budget was a teammate's energy system: 1,000 units a day, with an LLM call costing tokens over 1,000, rounded up, times a model-tier multiplier of 1.0, 1.5 or 2.0.

    When Omni moved from Sonnet to an OpenAI model, the voice path changed in four ways. Caching went from explicit markers to automatic prefix caching with a cache key. Assistant tool calls had to be encoded as Responses items, which I'd already fixed in the codec on August 20. I added a reasoning-effort knob. And in September I added native async tools. One gotcha I hit in a working session: the model writes a commentary line before its tool calls, and the SDK parsed that line as the structured answer, dropping the calls. So we never pass a response model on a call that offers tools, and the structured answer comes from a separate tool-less call.

    If a provider has an outage mid-voice-turn, here's what actually happens. The main model has no cross-provider fallback. If its stream errors, the turn publishes omni.process_failed instead of vanishing, and whatever was already spoken stays spoken. The side calls degrade gracefully: the planner times out at 4 s to a plan built from the raw query, memory at 3 s to no memories, and a slow Jev only costs its head start. If TTS fails, the rest of the turn goes out as text. What should happen is one retry on a second provider for the main model, a short spoken apology, and an alert.

    On streamed tool-call fragments: the OpenAI-compatible streams from Groq, DeepSeek and Cerebras send a tool call in pieces, with the id and name first and the arguments split across chunks. In August 2026 I assembled fragments by index into complete calls before parsing the arguments.

  • Observability and testing: trace_id per turn, Langfuse vs a Mongo llm_calls log, Slack alerts, what was tested, agent evals
    Answer

    In December 2025 I put one trace_id per chat turn through every stage: the pre-LLM router, the orchestrator, the main stream, the storage call, speech-to-text, the Gmail summarizer and the memory flush. Every OpenRouter call sent a request id and an llm_role such as pre-llm-pipeline, main-llm or storage-llm-pipeline. I stored the trace id on the chat turn too. The role is what makes it useful: you see which stage of a turn costs the time and the tokens, not just the total.

    In January 2026 I stood up a self-hosted Langfuse v3 stack in Docker Compose (web, worker, Postgres, ClickHouse, Redis and MinIO), remapped its ports and wrote the team's setup guide. Every model call became a generation under one root span per turn. What I owned is the deployment, ports, guide and integration, not Langfuse itself. About ten days later I replaced it with a Mongo llm_calls collection: trace id, user, role, model, tokens, input and output, behind one LLM_LOGGING_ENABLED flag. It dropped a six-container dependency and its keys for the one thing we used, per-call tokens by trace; we lost the UI.

    TurnUsage, which I added in September 2026, records milliseconds and tokens, including cached tokens, per Omni turn for the gate, planner, retrieval, reviews, every model iteration with its first token, and total. It's stored on the turn row; Ananda's usage dashboard showed it briefly before he moved usage into his observability module at the end of September. Every background model call is also stored on its run as an iteration. For crons and webhooks, I added an async context manager in January 2026. It catches an exception inside its block and posts to Slack with the job name, error type, the last 1,000 characters of the traceback and context fields. I wrapped it per user in the IRIS loop, so one failure no longer stops everyone, and around background webhook handlers, where errors had been invisible. The Slack base was a teammate's.

    Testing the voice pipeline, honestly, was mostly manual. I drive the app in the iOS simulator, tail its console through Metro's Hermes inspector, and read the turn rows in Mongo. On the client I wrote Jest tests for the barge-in cancel in screenVoice, the speaker queue, the cue speaker and the wake-word pipeline, but they were dropped in the app's Expo migration in September. On the backend I instrumented speech latency, meaning TTS first audio and STT connect and first transcript, into a p50 to p99 panel with two unit tests on the summarizer. That was removed with the analytics subsystem in July. So automated coverage of what I built is thin: today it's essentially CI on Lumen.

    Done properly, I'd test a voice pipeline at three levels. Use recorded audio fixtures through the real STT. Use fake STT, TTS and LLM to fire a barge-in at every point and assert the sealed turn equals the heard words. And run a timing harness that measures mic end to first audio byte.

    Why were my pytest files dropped from the stacked PRs? The original commits had them, and the stacked copies I made only carried product code. I also took pytest out of dev dependencies in August. What it says is that tests weren't part of our definition of done. Teammates removed the last backend tests in September and October, and on October 27 I added tests/ to .gitignore myself, so tests are local only and neither repo tracks unit tests today. We leaned on Ruff with every rule on and ty with all errors in CI, plus live runs on devices. I'd reverse that first.

    How do I evaluate an agent change? For the main model, mostly vibes plus targeted harnesses, and I'll say that plainly. For the browser agent, the feasibility study had 8 tests with written pass criteria. For films, Lumen has a paint recorder that checks what the engine actually drew, and a phone-scale report on text size, contrast and crowding. Deployment through October was Azure: Front Door in front of App Service with a health-checked slot swap. Ananda owns CI/CD; I have zero commits in deploy.yml or the Dockerfile, and since November his pipeline deploys two API regions. My deployment-side parts were Key Vault key wrapping for the user vault (loading secrets from Key Vault was Ananda's), Blob uploads with ownership checks, and multi-instance safety: cron locks, the pub/sub cancel broadcast and slot counters.

    With one week for testing, I'd start with the durable state machine and approvals against a real Mongo container. That means guarded transitions, recover() on crafted rows, slot-counter rollback, and an approved call running exactly once, because a bug there sends an email twice or spends money. Next I'd build a turn replay harness from stored turns with a scripted model, then a tool-choice golden set.

    For the browser agent's eval set, I'd use briefs across sites with a recorded expected end state, like the cart contents or stopping at the sign-in wall. I'd grade by reading the page, not by trusting the model's word, and add safety cases: deny-listed pages and prompt injection. For the film planner: briefs that must compile in Lumen with no dropped scenes, every object rendered, text size and contrast checks, a short human rubric for teaching quality, and cost and latency per film.

  • Research agent and web tools: Exa, Perplexity, Serper verticals, Parallel extraction, rounds, typed Findings, delta updates
    Answer

    An assistant needs web search because the model's knowledge stops at its training date and is shaky on specifics: prices, opening hours, news, reviews. Search grounds the answer and gives you sources. Over time AiRA used four kinds of web tools. First Exa search, which I integrated in September 2025. Then Perplexity, after a teammate swapped Exa out later that month; another teammate added multi-search, and I wrapped it as a web-search tool for the orchestrator in December. Exa came back in January 2026 through a teammate. Then Serper Google verticals: image, video and maps in June 2026, then places, news, reviews and shopping in July. And Parallel.ai, from July, to read pages.

    The intent gate I built in 2025 was one structured call per message. It returned topics to search, a rewritten search statement, a webSearch flag and a progress message. Web search was the default unless the question was about the user's own data or AiRA itself. We searched with the rewritten statement because the raw message is full of pronouns and context, like "what about the second one?", while the rewrite stands alone and is searchable. "Fresh web results win" was a rule in the answer prompt: when the web and the model's memory disagree, trust the web, and anchor "today" to the UTC time we pass in. Without it you get stale facts and wrong dates.

    Parallel's url_extract takes one to five URLs plus an objective. It returns excerpts focused on that objective, up to 10k characters per page or the full content, with a 40k total cap, failures reported per URL, and sources for citation. The cap is about context cost, latency and focus, because a full page drowns the answer. The Serper verticals I added were places, news, place reviews, sortable lowest-rated first to find dealbreakers, and shopping. The tool specs teach batching: one maps search, then reviews for every candidate in parallel.

    The research agent, from September 2026, runs in the background. The main model calls create_research with a brief. The worker runs a Luna tool loop over Google search through Serper, maps and places, and page reads, and finishes by filling a typed Findings model. Later the closing verdict became a separate tool-less structured call. It works in rounds where every call in a round runs at once. Round one is every search you can name now. Round two is everything that needed round one's ids and URLs, done for all options together. A third round happens only for a fact the brief needs. Latency is paid per round, not per call, and the only reason to wait is a real dependency. That keeps the calls few and the cost down.

    While it runs, it talks to the main model through numbered delta updates, each carrying only results no earlier update carried, and only when they're worth seeing, usually once after round one. It can ask the main model one question, and the answer comes back as steering. Page reads that land late slot into their own round, and deltas are tracked per result, so nothing is lost or repeated.

    Venue details have to come from tools because otherwise the model writes hours, prices and ratings from memory, which look plausible and can be invented. In July 2026 I made maps search the default venue tool and wrote the rule that venue details come from tools, never memory. On the synchronous Exa client, which was mine: a sync SDK call inside an async method blocks the event loop for the whole search. Under load, every request on that worker queues behind searches, sockets stall, and latency on unrelated endpoints rises. The fix is an async client or asyncio.to_thread. In our case a teammate replaced Exa about a week later, so it never got real load, but it was my mistake.

    I haven't built a research eval yet, so this is how I'd do it. Golden briefs with known answers. A citation check, where every claim in Findings must map to a fetched source that actually contains it; that part can be automated. Coverage, which the typed schema makes measurable as required fields filled. Freshness, from source dates against the brief. And cost per brief: tokens plus Serper and Parallel calls, with rounds and wall time read from the iterations we already store on each run.

1.12 AiRA — channels, connectors and other features

  • WhatsApp channel: provider moves, fast-200 webhooks with HMAC, stale-message drop, dev/prod split, WAHA LIDs, AiRA Relay MCP
    Answer

    AiRA lived inside WhatsApp because in late 2025 there was no AiRA app to install. Our mobile app repo only starts in late January 2026 and the only front end was a web app, so the assistant was something you messaged, and WhatsApp is where people already were. That's my read of the product call, not something written down. Voice arrived as voice notes we transcribed, and from December it also sent reminders and proactive messages.

    One typed message in that era: Meta posts to /webhooks/v1/whatsapp, the handler queues the work in FastAPI BackgroundTasks and answers 200 straight away. The job parses the message, drops anything older than 5 minutes, downloads media, finds or creates the user from an HMAC phone token, and uses their one whatsapp_session, shared with the app chat. Then TEMPEX, a Redis hash per user and chat changed only under a SET NX PX lock, merges a burst of short messages; if a newer one lands mid-reply, the stream is cancelled and the sent part carried into the next prompt. Then the normal streaming pipeline, each chunk sent through the Graph API.

    The provider moves. I built most of each step; the commits record what changed, not why, so the reasons are my recollection. Twilio, 21 October 2025, was the quickest way onto a number. Infobip, 14 November, was a second provider behind the same bridge. On 28 November I went direct to Meta's Cloud API and deleted the other two: no middleman, typed payloads. On 3 December the channel became Telegram, and on 20 December I moved Telegram into deprecated/ and made Cloud API the channel again, with reply and CTA buttons; as I remember it, our users simply lived on WhatsApp. Periskope, 24 December, was for groups, since a bot number only sees messages sent to it, never the user's own groups; it ran beside the Cloud API DMs. On 22 January 2026 we started on WAHA, and I removed the Periskope webhook the next day.

    A webhook must answer 200 fast because providers retry on timeouts and errors, and a retry means a double reply. Retries still come after outages, so I drop messages older than 5 minutes on WhatsApp and 300 seconds on Telegram: a late answer to a stale question is worse than none. The weak spot is that BackgroundTasks is in-process, so a crash after the 200 loses the message; a durable queue plus a dedup key on the message id is what I'd add.

    Signatures: my Periskope webhook checks HMAC-SHA256 over the raw body against x-periskope-signature with hmac.compare_digest and returns 401; my WAHA webhook does HMAC-SHA512 on x-webhook-hmac, and my Razorpay one HMAC-SHA256 with a 400. The Meta Cloud webhook's X-Hub-Signature-256 check was not mine: my teammate Shivam added it on 16 January 2026. Before that it only had the hub.challenge handshake, which proves you own the URL, not that a message is genuine. You sign raw bytes because re-serialised JSON differs. compare_digest takes constant time; a plain == stops at the first differing byte, so timing leaks how much of a forged signature is right.

    WAHA is a self-hosted WhatsApp HTTP API: the user links their own account as a linked device by typing a pairing code. Sandy and Shivam wrote the first WAHA wrappers and connect endpoint; I built the signed webhook, session lifecycle, chat import, LID resolution and typing. A LID is WhatsApp's privacy id, ...@lid, which hides the phone number, so one person can appear as a LID or a phone id. On import I fetch chats and the LID map in parallel and map them; for mentions, resolution goes cheapest first: local cache, the moderated-chat map, a global LID cache, Mongo w_lid, and only then the WAHA API. Every outgoing text marks the chat seen, shows typing and waits 50 ms per character, clamped to 0.6 to 6 seconds. The intent: replies don't land as an instant bot-like wall, and a long one never stalls.

    Dev and prod received the same Meta webhook, so a test message got two replies. I added one whatsapp_testing_phone_number setting checked two ways: a local server only handles messages sent from that number, and production ignores messages addressed to it. I also wrote a small distributor, Nginx in front of Express, that answers Meta's verification handshake at the edge and fans each /webhooks/* POST out to local backends with Promise.allSettled. Its limits: no forward timeout, and it re-serialises the body, which can break raw-body signatures. The onboarding bot number is in our dashboard: I pointed its wa.me connect link at a new bot-number setting instead of the general AiRA number, reasoning that the connect message must reach the bot running the connect flow.

    AiRA Relay is my own repo, 19 commits between 28 February and 9 March 2026; every Python line in it is mine, though it vendors the open-source WAHA Plus server. It gives any agent a WhatsApp account: a FastMCP server with about 20 tools (pair a phone, send media, summarise chats, find a contact, scan unreplied messages) plus a webhook receiver that pushes WAHA events back to the agent. MCP because any client, Claude Desktop or the OpenClaw gateway, gets WhatsApp with no custom integration. On 6 March I rewrote a 2,500-line module of module-level singletons as hexagonal: typing.Protocol ports, core services, adapters, an event bus and one composition root. It paid off two days later: replacing OpenClaw's completion endpoint with OpenRouter was a new adapter behind ILLMAdapter plus wiring; the port gained one optional argument, and interactive summaries kept MCP sampling. (The earlier move from MCP sampling to OpenClaw predates the rewrite.) Why OpenRouter, as I remember it: webhook-triggered work has no live MCP session to borrow a model from.

    The risks are real. WhatsApp's terms forbid unofficial clients and automation, so it's the user's own number that can be banned, not ours; human-like typing reduces that, it doesn't remove it. We also process messages from people who never agreed to AiRA, and a linked session is effectively the account, sitting on our server. Now I'd insist on a consent screen that names the ban risk, opt-in chats only, short retention and an encrypted session store.

  • WhatsApp group monitoring: Periskope rules, deterministic ids, per-group working memory, reply loops, unreplied-message scan
    Answer

    The rulebook let a user write rules like "tell me if anyone in my society group mentions water supply", and AiRA watched their WhatsApp groups and classified each message against the owner's rules. It was shared work. Shashwat built the rule and group models and the web app endpoints, Shivam built the classification pipeline, and I built the ingestion side: the Periskope webhook and its security, group and member sync, deterministic ids, group working memory and mentions. Later I added multi-chat rules, and on WAHA, saved suggestions and the unreplied scan.

    The Periskope webhook verifies HMAC-SHA256 over the raw body against x-periskope-signature with hmac.compare_digest and returns 401 if it's missing or wrong. Only then does it parse the typed payload and hand the work to BackgroundTasks, so the provider gets its answer immediately and never retries because we were slow. One thing I'm not proud of: that first commit also hard-coded the Periskope secret and API key in settings. My teammates stripped the hard-coded values at the end of December, and today secrets load from Azure Key Vault; anything that ever hit git should also be rotated, not just deleted.

    Group and member ids are deterministic ObjectIds: the first 12 bytes of a SHA-1 of the chat id or the phone number. Users created from chat get their _id from the same hash. So a person seen as a group member and the same person messaging AiRA one to one resolve to one user without a lookup table, and re-syncing a group is an idempotent upsert. The trade-off is that a hash of a phone number is an id, not privacy; the phone-number space is small enough to brute-force.

    Per-group working memory was a Redis list per user per group. A pipelined LPUSH plus LTRIM keeps the last N messages along with the previous decisions, the matched rule ids and urgency. It's fetched together with the user's rules in one asyncio.gather and goes into the classifier prompt. The point is that messages need context ("it's back now" means nothing alone), and seeing its earlier decisions tells the classifier it already alerted on this thread. If the user is @-mentioned, the message is prefixed "user has mentioned you saying".

    The reply loop: when AiRA posted in a group on a user's behalf, the webhook saw that post as a new group message and AiRA reacted to itself. In January I added a group-number setting and a duplicate check that skips a message when the sender is AiRA's number and the first mention is that user. On WAHA this became an ignored-numbers list checked in the webhook, plus dropping messages from the user's own account. For one rule spanning several chats, I merged groups and DMs into one WhatsappChats model and made a rule's chat ids and automations lists. On save the code diffs attached against detached chats and, in one asyncio.gather, creates an IRIS automation for each new chat, updates existing ones, and deletes the automation and its global task for removed chats. That keeps exactly one per chat, but it isn't transactional, so a partial failure can leave an orphan; my teammate Sandy's relationship model later took over that link.

    Suggestions: the groups service, Shivam's and Sandy's code, already had LLM prompts that read the chats a user chose to watch and proposed rules, but the results only came back in one response. I made them durable. They're written to a suggestions collection, GET /v1/suggestions returns the user's pending ones with chat names from one $in query, creating a rule with a suggestion_id marks it accepted, and accept and delete are both owner-scoped. Suggestions are generated only for chats where the user has sent at least one message, fromMe. The commit just says to skip those chats, so the why is my reasoning: chats you only read, like big announcement groups, produce rules nobody wants and burn LLM calls.

    The unreplied scan: I wrote it on 20 February and Ananda ported it into the restructured tree the same day. It reads last_checkin_at, makes sure the user's own LID is known, takes the 100 most recent moderated chats, and fetches DMs since the check-in in parallel. A DM counts only if the last message isn't the user's. A group counts only if someone @-mentioned the user, and we keep messages from that mention on. Mentions can be LIDs, so each goes through the chain: local cache, moderated-chat map, global LID cache, Mongo, then the WAHA API. The output is readable transcripts with real names. It's bounded with asyncio.Semaphore(10) because every chat is a call to our self-hosted WAHA serving one linked phone session, and a burst of 100 requests at once is the risk. 10 is also where I'd landed for suggestion generation after trying 15 and 30, but honestly nobody load-tested WAHA, so it's a sensible cap, not a measured one.

    The privacy problem is real. The other group members never agreed to anything, yet their messages went to an LLM provider, and we stored summaries and chat memory. What I'd do: process only chats the owner opted in, keep the alert rather than the transcript, short retention, no reuse beyond the owner's rules, and be explicit that AiRA only shows the owner what they could already read themselves.

  • LinkedIn via Unipile: hosted auth link, profile URL → knowledge file, state misuse, ToS and account-ban risks
    Answer

    Unipile is a third-party API that connects a user's LinkedIn account through a hosted login and then lets you read profiles and messages through its endpoints. Why not LinkedIn's own API: I built that direct OAuth first, code exchange at /oauth/v2/accessToken, the token stored AES-encrypted on the user, then /v2/userinfo, and with OpenID sign-in that only gives you the signed-in member's basic profile. As I remember the decision, the Darwin recruiter persona needed other people's profiles, which the open API doesn't give you, so we went through Unipile.

    Hosted auth: we request a hosted link that expires in 15 minutes and carries our internal user id. The user logs in on Unipile's page, the callback tells us the account, and we fetch it and parse it into typed models (account, premium features, organisations, proxy), then upsert into unipile_accounts. A reusable httpx wrapper came out of that work. For enrichment, a regex URL extractor with platform classification spots a LinkedIn link in a chat message, Unipile resolves the profile, the full payload goes into unipile_profiles, and an LLM "recruiter analyst" writes a structured summary that's stored as a knowledge file. Darwin's orchestrator can ask for it by id, and a profile arriving moves the funnel forward.

    Using the user id as the OAuth state was a mistake, and I call it out myself. state must be unguessable, single use and bound to the user who started the flow. A user id is predictable, so an attacker can authorise their own LinkedIn and deliver a callback carrying the victim's id. That links the attacker's LinkedIn to the victim's AiRA account, classic OAuth CSRF, and since nothing is single use, a callback can be replayed. By October our connectors did it properly: the Google flow used an HMAC-signed state carrying a one-time nonce kept in Redis, and the newer Swiggy and Stripe Link connectors added PKCE on top.

    Those commits also carried dev scaffolding: a hard-coded tunnel URL for callbacks, a fixed profile id and print debugging. In one merge I did keep our variable over the other side's hard-coded token. How I keep this out of main now: URLs and ids come from typed settings validated at boot, never literals. Lint has to fail the build, and today our Ruff config selects ALL, which bans print, while CI runs ruff check, a format check and ty check on every PR. Scaffolding stays on a local branch or behind a local-only setting, and anything secret that ever hit git gets rotated, not just deleted.

    The ToS risk falls on the user. LinkedIn's terms prohibit scraping and third-party automation, and a service like Unipile drives the user's real session, so LinkedIn can restrict or ban a professional account the user cares about far more than they care about us. There's also a third-party problem: the profile owner never agreed to be summarised. I'd keep it read-only and low volume, show a clear consent with that risk, store a summary rather than the raw payload, and prefer official APIs wherever they cover the need.

  • Multimodal chat input: image, document and audio content parts, early STT, resume parsing, the image-generation agent
    Answer

    A chat message could carry text, images, documents and several audio clips at once. In November 2025 I moved the chat endpoint to multipart form data with q plus lists of images, files and audio. In an OpenAI-style API each item becomes a content part next to the text: image_url with a base64 data URL, file with the filename and data, and input_audio with base64 data and a format. I read all the attachments concurrently with asyncio.gather and sent them to OpenRouter.

    The "closed file" bug: FastAPI's UploadFile objects belong to the request. Once the handler returns a StreamingResponse, request handling is over and the uploads' temp files get closed, but the generator body only runs after that. So any read inside the generator hit a closed file. The fix was to read the bytes before returning the StreamingResponse and pass the bytes in. It's a lifetime bug: work that outlives the request must not hold request-owned resources.

    Speech-to-text before Deepgram and Cartesia was Gemini 2.5 Flash through OpenRouter. It started as a task at the very beginning of the request, so it overlapped the other setup, and the transcript was added to the query before selection, generation and storage. At first it returned strict JSON, {text}. In December, while adding Telegram voice notes, I rewrote it as a plain-text multimodal completion, and the same helper also handled files. The commit doesn't say why; my reasoning was that a transcript is one string, so a JSON wrapper only adds ways to fail, like an escaping problem or a truncated object that breaks the whole parse, plus extra tokens.

    Voice-note bug: the transcript drove the reply but wasn't written into the stored chat, so history and memory saw an empty user message. In January 2026 I wrote the transcript back into body.query, so everything after transcription sees the spoken text. The lesson is one source of truth for the turn's text. For Telegram media types I took the response header first, then the file extension, then magic bytes for JPEG, PNG and GIF, because downloads often come back as a generic binary type. Voice was downloaded in parallel with other media, and a media error degraded to "no media" instead of failing the turn.

    Resume parsing ran in a background task: attached files went to a strict-JSON parser for contact details, experience, skills, education, projects and certificates, and the result was saved on the user's profile. Background because the user is waiting for a reply, not for a profile update, and a parser failure must never break the turn.

    The image agent, December 2025: the orchestrator calls generate_image_and_send_to_user with a detailed prompt. The service calls OpenRouter chat completions with modalities=["image"] on a configurable model, decodes the base64 data URLs that come back, uploads the bytes to WhatsApp's media endpoint to get a media id, and sends the image by that id; for its first days it was a Telegram multipart upload. The tool also tells the assistant not to announce the image twice. The first version used a blocking requests.post. Inside an async def that blocks the event loop thread, so every other request on that worker freezes for the seconds an image takes. I swapped it for our async HTTP client a day later.

    The tool description banned real people and NSFW content and gave a prompt formula. Prompt rules are not enough. They're advice to a model that can be talked around. You still need the provider's own filters, a moderation check on the prompt, and logging so abuse is visible.

    A 40 MB PDF plus three voice notes at once: the slow parts are the upload over a phone network, three transcriptions (run them in parallel), and a model reading a huge document before its first token. The expensive part is the PDF's tokens, paid on every turn it's re-sent. I don't recall a size cap in that first multipart version, and the commits show none. Later designs fixed the shape: bytes go phone to Azure Blob through a short-lived SAS URL so the API never carries them, enrichment has a size cap with a 413 (my 5 MB before Claude in April 2026, 50 MB on today's Gemini path), and the model sees one-line stubs and opens a file only when it needs it. I'd also cap audio duration and page count, and extract and chunk big PDFs instead of sending them whole.

    On resumes I should have asked: did the user explicitly agree to parsing, and for what purpose? How long do we keep it, and can they see and delete it? Is it encrypted at rest? Does the LLM provider retain it? It's sensitive personal data, so purpose limits and deletion on request should come first.

  • Attachments opened on demand (stubs, open_file, semantic search, SAS downloads) and FCM reply push notifications
    Answer

    A forced tool call is setting tool_choice to one named tool, so the model must answer by calling it, and its output is the tool's arguments, shaped by the schema. That's reliable structured output. I built attachments in April 2026: the bytes land in Azure Blob, then POST /v1/attachments/{fId} loaded them and sent them to Claude Haiku 4.5 as a document or image block with tool_choice forced to record_attachment_metadata, returning a title of up to 80 characters and a one or two sentence description, with 415 for unsupported types and 502 if the tool call was missing. Five days later my teammate Shashwat moved enrichment to a Gemini Flash Lite call with schema-validated structured output, which is what runs today.

    Enrichment is idempotent. If a record exists we return it without a new LLM call. Under a race, two requests both miss and both call the model, but a unique key means the loser gets a duplicate-key error and returns the winner's record. Worst case is one wasted LLM call, never two records.

    The model sees one-line stubs instead of the files ([ATTACHMENT_FILE] in my version, [FILE] blocks today), because re-sending a PDF on every turn costs thousands of tokens each time. A turn's attachments are linked to that turn's memory cluster, so retrieval surfaces the stub when it's relevant. If the model needs the content, it calls open_file("attachment:<id>"), and we download the file and add it to the next user message as a real Anthropic document or image block. Before sending to Claude I capped files at 5 MB; my code comment gave the reason as Anthropic's per-block limits and the latency of large payloads. Today's Gemini path allows up to 50 MB. Either way the model knows every file exists but pays for one only when it reads it.

    Semantic search: after enrichment, "title plus description" is embedded and upserted into Qdrant with a deterministic uuid5 id, so re-indexing is idempotent, and an indexing failure never fails the upload. The always-loaded search_attachments tool embeds all queries in one call, runs the vector searches in parallel, keeps the top 5 at score 0.45 or more filtered by user, de-duplicates, and fetches the records with one $in query. That's how "the PDF I sent last week" works.

    Download links: the route checks the record belongs to this user, then signs. The URL is a read-only SAS from a user delegation key, valid one hour by default, and the adapter caps any link at seven days. The blob key embeds the user id, so another user's id produces a key no URL is issued for, and the batch endpoint filters requested ids down to the ones the user owns before signing. If a link leaks, it dies in an hour.

    FCM is Firebase Cloud Messaging. A push token identifies one app install on one device, not a user, so a user can have several and tokens rotate. Reply push, March 2026: after a non-system turn, send_fcm_if_registered looked up the user's Android token and called my teammate Ananda's Firebase wrapper, with title "AiRA" and the reply minus expression tags, cut to 200 characters. It's best-effort: errors are logged and never break the turn, because the reply already reached the app live, and a Firebase outage must not fail a chat. The gap was Android only; today push goes through a device entity Ananda and Shashwat built that stores both APNs and FCM tokens.

    On the revert: on 13 March 2026 I pushed two client commits, titled just "revert", that removed Ananda's Firebase push integration and his dev CI workflow, merged as one PR. The message records no reason, so what I can give you is what the diff shows plus my recollection: it was a clean removal across Android, iOS and JS, plus restoring the PCM player bridge, rather than a partial disable, which fits it breaking our builds. The same day I wired his backend Firebase wrapper into the reply flow, so it wasn't a rejection of push. I won't dress up the conversation; the right way is to tell him first with the evidence, say a revert isn't a judgement, and help re-land it in smaller pieces.

    If the description is wrong, search silently fails. To detect it, I'd log searches that return nothing and then see the user browse to the file anyway, which is a labelled miss, and keep a small eval set of queries against known files. To fix: embed the filename and some extracted text too, add keyword matching on the title, let the user edit the title, and re-enrich with a better model. Because the ids are deterministic, re-indexing is safe.

  • News ingestion and the global fact store: RSS/Atom feeds, triage editor, tombstones, user-contributed facts, poisoning risk
    Answer

    This was our social news platform, Posts, Asks and Takes, in July and August 2026 before the product moved to Omni. The surrounding post code was shared with Shashwat and Ananda, and the pieces below are mine. RSS and Atom are both XML feeds a publisher exposes: title, link, date, a summary. Atom is the stricter spec with required ids and update times; RSS is looser. Both are free and come straight from the publisher, but you get what they put in, usually no full text, and no search or cross-source dedup. A paid news API gives search, ranking and normalised sources, and you pay per call. I wrote one stdlib RssAdapter for both formats, later adding Google Trends with a traffic filter.

    JSON-LD is schema.org data publishers embed in a <script type="application/ld+json"> tag for search engines. A news article there carries the body text, headline, dates and image. Reading it gives clean article text without fighting each site's HTML. The fallback was Parallel.ai extraction, and the hero image came from JSON-LD or og:image.

    I replaced SerpApi, which I'd added myself, with RSS on 5 August. The commit doesn't state the reason; mine was cost: SerpApi is paid per search and we swept the home feed plus every section every 6 hours, while RSS costs nothing. About 20 curated publisher and Google News feeds are fetched in one gather. Then a DeepSeek V4 Flash "triage editor" dedupes the same story arriving from many feeds, drops junk and picks at most 100, which caps what everything downstream costs. A link that keeps failing gets a NewsDrops tombstone after 3 failures, so a paywalled or broken page doesn't burn an extraction every run forever. The composer can answer publishable=false: if you force a model to always produce a post from thin or contradictory text, it pads or invents. A structured "no" is cheaper than a hallucinated story, and the pipeline deletes any partial work.

    User facts: only public chats contribute, and I have to be straight that chats were public by default, with the user able to mark one private, so it was an opt-out, which is weaker than it should have been. A fact the user states is queued, and an hourly Redis-locked cron routes it. It looks for candidate posts from the last two days by vector search at a 0.8 floor, then a router picks one of three actions, or drops the claim as not routable: attach it as a Take on a recent post, have a web-verifying composer agent write a new story, or absorb it into the knowledge store. Executors run four at a time. Honest gap: my own docstring says a crash mid-batch can restage a Take, because the executors aren't guarded against re-runs.

    The store has two tiers: raw Knowledge points and consolidated Facts. The hourly synthesis refines the raw claims with nearby existing facts as context. For each new fact it finds stored facts at 0.88 similarity or more, and a cheap model decides whether the new one supersedes them, meaning a later value, a correction or a restatement. If that check fails or it can't tell, both facts are kept. Recall uses a 0.65 floor, over-fetches three times and reranks with an exponential decay of 0.0006 per hour; anything at 0.90 or above is exempt, so decay only breaks near-ties. A content patch from the opinion agent applies only if its citations came from pages the agent actually opened: its tools record every URL they surfaced, so the model can't cite a page it never read or make one up. That's a grounding check in code, not trust in a prompt.

    Poisoning is the real problem: one user's false claim becomes everyone's answer, injected into chat turns without anyone asking. What we had was not enough. There was the public-chat gate, web verification and the right to decline on the composer path, and a supersession rule that keeps both facts when unsure. I'd add corroboration from independent users or a source before anything reaches the shared tier, per-user rate limits and trust, quarantine for new facts, provenance on every fact so one user's input can be purged, and showing it as "users report", not as truth.

    On 18 August I deleted the whole platform, 43 files and about 4,900 lines, with no data migration. I think it was right. The commit doesn't spell out the reasoning, so this is how I remember deciding: the product had moved to Omni, nothing reachable used it, and dead code costs every reader and every refactor. Our own rule is that removing a feature removes its whole surface. Git keeps the code and the collections were simply no longer read, so it was reversible. What I'd do differently is have a plan for the data left behind, because those collections still hold user contributions, and orphaned personal data needs a retention decision too.

  • Smaller systems: Razorpay subscriptions, coin purchases, quests, Darwin persona, crawler, vote tallies, multi-session chat, speaker ID
    Answer

    Five things I built at AiRA that aren't on my resume: the WhatsApp channel and the MCP relay, Razorpay subscriptions, the gamification layer of coins, bond levels and quests, the Darwin recruiter persona, and the Ask and Takes opinion tallies. Also a Playwright crawler, multi-session chat, a relevance-engine prototype, and speaker identification, which I later deleted.

    Razorpay, December 2025: during onboarding the assistant agreed a monthly price and called get_subscription_link with the negotiated amount. The backend created a per-user plan and a subscription, stored it as pending, and returned the link. Amounts at or below ₹10 were refused, and so was a second plan when one was already active. The webhook checks HMAC-SHA256 on the raw body with compare_digest and returns 400 if it's invalid. Processing upserts the subscription on its id and the payment on invoice plus subscription, setting created_at only on insert, so Razorpay's retries are harmless: the same event writes the same state. Weak spot: the model sets the price and the only server-side guard was the floor; I'd bound the range on the server.

    Coins: a purchase is a single Mongo update filtered on coins >= price, so two concurrent buys can't both pass, and you get 402 if you're short. Later it returns 409 if you already own the item, and the inventory insert became an upsert on user plus item. The decrement and the insert aren't in one transaction, so a crash between them could cost coins; a transaction would close that. Quests cost no extra LLM call: the chat request carries quest_id, and the storage LLM that already runs after every turn gets an active-quest block and returns quest_completed and progress notes. A daily cron assigned quests, 20 users at a time. The energy system was my teammate Sandy's. The crawler, October 2025, used one shared headless Chromium, a semaphore of 10 pages, a depth limit I tuned from 2 down to 1, and a Redis list as the queue, with child links pushed at depth plus one. I don't quote a pages-per-minute figure because we never measured one.

    Darwin was a recruiter persona that walked candidates on WhatsApp through a staged funnel. The script is data: ordered objective files, knowledge files and sample dialogues. Each turn an orchestrator LLM sees the current objective, key moments and recent turns, and returns whether to transition, which objective, and which knowledge to load. After the reply, a state tracker returns only the changed fields, which are deep-merged into the candidate's record. The thinking behind data was that non-engineers could change the funnel without a deploy; behind changed-fields-only, that the output stays small and the model can't clobber fields it never meant to touch.

    Ask and Takes: counts must never come from the LLM, because they're arithmetic and a model will happily produce plausible wrong numbers. The model only classifies each Take into an option, and code does the tally. An AskStats document is updated with $inc deltas (old option minus one, new plus one, total plus one only on a first submission), so placement reads one document whatever the number of Takes. An hourly exact rebuild repaired drift, then it was paused and deleted, so at the end there was no repair if a write was lost. Stale submissions are blocked with a find_one_and_update guarded on the exact staged text. Multi-session chat, May 2026: short-term memory moved from one Redis key per user to stm:{user}:{session}, and history and rewind were scoped to the session. From then on every WebSocket frame, and every TTS audio chunk, carried sId, so the app routes each stream to the right tab. A background job titles the session, and status writes were folded into existing gathers so they cost no extra wait.

    The relevance engine was a prototype: one commit of about 3,500 lines on a branch, never merged. Events come in batches of up to 50, are deduplicated by idempotency key with $setOnInsert upserts, and are folded into one per-user-per-post state document, with no LLM on that path. An LLM scores each post on 12 factors, stored in Qdrant as two named vectors, 384-d semantic and 12-d factors. A compactor builds interest clusters from weighted events (like 1.0, save 2.0, share 2.3, not interested minus 2.0). The mixer pulls candidates in parallel, filters, ranks with fixed weights, then applies diversity rules: never the same author twice in a row, at most two of one type or cluster. The weights were hand-set with no evaluation.
