    Speaker identification, May 2026: in passive mode we sliced buffered audio by Deepgram's diarized word timestamps, made a Resemblyzer voice embedding on a thread, and matched it in Qdrant at cosine 0.80, so transcripts said who spoke. I built the core; Shashwat added the review endpoints. I deleted it on 3 July, in a commit I titled as a readability refactor, which I'd label honestly today. The commit gives no reason, so this is my recollection: it dropped about 47 packages including torch and CUDA, the per-session audio buffers were costing Redis memory the same week we fixed a Redis out-of-memory problem, and we were moving to one STT profile without diarization. The threshold moved 0.80, 0.85, 0.80 and we never measured accuracy, so we were paying for a feature we couldn't show worked.

  • Client and web pieces: wake-word matching, film WebView player, streaming web chat, Next.js SSR and route guard, meeting recap blocks
    Answer

    I committed to four front ends. The legacy Next.js web app, July to November 2025, about 180 commits of my own plus merges. The React Native app, later Expo, from February to October 2026, about 90 authored commits, plus co-author credit on about 20 of my teammates' squash merges that carried my code or re-listed my commits. The aira-home website, 17 commits across Nuxt and then Hugo. And the small Next.js dashboard, 2 authored commits plus 5 merges of teammates' work. A Next.js middleware, renamed proxy in Next.js 16, runs before a route renders and can redirect or rewrite based on the request, typically cookies.

    My dashboard guard is about 19 lines. Public prefixes like /setup, /_next and /assets pass, and anything else without an access-token cookie redirects to /setup. That is not security. Anyone can set a cookie with that name. It's UX, so a signed-out user doesn't land on a broken, empty page. Real authorization is the backend verifying the JWT's signature and expiry on every API call. My teammate Sandy later added CSP nonces and security headers to that file.

    The SSR migration in the web app, on a branch that reached our dev branch, moved pages to async server components. They read the auth cookie with next/headers, call the backend, prefetch in parallel with Promise.all (the memory page loads user, counts, status and the first 100 memories at once), and pass initial props down so the client hooks skip their first fetch. The Redux store became one per request: makeStore() per React tree, hydrated with the user. A module-level store on a server is shared by every request, which can leak one user's data into another's render.

    Streaming chat on the web: a fetch POST, then the body piped through TextDecoderStream. I keep a buffer across chunks, cut at each }\n and parse NDJSON objects one by one, so a half-received object waits for its tail. m is a status line, d an answer delta, l the number of retrieved contexts. For reloads, the chat page opens a second GET stream on /v1/chats/status when it mounts and keeps the input locked until it reports completed, so a reload mid-answer can't fire a duplicate request.

    The wake-word classifier, inside the Live Mode pipeline my teammates Sahad and Sanket built, strips fillers and punctuation, then runs trigger regexes in priority order, stop over deactivate over activate, each capturing the next word. An accept set (aira, ira, ayra, kira) and a reject set (are, our, array, error, aria) decide instantly, so "hey Ira" passes and "hey array" doesn't. Anything else must pass a Metaphone gate, sounding like AR, IR or ER, and then Jaro–Winkler similarity to "aira" of at least 0.85. It scans a rolling 8-second window of final utterances plus the current partial, because the iOS recogniser can split "hey" and "aira" across two recognition tasks, and it debounces each verdict for 1.5 seconds so a partial and its final don't trigger twice. False accept and reject rates were never measured, and that file went away in the Expo migration. I also deleted our custom Swift VAD, 279 lines; my reasoning was that the recogniser's own endpointing already detects the pause, and two detectors disagreeing is worse than one. That left one tested TypeScript pipeline.

    The doubled Deepgram audio: an activity screen and its voice sheet each mounted a second mic PCM forwarder while Live Mode's forwarder stayed mounted underneath, so every frame went up twice and the transcript came out corrupted. I don't have notes on the exact debugging steps; the symptom, a garbled, duplicated transcript only on those screens, points at the input side, and the cause was the extra forwarders. I removed them, and Sahad then made a single mic-capture owner. The lesson is that a hardware resource needs exactly one owner.

    The film player, in its first version, is a self-contained HTML page in a WebView with a strict CSP: default-src 'none', images only as data URLs, inline script plus 'unsafe-eval' because the engine's schema validators compile with new Function. Navigation is locked, and the spec is embedded as an escaped JSON string so model text can never close the script tag. Native audio is the clock: film time follows the audio position, and requestAnimationFrame interpolates between progress events with a clock that never goes backwards. My reasoning: WebView timers drift and get throttled, while a viewer notices audio glitches instantly, so the picture should follow the sound. Going full screen moves the only WebView without restarting the audio.

    The meeting recap became data: I refactored Sanket's recap screens into a session plus chapters, each a list of {type, args} blocks, rendered from a 30-entry registry. The LLM picks blocks and arguments, so new recap layouts need no app release. An old app skips unknown block types and hides missing chapters instead of crashing. Speaker avatars get a stable colour from a string hash mod 6, so the same name always gets the same colour, plus the first letter as the initial.

    The MIND page on the old Nuxt site laid out our memory paper as web sections; I built its first version, Ananda renamed and extended it the same day, and I'm listed there as a co-author. The download fetches the PDF into a Blob, makes an object URL and clicks a temporary link with download="MIND.pdf", because a plain link often just opens the PDF in a tab. On the static Hugo site, signed-in state comes from a script that calls /v1/users/me with credentials: "include" and shows or hides nav elements marked for signed-in or signed-out users, re-checking after each client-side navigation. A static page has no server session, so asking the API is the only reliable signal.

1.13 AiRA — generative UI and canvas

  • OpenUI Lang: UI statements streamed beside speech, REPAINT vs PATCH, client-resolved image slots, prompt generated from components
    Answer

    Generative UI means the model decides what goes on the screen, not just what it says. In a voice-first app that matters a lot: AiRA should say one or two sentences while the screen holds the list, the map or the steps. Our prompts put it as: the screen does the explaining and the voice points at it.

    How we got there: we started with about a dozen render_* tools, one per card. In August I first collapsed them into one card tool, then replaced the tools with OpenUI Lang. The model writes name = Component(args) lines inside its normal text, inside a ui block. A stream filter splits it: text outside the block goes to speech, and the Lang lines go to the phone, which draws each line as it arrives. The fence started as <<ui>> when I built it in August; in September my teammate Shashwat moved it to a Markdown code fence; and on October 16 I changed it to a <ui> line and a </ui> line, because a code block inside a component's Markdown text closed the backtick fence early and the code leaked into speech. Now </ui> only closes at a line edge, and a Lang statement is one line, so a string can't end the program.

    Why a streamed language instead of tools? Tool arguments are a JSON blob, so the screen waits for the call; Lang paints line by line, and the model names a child before defining it so the layout mounts while content streams. Speech and screen come out of one generation, and the prompt shrinks to one stable catalog. Arguments are positional only, and the protocol tells the model never to write name=value, because a named argument lands in the wrong slot and breaks the render.

    Keeping the prompt in sync with the components: first I had a Node generator in the API repo that built the protocol prompt from one component schema; it was removed in early September. Then I wrote one in the app using the TypeScript compiler API: it reads each component's JSDoc signature and Props interface and fails if they disagree, and Sahad later extended it to cross-check the parser's own component table. The honest gap is across repos: the catalog the backend loads is still a prose file people edit in the API repo, so it can drift. I'd add an API CI check against the app's table.

    The model never writes image URLs; it writes Image("description") or GeneratedImage("description"). Models invent URLs, real ones need signing and expire, and the description is the better input anyway. The phone draws a placeholder and asks the server to resolve the slot. The server checks the user owns the session, runs image search or generates the picture into blob storage with a signed URL, writes it back into the line, streams the filled line, and stores it so a cold start restores it. Every failure still answers, because the phone has no timeout.

    REPAINT versus PATCH: the screen persists across turns and each name keeps its last value. A repaint is a new root line, which replaces the page; a patch re-sends only the lines that changed. Patches are fewer tokens, faster, and keep the reader's place. New things always get fresh names, because an old name that's off screen may still be bound and would come back. Changes the user makes between turns, like paging through steps, sat in a Redis hash on my branch and were handed to the next turn; in November the team rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis, so that piece is theirs now.

    Versioning with old app builds still installed: we don't have a protocol version on the wire today. The levers are coarse: a health endpoint returns the current app version per platform, which is the hook for asking old builds to update, and the client keeps aliases so old stored screens still replay. What I'd build: the app sends a hash of its component table on the socket handshake, the server picks the matching catalog for the prompt, changes stay additive, so new components and optional arguments only at the end and never reordering positional ones, and an unknown component draws nothing and gets reported. The backend protocol and slot resolution are mine; the component folder is my teammate Sahad's, and on the client I removed about 7,700 lines of old card-envelope and duplicate activity code, in a screen mostly built by Sahad and Sanket, when it moved to one Lang renderer.

  • Jev gate and warm-start opener: typed pre-LLM decisions with a 1.5 s timeout, failing open, never speaking at the wrong moment
    Answer

    Jev is a fast typed evaluation model from TypeSafe AI. It never writes text; it only answers multiple-choice and yes/no questions with a probability for each option. We use it as a gate before the main model with a 1.5 second budget. My teammate Shashwat wrote the adapter; I built the gate itself, the questions and how the answers are used.

    Before each turn, one batched evaluation decides: should we fetch memories, which short acknowledgment clip AiRA plays as the user finishes, and, when the screen has them, whether to move the reader's step or beat in a guided run, which ranking chips or sort to apply, and which component to scroll to and highlight. The first version in September also chose an early web search, with an objective and up to three queries, and which skills to load; we moved both back to the main model within about a week, web search on September 24 and skills on October 1, since it has those tools anyway. Each answer has a floor, for example 0.30 for memory and 0.50 for moving the screen. The decisions are stored on the turn, sent to the phone, and given to the main model in a short section, so it doesn't redo a screen move Jev already made. While the user is still talking, Jev also picks AiRA's face and listening sound. Why a gate: these are classification questions, and a typed answer is faster than a full LLM call and can't invent a tool argument.

    If the gate is slow or fails, it fails open. After 1.5 seconds we give up and use a fixed default: fetch memory, since that's the safe side, and make no screen moves. The main model still has its own tools and can do all of it, so a missed gate costs one turn its head start, never the turn. Every call is recorded with its duration and every probability. The weak spot is that the floors are hand-set constants with no labelled set behind them to tune against, and I haven't built a percentile view of gate latency yet. That's what I built through October; the team's November rewrite carried the gate over with the same 1.5 second budget.

    The warm-start opener is mine, later extended by Shashwat: AiRA can speak first when you open the app. Ahead of time, a small fast model turns follow-up briefs into up to three waiting lines per user. When the app opens, Jev reads the moment: the clock, the last conversation, the screen, and recent app events like connects and moving to the foreground. It picks a waiting line, a greeting category or silence, with a 2 second budget, because a fresh connection's first call ran past one second. To avoid speaking at the wrong moment: silence is a real option that can win, a line that's no longer relevant loses to "not relevant", and nothing is voiced while the user is speaking; if the mic says they're talking, the open goes out silent and spends nothing. A failed Jev call here means silence. That's the opposite of the gate on purpose: for a turn, failing open is cheap; for an opener, being quiet is cheap and speaking at the wrong moment is expensive.

  • Canvas files: AI-written HTML mini-apps with a Node vm sandbox, slides and Word as JSON with deep-merge patches, skill files
    Answer

    A canvas file is a document AiRA writes and the app shows at once, and later finds and edits. It's one of three types: an HTML mini-app, a PowerPoint-style slide deck, or a Word-style document. I built the first version in March, my teammate Sandy extended it, and I rebuilt the tool on my own in April for the new architecture.

    The canvas tool has create, update, rewrite, search and theme calls, and every change re-indexes the file's description in Qdrant and pushes a WebSocket event. Rewrite is the interesting one. When AiRA changes an app's structure, the user's saved data has to be reshaped, so the model writes a JavaScript migration function. That's untrusted code on our server, so it runs in a short-lived Node subprocess, in a vm context with only safe globals like JSON, Date and Math and a 5 second timeout, with a rollback snapshot kept on the file. Honest weaknesses: Node's vm isn't a security boundary, since host objects passed in can be walked back to the real process; the subprocess inherits our environment; and the timeout only covers synchronous code. I'd move it to an isolate or a locked-down container with an empty environment and a hard wall-clock kill.

    Why the server rejects localStorage: the app saves an HTML app's state through window.data, which the app tracks and persists through the native bridge and our API. If the model uses localStorage, state lives inside the WebView where we can't see it, save it or restore it on another device. Prompt rules alone didn't stop it, so the server rejects HTML that uses localStorage, sessionStorage or the bad init patterns, with an error telling the model what to fix. The window.data idea was my teammate Sandy's; the server enforcement is mine.

    Slides were JSON objects of type text, image or shape, each with x, y, w and h on a 10 by 7.5 inch grid. Word documents were sections with headings, paragraphs, bullet and numbered lists, and tables. JSON, because a model can't reliably write OOXML and we wanted to edit pieces. Edits send a partial patch that's deep-merged, so changing one theme colour keeps everything else: fewer tokens, and no risk of the model dropping a slide while retyping the deck. The gotcha is that only objects merge and lists are replaced wholesale, so editing one slide means re-sending the slide list; id-keyed items or JSON Patch operations would fix that.

    update_file on HTML replaces the first exact occurrence of old_str with new_str. Two things go wrong. If the snippet appears twice, it silently edits the first, maybe the wrong button. Worse, if it isn't found at all, Python's replace returns the content unchanged and the tool still reports success, so the model thinks it edited. The fix is to count matches: zero is an error, more than one is an error asking for more context, exactly one applies.

    Finding a file from last week: every file's description is embedded in Qdrant with the user and source as filters; a search embeds the query, takes the top 10, drops duplicates and loads the files from Mongo. For "last week" specifically I'd add a date filter.

    Skill files: the orchestrator prompt used to carry every file type's rules. I moved them on demand: the model sees a short list, frontend, canvas-html, canvas-powerpoint and canvas-word, loads one by setting skill_name, and its rules are injected for that task. The canvas prompt shrank by about 400 lines, for the cost of one extra step when a task needs a skill.

    python-docx was added and removed the same day, and as far as the code shows, rendering stayed in the app, from the structured JSON. The JSON is the source of truth, the app draws it natively and a patch shows instantly; server-side rendering would mean generating files and re-rendering on every edit. A .docx export would be a separate on-demand step.

    And frontend_skill.md: honestly, it reads like a public skill I pulled in, and I committed it without credit; I removed it from the prompts in April. The right way is to check the source's license, keep a header in the file with the source and license, list it in third-party notices, which I do in Lumen, or rewrite it in my own words if the license doesn't allow reuse. Prompts are content, and licenses apply to them.

  • JSON-driven native UI engine: state, derived, actions and ui tree; safe expression parser; action steps; validation before render
    Answer

    This was a branch-only prototype from April 2026 that didn't ship. The problem: AI-made mini-apps were HTML in a WebView, which starts slowly, doesn't feel native and runs arbitrary JavaScript. Before building, I compared three ways to render LLM-generated UI in a spike repo: HTML and JS in a WebView, React Native code generated and compiled on the device, and native components driven by JSON. I picked the third for safety, native feel and payload size. For an LLM product, JSON is a small target the model can hit and you can validate it before anything shows.

    A schema has four sections: state, derived, actions and ui. The flow: normalize, which parses the JSON, expands compact aliases and maps HTML-ish names like div to View; validate every component, action type and expression up front; hold state in a store and recompute derived values when it changes; render the tree recursively from a component registry, skipping nodes whose visibleWhen is false and repeating each with the item and index in scope; then events run named action flows, and state saves back through the canvas API. Props resolve four ways: bind reads a state path and wires onChange, expr evaluates an expression, action wires the executor, and double-brace strings are interpolated. I validate the whole schema first because a half-drawn broken app is worse than a clear error, and a bad expression should fail at load, not on a tap. If rendering still throws, a try/catch shows a "Canvas render failed" panel instead of crashing the screen.

    Why not eval the model's expressions? Because that hands model output the whole JavaScript runtime: network, storage and the native bridge. So I wrote my own tokenizer, parser and evaluator. The tokenizer uses sticky regexes for identifiers and numbers and matches multi-character operators first; a recursive-descent parser builds an AST; the evaluator walks it and can only call whitelisted helpers. Recursive descent encodes precedence as a chain of functions, loosest first: conditional, logical or, logical and, equality, comparison, additive, multiplicative, exponent, unary, then member access and calls, then primary values. Each level parses its operands by calling the next tighter level, so tighter operators bind first; left-associative levels loop, and exponent recurses to the right. Prototype escapes are blocked by refusing __proto__, prototype and constructor on every member access, including computed brackets, and the scope is a whitelist with no globals.

    Then the part I'd own up to. A later commit added a fallback: when the safe parser couldn't handle a model expression, it ran it as real JavaScript through Function with a with (scope) block, and the validator accepted anything that compiled. The reason was that models wrote block arrow functions, spreads and new Date().toISOString(), and screens went blank. But it removed the sandbox: the forbidden-key check only applies on the safe path, unknown names fall through the scope proxy to real globals, and any safe-path error, not just a parse error, re-ran the expression as JavaScript. The right fix was to grow the safe language to what models actually write, or reject and feed the error back. I wouldn't ship it that way.

    Actions are ordered steps: run, set, condition, validate, api, log and alert, each with an optional when, plus onSuccess and onError branches. When validate fails it collects field errors and throws; the flow stops, records the error, and runs onError if there is one. Steps before it are not rolled back, so validate goes first, or I'd snapshot state at the start of an action and restore it on failure. The api step was a bare fetch to whatever URL the schema resolved, with no allowlist. To make it safe: no direct fetch from schema, every call through our server as a proxy with a per-app host allowlist, auth added server-side, never a secret in the schema since it's model output, and rate limits.

    Styling started as Tailwind class names plus a mapper to React Native styles, about 377 lines when I deleted it. I switched the model's contract to plain React Native style objects, because the model writes those directly and the mapper was a lossy layer that re-resolved classes against screen width in every component. The alias normaliser expands compact keys, like s, d, a and u for the four sections, back to full names on load; in the spike's report, an example schema measured about 3.0k tokens minified against about 5.7k for the original.

    Why it didn't ship: it never reached master, and in August the team moved to OpenUI Lang. My read is that a whole JSON document has to finish before it can render, while in voice-first Omni the screen has to paint while the model speaks, and Lang streams line by line. What would have made it ship: a streaming format, no Function fallback, a real test suite, and versioning. There's no schema version field today. I'd add one at the root, run code-owned upgrade steps from version to version on load so last month's saved UIs still render, keep component changes additive, and keep a set of saved schemas as a regression suite.

    Testing the evaluator: table tests of expression, scope and expected value; hostile inputs like constructor.constructor, __proto__ through brackets and built-up strings, very deep nesting and very long inputs; grammar-based fuzzing with properties like "never throws anything but a typed error, never reaches a global"; and a differential test against real JavaScript for the safe subset. The engine, the components and the prompt were mine; my teammate Sahad moved the renderer to TypeScript and worked on the viewer.

1.14 Other projects and take-homes

  • Kilnic: bitmask RBAC with tenant caps, four gRPC services each with its own Postgres, refresh-token rotation, JWKS verification
    Answer

    Kilnic is a multi-tenant clinic platform backend I built with a friend, Rohit Raj, in April and May 2026. I wrote 21 of the 24 commits and about 6,600 of roughly 8,100 code lines on the branch. Only my RBAC models PR is merged to main; the rest sits on a feature branch, unmerged and never deployed, so I describe it as built, not shipped. Multi-tenancy means many clinics share one system and never see each other's data. A tenant is a Tenant row (a clinic) with a service_mask of the services it subscribes to. Roles are scoped per tenant, and every clinical row carries a tenant_id.

    gRPC is RPC over HTTP/2 with Protocol Buffers: typed stubs from .proto files, binary encoding, streaming. REST is resource URLs with JSON, easier to debug and call from a browser. Kilnic is my gRPC project. HS256 signs with one shared secret, so every verifier could also mint tokens; RS256 signs with a private RSA key, so only identity issues tokens and everyone else verifies with the public key from a JWKS endpoint, /.well-known/jwks.json, a JSON list of public keys keyed by kid.

    The RBAC is bitmasks. Every permission and feature is defined in the database with a bit value that must be a positive power of two. A role holds a service mask and a permission mask per resource, and a check is one bitwise AND. It's compact and fast, and capping is trivial. Rohit's first mock applied user overrides with XOR, and I changed that to OR. XOR toggles, so an override that "grants" a bit the role already has silently removes it. OR can only add. The tenant cap: effective permissions are (base | override) & cap. On write I check every key is a known, active service or resource, masks use only defined bits, and a role or override is a subset of the tenant's subscription. Role changes go to an audit log with before and after values.

    I split the Django monolith into four services in five planned phases: identity, medicines, prescriptions and vitals, each with its own Postgres. To be straight about how: the split, the managers-refactor spec and the async fan-out were pair-built with Claude Code, spec then plan then execute, and those commits carry its co-author line; I can defend every choice in it. The cost is real. There are no cross-database foreign keys, so references are plain ids validated over gRPC at write time. Medicines has one call for active medicines (for writes) and one that includes deactivated ones, so old prescriptions still render. Client wrappers have a 5 second timeout and map NOT_FOUND to a validation error. Was it right for two people? I'd take it back. A modular monolith with clear app boundaries would have shipped faster. The split was partly learning and partly a bet on Rohit's much bigger planned design (Go, FastAPI, NestJS, Kafka), none of which was built.

    Access tokens are RS256 JWTs that last 900 seconds. Refresh tokens are opaque random secrets that last 30 days, and I store only their SHA-256, so a database leak gives nobody a usable token. They're long and random, so a fast hash is fine, unlike passwords. Rotation runs under select_for_update on the session row. Presenting an already-rotated token is treated as theft and revokes every live session of that user, and the revocation commits before the error is raised. Other services verify tokens locally against JWKS, cached for an hour and refetched when an unknown kid appears, falling back to the stale cache if the fetch fails. They still call CheckSession over gRPC, because otherwise a revoked session keeps working for up to 15 minutes. The trade-off is one identity call per request, the coupling JWTs were meant to avoid; a short revocation cache softens it.

    Medicine search embeds the query with embed_anything, a Rust BERT model loaded lazily as a singleton and run with asyncio.to_thread. It searches Qdrant filtered by tenant_id, drops hits under 0.85, and hydrates the rest from Postgres, skipping inactive rows. Sync to Qdrant runs on transaction.on_commit in a 2-worker thread pool with no retry. A crash after commit or a Qdrant outage loses the update for good. The fix is an outbox row written in the same transaction, a worker that retries with idempotent upserts keyed by medicine id, and a periodic reconcile.

    On the async side, prescriptions and vitals run the three independent identity lookups (tenant, doctor and patient) with asyncio.gather. That should turn about three round trips into about one, but that's inferred, not measured. If one fails, plain gather raises the first error while the others keep running. A TaskGroup cancels the siblings, and gRPC codes map to domain errors. check_permission reads bits from the database on every call; there's even a TODO for a cache, and it ignores its service argument. I'd cache the effective mask per user keyed by a role version, bump that version on any role, override or tenant-cap change, with a short TTL as a safety net.

    The worst gap is the RBAC update view: it had permission_classes = [], so any signed-in user could change role assignments. That's privilege escalation. A test where a non-admin calls the update and expects 403 would have caught it, and so would a test that walks every view and fails on empty permission classes. Honestly, the final branch has no tests: my auth commit deleted the earlier pytest suites, so I don't claim Kilnic as tested.

  • StatusPulse: multi-tenant status pages, HttpOnly JWT cookie, invites, org scoping, live updates over a websockets bridge
    Answer

    StatusPulse was my Plivo take-home: a multi-tenant status page and incident tool. A company's team manages its services and incidents, and its customers see a public status page. I built it in three days, June 28 to 30, 2025, with Django REST Framework and React, deployed on Render. It's the project of mine with a public status page. A service has four levels: operational, degraded performance, partial outage, major outage. An incident goes investigating, identified, monitoring, resolved, with a severity from low to critical and a resolved timestamp.

    The JWT sits in an HttpOnly cookie because JavaScript can't read it, so an XSS bug can't steal the token, while anything in localStorage is readable by every script on the page. The SPA and the API were on different Render subdomains, which count as different sites. The cookie therefore needed SameSite=None; Secure to be sent on credentialed cross-site calls. That opens CSRF: any site can make the browser send it. The fixes are a CSRF token (double submit) or putting both under one domain and using SameSite=Lax. The token lasts 24 hours with no refresh token. That's simple, but a stolen cookie stays good for a day, I can't revoke it, and users get logged out daily.

    Invites: an admin creates an invite bound to the invitee's email, with a UUID token, single use and valid for one day. The link goes to the signup page, which pre-fills the organisation and email. After joining, the admin grants or revokes access per member. Org scoping: every user belongs to one organisation with a role (admin, team or user). Every read is filtered by the caller's organisation, and every write checks the object belongs to it, with custom DRF permission classes like IsOrganizationAdmin. One gap: incident update and delete only require login, not the access flag, though the org check still applies. The public page uses unauthenticated endpoints. Anonymous visitors see only services marked publicly_visible; signed-in members see everything.

    The timeline is one table that points at either a service or an incident through a GenericForeignKey (content_type plus object_id). It stores who did it and old and new values for status changes. I get one events table and one query for a mixed history. I lose database-level integrity: there's no real foreign key, nothing cascades, deleting a service can leave dangling rows, and joins are awkward.

    Live updates: a post_save or post_delete signal on Service or Incident builds an event and sends it over a raw TCP socket to port 9000. A standalone asyncio websockets server listens there and broadcasts to rooms like the organisation room or a service's incident room. The React client keeps one WebSocket manager, joins rooms per page, and on any event re-runs the Redux thunks that refetch data; it doesn't trust the payload. Why not Django Channels? It was in the requirements file but unused. A separate small server was quick to reason about next to plain Django. Channels would have been the more standard choice, and it would have avoided my deploy problem.

    That problem: the socket server listened on localhost, and Render exposes one public port, so in production the browser's socket most likely never connected. What kept the UI current was the fallback I added on the last day: after create, update or delete, the client refetches over HTTP when the socket is down. That covers the person making the change, not other viewers. The signal also runs inside the request. The bridge call has a 2 second timeout and catches its errors, so a dead bridge doesn't fail the request, but it can add up to 2 seconds, and the event fires even if the transaction later rolls back. I'd send it on commit, best-effort, through a queue.

    The organisations list was public and showed user counts. Yes, that's a leak: anyone can enumerate a vendor's customers and their team sizes. I'd list only organisations that opted into a public page, drop the counts, and address pages by slug. Automatic status from uptime checks: each service gets a check config (URL, interval, timeout, expected status). A scheduled worker runs the checks and stores results. Status changes only after several consecutive failures or successes, so it doesn't flap. High latency maps to degraded and failures to outages, and the change goes through the same write path, so the timeline and live events fire and an incident can open automatically. Ideally the checks run from more than one region.

    What I cut for three days: SQLite instead of Postgres, a hard-coded secret key and a committed database file, no tests, no refresh tokens or CSRF tokens, Django's dev server on Render, and a socket deploy I never verified end to end. Day one was auth, signup, invites and Redux. Day two was services, incidents, the timeline, and swapping my custom middleware for a proper DRF authentication class. Day three was WebSockets, a run of Render fixes for CORS, cookies and URLs, and the HTTP fallback.

  • AI take-homes: PdfAnalyser page-cited RAG, browser_ai plan-then-execute agent, the AI stack builder node graph
    Answer

    These were three job-hunt take-homes from July 2025, each built in a day or two. PdfAnalyser is chat with a PDF, where every answer jumps to the page it came from. browser_ai is an LLM browser agent: a planner writes steps and an executor turns each step into Playwright actions. The AI stack builder is a drag-and-drop canvas for wiring query, knowledge base, LLM and output nodes, with a FastAPI and Postgres config backend.

    PdfAnalyser: the upload goes to Express, then to LlamaParse. That's LlamaIndex's cloud parser, which turns complex PDFs (tables, columns, odd layouts) into per-page markdown, where a plain text extractor scrambles columns and loses table structure. Parsing is a job, so the client polled a status endpoint on an interval. When it said success, the server fetched the per-page markdown. A webhook or an SSE push would be better than polling. I chunk each page separately, 500 characters with 30 of overlap, keeping the page number in metadata. Overlap stops a sentence from being cut in half at a boundary, and chunking per page means a chunk never spans pages, so the citation is exact. Embeddings were MiniLM through HuggingFace Inference. The top 10 matches go into the prompt labelled "Page N", and Gemini 2.5 Flash must return strict JSON {text, page}. The UI shows a page chip, and clicking it scrolls the embedded PDF viewer to that page.

    The store was LangChain's in-memory vector store, replaced on every upload. That's fine for a single-user demo with one document. It's wrong for production: it's lost on restart, there's no multi-user or multi-document isolation, it's bounded by RAM, and it can't be shared between server instances. The file was even named after Chroma, which was never used.

    browser_ai: one call asks the model for a JSON plan with a goal, steps, expected outcomes and error handling. For each step I take a compact DOM snapshot and ask an execution prompt for concrete actions (click, fill, navigate, wait, press, scroll), run them with 3 second pacing, scroll and retry on failure, and stop when the model reports all steps done. To shrink the page I keep only elements that are visible in the viewport and a short attribute whitelist (id, aria-label, role, name, placeholder, title, alt, type), and I drop scripts and styles. The model backend is chosen by an environment variable that maps to a handler with one interface: Gemini 2.5 Flash by default, local Ollama models, or a llamafile server.

    It ran in the user's own Chrome profile through a persistent context. That way it's already logged in everywhere, which is the point, and also the risk: the agent can act as the user, so a misread step or a malicious page can send, buy or delete for real. The defence against injection is to treat page text as data, fenced and labelled as untrusted. Plan from the user's goal, not from page text. Allowlist domains and actions, and require confirmation for anything risky like submit, pay or send. At AiRA our browser agent works that way: risky steps need the user's approval. To measure task completion: a fixed task suite with checkable end states (the order exists, the field holds the value), then success rate, steps, tokens or cost, time and human interventions, repeated several times because runs are not deterministic.

    The stack builder was built overnight, from about 6 pm to 2 am. I put the night into what a reviewer clicks first: the canvas with four node types, stack creation and saved config. I faked the chat reply with a timeout instead of calling a model, and I should have said so plainly in the README. To actually run the graph: validate it's acyclic, sort it topologically, give each node type an executor (query passes text, knowledge base retrieves, LLM gets query plus context, output streams), pass outputs along edges, and surface errors per node.

    Before pushing a take-home I now check .gitignore first, then that there's no node_modules, then a secret scan, then a README that says what's real and what's stubbed. I learned that the hard way: browser_ai went up with node_modules and a .env holding an API key. The right fix is to rotate the key and purge it from history before I link those repos anywhere.

  • ML prototypes: GRU behaviour classifier exported to ONNX, live speaker ID with Resemblyzer embeddings and thresholds
    Answer

    Both are small personal ML prototypes from 2026: a behaviour classifier that guesses what a user is doing from UI events, and a live speaker-ID loop. I present them as pipelines I can explain end to end, not as models with proven accuracy. The behaviour tracker predicts one of 9 behaviours: skimming, hunting, normal, ignore, frustrated, engaged, confused, comparing and deep reading. A GRU is a recurrent cell with an update gate and a reset gate and a single hidden state. An LSTM adds a separate cell state and three gates. A GRU has fewer parameters and trains faster, and on short sequences it usually does about as well. Mine is one layer with hidden size 48.

    Input: each raw event (scroll, click, touch, selection, idle, error, navigation) becomes a vector with a one-hot event type, coordinates normalised by screen size, and the time gap since the last event. Training uses windows of 50 events with stride 8, keeping sessions with at least 8 events. Windowing gives fixed-length sequences and many examples per session. A small stride gives more samples, but neighbouring windows share 42 events, so they're highly correlated, and the split must be by session or test windows leak into training. Streaming inference scores one event at a time and carries the GRU hidden state per session between events, resetting it after a long idle gap. Training on 50-event windows while serving on an unbounded stream is a mismatch I'd test.

    Most of the training data is synthetic, from a generator script. The model mostly learns the generator's rules, so any accuracy on that data says little about real users. I also built a React collector app that records real labelled sessions and writes JSONL into the repo through the File System Access API, but only 720 real events are committed. ONNX is an open model format, so ONNX Runtime can run the model without PyTorch on a server, in a browser or on a phone. I exported through a wrapper that makes the hidden state an explicit input and output, and a parity test runs the same inputs through PyTorch and ONNX Runtime and compares within a tolerance. For browser or phone I'd use ONNX Runtime Web or Mobile and keep the hidden state per session in app code. At hidden size 48 it's tiny. No evaluation results are committed, so I say exactly that: the pipeline, the export and the parity test are done, and the missing piece is real labelled sessions and a held-out evaluation.

    A speaker embedding is a fixed-length vector that captures how a voice sounds, so the same speaker lands close in cosine similarity. resemblizer, my live speaker-ID prototype, captures mic audio at 16 kHz into a queue. The main loop keeps a rolling buffer capped at 3 seconds and drops older audio with a warning if it falls behind. It embeds each 1-second slice with Resemblyzer (256 dimensions) and compares it to every stored centroid. A best score of 0.95 or more merges into that centroid. Between 0.75 and 0.95 starts a new cluster for that same speaker. Below 0.75 registers a new speaker. Each speaker keeps up to 8 running-mean centroids, so loud, quiet and far-from-the-mic versions of one voice each get their own centre instead of being averaged into mush.

    I also benchmarked four encoders behind one interface: Resemblyzer plus SpeechBrain ECAPA-TDNN, x-vector and ResNet. I ran resemblizer as a spec, plan, implement loop with Claude Code, and the benchmark commits carry its co-author line. Each needed its own threshold (0.75, 0.30, 0.65 and 0.40) because each has its own embedding space and score distribution, so a cosine of 0.7 means different things in each. The benchmark writes a report per encoder with latency per chunk and a speaker timeline, and the store and encoders have unit tests. In the same weeks of May 2026 I built voice-print speaker identification inside AiRA on the same idea: Resemblyzer embeddings and Qdrant cosine search at a 0.80 threshold. I deleted it in July to drop torch, CUDA and the Redis memory it used.

    Evaluating it properly means labelled recordings and a diarisation error rate, a threshold sweep to choose the operating point, confusion tests on similar voices (same gender and accent), and latency. With two people talking at once, a 1-second window holds both voices, so the embedding is a blend and may match neither: you get a wrong label or a fake new speaker. I'd add overlap detection, mark those windows low-confidence, and require several consistent windows before creating a new speaker.

  • Agent-built apps and browser media: fitness tracker merge, reel_share game runtime, MotionEase worker, Fieldom Stream overlay
    Answer

    These four are small apps where the interesting part is one design decision each. In two of them, the fitness tracker and reel_share, I wrote the specs and drove an AI coding agent through them, and I say so up front. The fitness tracker is an Expo React Native app from May 2026 for spends, nutrition, water, weight, loans and a cycle tracker. All data lives on the device as one typed JSON document in AsyncStorage. Old data keeps loading after model changes because the loader merges stored data with the seed, filling any missing section, and runs on-load migrations. Old free-text meals become the newer labelled shape with items, for example. A hydrated flag stops the seed from overwriting storage before the load finishes.

    Backup is a versioned JSON envelope, and import refuses files from a newer schema and shows counts before applying. The merge is idempotent, additive and local-wins. Records match by a stable key (bank plus account, card name, loan id). Transactions and meals have no id, so they match by a content hash over time, label and sorted items. Importing twice adds nothing, and on conflict the local copy wins. The trade-off is written in the spec: there are no tombstones, so deletions don't travel. Cycle prediction is pure functions. Flow days become contiguous periods, periods become cycles between start dates, and the average uses the last 6 cycles. It shows an estimate-only state until 2 cycles exist.

    reel_share, from August 2026, is a platform for playable posts: short interactive games that sit in a vertical feed like a reel, with 17 experiences across DOM, Canvas 2D and Babylon.js 3D, each loaded lazily. The runtime is an explicit state machine: created, initializing, ready, running and paused, ended, destroying, destroyed, plus error, with an allowed-transitions table. The host can pause at any moment and the tab can hide, so illegal transitions should throw instead of leaving half-states. Teardown happens exactly once, and pause reasons are a set, so a visibility pause and a user pause don't cancel each other. To talk to a Flutter WebView, every event is a versioned envelope (protocol version, game id, session id, timestamp, type, payload) sent as a CustomEvent and also to GameHost.postMessage when a native host exists. The host controls the game through pause, resume, restart and mute.

    The bot arena is an open 3D space, so it gets a Yuka state machine (patrol, search, chase, attack, retreat) and Recast navmesh routing, and Recast loads only with that arena. Endless Dodger is a few lanes, so a small controller that scores lanes by time to collision does the job without the extra bundle. A seeded random generator, ?test=1&seed=42, makes every run replayable, which keeps Vitest and Playwright tests deterministic. There are 13 unit suites and 5 end-to-end specs behind one check command. About 17,700 lines arrived in one commit, built by an agent against three long implementation plans. I own it by saying that plainly, defending the architecture choices above, and walking through or changing any file live.

    MotionEase, from December 2024, is an Electron overlay for motion sickness, similar in idea to Apple's Vehicle Motion Cues: dots on screen move opposite to your head movement, so your eyes get a cue that matches the motion you feel. I never tested whether it actually reduces sickness. TensorFlow.js MoveNet pose detection runs in a Web Worker so the UI thread stays free. Frames come from ImageCapture.grabFrame() as an ImageBitmap, transferred to the worker zero-copy. The window is transparent, always on top and click-through with setIgnoreMouseEvents. Hovering the reset button turns mouse events back on through a whitelisted IPC bridge, and leaving turns them off. Context isolation is on and Node integration off.

    Fieldom Stream was a four-day sprint with Rohit in April 2025. Headless Puppeteer opens a CricClubs scorecard and installs a MutationObserver, and every change pushes the page HTML over Socket.IO to the streamer's browser. There it's parsed into teams, score, overs, batsmen and bowler, and drawn onto the webcam canvas, which captureStream turns into a video stream. Rohit's side piped video to YouTube over ffmpeg and RTMP. To be precise, in the final code my overlay path recorded locally, and the chunks that reached YouTube came from the plain webcam path. html2canvas on every frame rasterises the whole DOM each time and caused heavy re-renders. My first fix was merged and then reverted; the lasting one drew the scoreboard directly with Canvas 2D. Sending the whole HTML on every mutation is wasteful. I'd extract only the needed fields in the page, diff against the last values, debounce, and send just the changes.

  • Web3 with Foundry: Chainlink VRF raffle, over-collateralised stablecoin and health factor, reentrancy, on-chain NFTs
    Answer

    These were course projects. I did Cyfrin Updraft's Foundry track between November 2024 and March 2025, and I present them that way: none is an original protocol. A smart contract is code deployed on a blockchain whose state changes through transactions. Gas is the fee for computation and storage, which makes every loop and write cost money. Foundry is the Solidity toolkit: forge builds, tests and runs deploy scripts, cast calls contracts and the chain from the command line, and anvil is a local chain. ERC-20 is fungible tokens, just balances. ERC-721 is unique tokens, each with an owner and its own metadata URI.

    A contract can't make its own random number because execution must be deterministic: every node re-runs it and gets the same result. Anything on-chain like a timestamp or block hash is predictable or can be nudged by validators. Chainlink VRF solves it. The raffle requests randomness, an oracle generates random words with a cryptographic proof that's checked on-chain, and the coordinator calls fulfillRandomWords, where I pick the winner as the first random word modulo the player count, reset the raffle and pay out. Mine uses VRF v2.5, and a script creates and funds the subscription and adds the consumer programmatically. Chainlink Automation calls checkUpkeep off-chain. It returns true when the interval has passed, the raffle is open and it has a balance and players, and then performUpkeep runs on-chain and requests the randomness. There's an OPEN and a CALCULATING state so nobody can enter while the draw is pending and change the player list under the random number.

    I tested it with about 12 Foundry unit tests: entry-fee reverts, player recording, events, entry blocked while calculating, checkUpkeep true and false cases, the request id read from logs, and a full payout test that's skipped on forks. The fuzz test feeds random request ids to the mock coordinator's fulfillRandomWords and expects a revert, because no request exists yet.

    The stablecoin is over-collateralised: you deposit wETH or wBTC worth more than the stablecoin you mint. With a liquidation threshold of 50, you need 200 percent collateral. The health factor is collateral value times the threshold divided by debt, and below 1 the position can be liquidated. My liquidate() is a stub with its body commented out, and I say so. It should check the user's health factor is below 1, let the liquidator burn some of the user's debt, give the liquidator that value in collateral plus a 10 percent bonus, and require that the user's health factor improved and the liquidator's stays healthy.

    Reentrancy is when a contract makes an external call before updating its own state, and the callee calls back in and repeats the action, like withdrawing twice. The defences are checks-effects-interactions (update balances before sending) and a reentrancy guard. Honestly my engine has neither a guard nor an oracle staleness check; it reads latestRoundData and uses the price. With a stale feed, collateral is valued wrong: healthy users get liquidated, or bad debt gets minted. I'd check updatedAt against the feed's heartbeat and the price is positive, and pause when it's stale. The NFT builds its whole metadata on-chain and points the image at a Base64 SVG data URI, so it doesn't depend on someone keeping an IPFS file pinned. The cost is gas.

    I also wrote a tiered crowdfunding contract with a factory, following thirdweb's tutorial, with active, successful and failed states and a backer refund on failure. Done safely: pull payments, where each backer withdraws their own refund instead of the contract looping over everyone. Zero their balance before sending, check the deadline and state on every path, and never let a failing transfer block others. What carried over to backend work: thinking in state machines and invariants, assuming every external call can fail or call back, idempotent state changes, and fuzz-style tests for the paths you'd never think to write by hand.

  • Trading backtests (lookahead, survivorship, costs, placebo, out-of-sample) and the TradingView MCP server
    Answer

    The question was whether retail indicator strategies make money on NSE after real costs, and whether any beat simply holding the index. The core runs were at the end of October 2026, with sub-studies into early November, as private research with Claude Code as my pair: I set the questions, the bias checks and the pass rule, and the agent wrote much of the code, as the commits' co-author line shows. Lookahead bias is using information you wouldn't have had yet, like filling at the close that made the signal. Survivorship bias, in one line: testing only survivors flatters the past. Overfitting is tuning to noise; out-of-sample testing judges settings on data they weren't picked on, and walk-forward repeats that on a rolling window.

    To avoid lookahead, signals use a bar's close and fill at the next bar's open. A gap through the stop exits at the open, and if stop and target hit in the same bar I assume the stop. Higher-timeframe filters use only the last fully closed bar, the daily trend filter uses yesterday's close, and GARCH and HMM models are refit each January on data up to the previous year-end. Tests check no-lookahead for the fill rule, GARCH, HMM and the higher-timeframe side, ATR, EMA and ADX match hand-computed values, and trades were re-checked against raw candles, for example 400 random trades in one sub-study with zero mismatches. The suite has 34 pytest tests, and testing found real bugs, including weekly Sharpe being 2.2 times too high because weekly returns were annualised as if daily.

    Survivorship is my biggest caveat. The universe was today's Nifty 200, because I didn't have historical membership. The momentum backtest made 35 percent a year since October 2020, while the real Nifty 200 Momentum 30 index made 14.6 percent, so the absolute numbers are inflated. Costs are real broker charges plus slippage: with FYERS on 10 lakh rupees, a round trip was about ₹402 intraday versus about ₹2,287 for delivery, which is why intraday signals held as delivery were the worst of both worlds. Slippage was 0.02 percent per side, re-run at 0 and 0.03, and the "best" short-timeframe settings died there: in my SuperTrend study, a 15-minute setting went from about +₹13,000 to about −₹1.25 lakh just by adding 0.02 percent. An edge thinner than costs isn't an edge.

    The placebo keeps exits, sizing and trade count the same but picks entries at random, over 20 seeds, so a strategy has to beat luck, not zero. Weekly pullback made 18.5 percent out-of-sample against a random median of 10.6 and beat all 20 runs. Momentum, compared against random portfolios, made 27.1 against 14.0. The daily breakout beat only 75 percent. Daily and weekly split 2015–2021 in-sample, 2022 to October 2026 out-of-sample; hourly 2023–24 versus 2025–26; 15-minute and 5-minute data were too short to split. The pass rule: positive out-of-sample expectancy after costs over at least 100 trades, Sharpe above Nifty or positive alpha, and drawdown no worse than 1.5 times Nifty's. Out-of-sample, the 200-day market filter was the one equation that helped: plus 0.26 Sharpe and about 8 points less drawdown on average across 16 cases. Volatility targeting, GARCH and the HMM regime filter averaged slightly negative. True walk-forward exists only as those yearly refits.

    I tested hundreds of variants, 224 runs in one break-even analysis alone. My guards: parameters picked in-sample and judged out-of-sample, the placebo, few parameters, robustness across neighbouring settings (all 8 weekly-pullback settings were profitable out-of-sample), and naming the families to test before seeing results. I didn't apply a deflated Sharpe or any formal multiple-testing correction; that's next. On 3.5 months of 5-minute bars I trust almost nothing: the opening range breakout was profitable in 17 of 20 settings, but that's about 170 trades in one market phase, and the 15-minute version lost money in all 43 settings on Nifty 200. It's a lead, not a result.

    The TradingView MCP server isn't mine: it's an open-source, MIT-licensed project, tradesdontlie/tradingview-mcp. MCP, the Model Context Protocol, lets a server expose tools (actions), resources (readable data) and prompts to a model over a transport like stdio or HTTP. This one drives the TradingView desktop app through the Chrome DevTools Protocol and exposes 84 tools for charts, indicators, Pine Script and replay. Around it, I registered it in Claude Code as a stdio server pointed at TradingView's debug port 9223 and fixed its path after a folder move. With the agent I also wrote an exporter that pulls candles to CSV on its own debug port, because my browser held the default 9222, plus Pine strategies and Python scripts that re-cost TradingView's results with real charges and slippage. It relies on undocumented app internals, so an update can silently break it: pin versions, health-check each session, validate exported data.

    Could the agent place a real trade? It exposes no order tools; its only "trade" is inside TradingView's replay simulator, and its README lists real trading as out of scope. I'd still make that structural: no broker keys on that machine or anywhere the agent can reach, and any future order path behind human confirmation. My 30-second verdict: most retail indicator strategies lose after costs, and intraday showed no proven edge. What survived out-of-sample was monthly momentum rotation, about 27 percent a year in both periods with Sharpe around 1.15 but inflated by survivorship, and a weekly pullback at 18.5 to 26 percent with Sharpe 1.12 to 1.28. Taxes aren't modelled, and my own guess, not a backtest number, is that real returns sit much nearer the real momentum index's 14.6 percent.

  • learning-os and the portfolio: one subagent per job, checker agents, Swift PDF/OCR tool, git submodules
    Answer

    learning-os is my personal learning workspace: books go in, study pages come out, and the projects I build to practise sit beside them. It's one private Git repo that holds the instructions, the agent definitions and the tools, with the content in submodules. I used submodules because visibility differs. The books are copyrighted PDFs, so that repo is private. My notes are public. Each project is its own repo, the portfolio public and the trading work private. I built all of it, the portfolio included, with Claude Code as my pair, and nearly every commit carries its co-author line; my part is the design, the rules and the checking.

    A Claude Code subagent is a separately defined agent with its own instructions, tools and fresh context that the main session hands a task to and gets a report back from. My pipeline from a PDF chapter to a study page has eleven steps. Pick the chapter and its page range, then crop the book's figures to files with a manifest. A page-builder agent writes the page, then does a completeness pass against the PDF and an enrich pass. A scene-writer agent writes picture prompts and never touches HTML. Pictures come from Gemini through a relay or from an SVG-illustrator agent that renders its own SVG and looks at it. A script inserts the pictures, another adds the light/dark toggle, the index is rebuilt, and a page-checker agent checks the result. Then I commit inside Notes and bump the submodule, and only when I ask for it.

    One agent per job instead of one big agent because each gets a narrow context and a clear contract, and the one that checks isn't the one that wrote. That's where most of the quality comes from. The checker script verifies that every book figure is used exactly once, every image exists, there are no external resources, and every inline script parses with node --check. It exits non-zero on any problem. The checker agent adds browser checks: no console errors, the theme toggle works, contrast is readable in both themes, and no horizontal scroll at 375 pixels. The insert script must be idempotent: running it twice changes nothing.

    The PDF tool is about 120 lines of Swift on PDFKit and Apple's Vision framework. It does info, outline, text, find, render and OCR, and the OCR runs offline at about 0.35 seconds a page. I wrote it because this Mac had no poppler, the agents' file reader couldn't render PDFs, and there was no network to install a Python PDF library. Gemini's page won't fetch localhost, but it will navigate to it, and a URL hash survives both ways. So my relay redirects to Gemini with the job in the hash. A page script sends the prompt and waits for a new image, then navigates back to a local save URL with the image as base64 in the hash. I also added a duplicate guard using a small average hash, after a reopened chat saved an old image under the next name.

    Pages are written plate by plate: each section is written to disk as it's finished, behind an insertion marker, so a run that gets cut off leaves a resumable partial, and every step can be re-run. Long agent runs do get cut off, so that matters more than it sounds. For the relay hitting Gemini's daily limit, about 20 images a day on that account, the written rule is stop, report and resume after the reset. Never switch accounts or go incognito, because that's evading a limit I agreed to, and the work cloud credentials never go into it.

    The portfolio is vanilla TypeScript and Vite; the only dev dependencies are TypeScript and Vite. It's a handful of sections and small animations, so a framework would be weight with nothing to manage. The demo films are plain markup: an element with a duration and child elements with data attributes for when they appear, type, tap or change class, while CSS variables drive bars and timers. An IntersectionObserver plays a film when it's on screen and pauses it off screen, only one plays at a time, and with reduced motion a film rests on its last frame. My rule for employer work on a public site: describe product behaviour only, the kind AiRA already shows publicly. No internal architecture, vendors, code names, security design or unreleased work. I also ran a pass that removed claims an audit couldn't back up.

    With submodules, if you push the workspace repo before the children, it points at commits the remote doesn't have, and a fresh clone breaks. So the rule is to push children first and the workspace last. What it taught me for production agents: narrow tools and narrow roles, a checker that isn't the writer, idempotent and resumable steps, honest stops when a resource runs out, and a human approving anything that commits or publishes.

2. Data structures and algorithms (P0) →

AiRA: Mobile Google Sign-In (native flow)

In one line: the phone asks Google "who is this?", Google gives back a signed ID token, the phone sends it to our API, and our API checks it itself, finds or creates the user, and returns our own session token.

Four parties take part:

   📱 AiRA app          🔵 Google SDK          🖥️  AiRA API          🗄️  MongoDB
  (React Native)       (on the phone)          (FastAPI)            (users)

The full flow

 ┌────────────────────────────── PHASE 1 · ON THE PHONE ──────────────────────────────┐
 │                                                                                     │
 │  User taps "Continue with Google"                                                   │
 │        │   sign-in-screen.tsx → google.mutate()                                     │
 │        ▼                                                                            │
 │  useSignInWithGoogle  →  requestGoogleIdToken()                                     │
 │        │                                                                            │
 │        │  already signing in?  ── yes ──▶ ❌ error "already in progress"            │
 │        │  no                                                                        │
 │        ▼                                                                            │
 │  GoogleSignin.configure({ webClientId, iosClientId, offlineAccess: false })         │
 │        │        (offlineAccess:false = ask for IDENTITY only, no Gmail/Drive access)│
 │        ▼                                                                            │
 │  Signed in on this phone before?  (GoogleSignin.hasPreviousSignIn)                  │
 │        │                                                                            │
 │   YES ─┤                                              ├─ NO                          │
 │        ▼                                              │                              │
 │  Show "Continue as Dhanesh?"                          │                              │
 │  (iOS: Alert · Android: bottom sheet)                 │                              │
 │     │            │                │                   │                              │
 │  Continue     Different         Cancel                │                              │
 │     │          account            │                   │                              │
 │     ▼            │                ▼                   │                              │
 │  signInSilently()│          return "canceled"         │                              │
 │  (no Google UI)  │                                    │                              │
 │     │ same user? │                                    │                              │
 │     │ yes        ▼                                    ▼                              │
 │     │       signOut() + clear saved account ──▶ hasPlayServices() → signIn()         │
 │     │                                            (Google's account picker)           │
 │     │                                                 │ user cancels → "canceled"    │
 │     ▼                                                 ▼                              │
 │  ◀──────────────────── 🔵 Google returns  idToken  (a signed JWT) ───────────────────│
 │        │                                                                            │
 │        ▼                                                                            │
 │  Save { id, firstName, email } for next time  (googleAccountStorage)                │
 └────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          │
          │   POST /v1/auth/google
          │   body: { "id_token": "<Google JWT>", "tz": "Asia/Kolkata" }
          │   (checked with Zod on the phone; GoogleLogin model on the server: extra="forbid")
          ▼
 ┌────────────────────────────── PHASE 2 · VERIFY (API) ──────────────────────────────┐
 │                                                                                     │
 │  auth_c_v1.google_native_signin → auth_s_v1.google_native_signin                    │
 │        │                                                                            │
 │        ▼                                                                            │
 │  verify_native_id_token()   (google_adp.py)                                         │
 │     1. read "kid" from the token header                                             │
 │     2. fetch Google's public key for that kid (JWKS)  ─────▶ Google certs           │
 │            can't reach Google? ──▶ ❌ 503 "temporarily unavailable"                 │
 │     3. check the RS256 signature                                                    │
 │     4. aud == our web client id    (made for OUR app?)                              │
 │     5. iss == accounts.google.com  (made by Google?)                                │
 │     6. aud, exp, iat, iss, sub all present; not expired                             │
 │     7. email_verified == true      (StrictBool, no "true" string accepted)          │
 │            any check fails ──▶ ❌ 401 "could not be verified"                        │
 │        │                                                                            │
 │        ▼  identity = { subject (google_sub), email, first/last name, hosted_domain }│
 │                                                                                     │
 │  email_token     = HMAC-SHA256(secret, lowercase email)   → to SEARCH               │
 │  encrypted_email = AES-256-GCM(email)                     → to STORE                │
 └────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          ▼
 ┌──────────────────────── PHASE 3 · FIND, LINK OR CREATE (API + DB) ─────────────────┐
 │                                                                                     │
 │  user_ent.resolve_google()     (loops at most 2 times)                              │
 │        │                                                                            │
 │        ▼                                                                            │
 │  ① find user WHERE google_sub = identity.subject ──── found ──┐                    │
 │        │ not found                                             ▼                    │
 │        │                                          inactive / other provider?        │
 │        │                                            yes → ❌ 401   no → ✅ LOG IN   │
 │        ▼                                                                            │
 │  ② find user WHERE email_token = email_token ─────── found ──┐                     │
 │        │ not found                                            ▼                     │
 │        │                                 inactive / other provider / different sub? │
 │        │                                   yes → ❌ 401                              │
 │        │                                   same sub → ✅ LOG IN                      │
 │        │                                   no sub yet:                               │
 │        │                                     email not @gmail.com AND no Workspace   │
 │        │                                     domain? → ❌ 409 "legacy sign-in needed"│
 │        │                                     else → ATOMIC LINK:                     │
 │        │                                       find_one_and_update                   │
 │        │                                       WHERE id=… AND google_sub=null        │
 │        │                                       SET google_sub = subject              │
 │        │                                       → ✅ LOG IN                            │
 │        │                                       (someone else won the race → loop)    │
 │        ▼                                                                            │
 │  ③ CREATE new user { email_token, encrypted_email, names, google_sub }              │
 │       insert_once → ✅ NEW USER   (duplicate key from a parallel request → loop)     │
 │                                                                                     │
 │  new user? → create the scratchpad (name, timezone) as a BACKGROUND task            │
 └────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          ▼
 ┌──────────────────────────── PHASE 4 · ISSUE THE SESSION (API) ─────────────────────┐
 │  session token = JWT { sub: user_id, iat: now }   signed HS256 with our secret      │
 │                                                                                     │
 │  200 OK  { "access_token": "<AiRA JWT>",                                            │
 │            "token_type":   "bearer",                                                │
 │            "expires_in":   604800 }          ← 168 hours = 7 days                   │
 └────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          ▼
 ┌────────────────────────────── PHASE 5 · BACK ON THE PHONE ─────────────────────────┐
 │                                                                                     │
 │  reply checked with Zod (GoogleSessionSchema)                                       │
 │        │                                                                            │
 │        ▼  startSession(access_token) — the ORDER matters:                           │
 │   1. save token → Keychain  (aira_access_token, WHEN_UNLOCKED_THIS_DEVICE_ONLY)     │
 │   2. load the profile with the token  (max 4 s, never blocks sign-in)               │
 │   3. authStore.markSignedIn()  → the app shows home (or setup, if not finished)     │
 │   4. emit "auth:login"         → the WebSocket connects with Bearer <token>         │
 │                                                                                     │
 │  From now on, every request: Authorization: Bearer <AiRA JWT>                       │
 └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

   409 in Phase 3? → the app switches to the browser sign-in automatically

Each phase in plain words

Phase 1: the phone gets proof from Google

  • The Google Sign-In SDK (@react-native-google-signin/google-signin) shows Google's own account screen, so the user never types a password into AiRA.
  • offlineAccess: false means we ask Google only who the user is. We get no permission to read their Gmail or Drive. That comes later and separately, only if they connect it.
  • Returning users get one tap:
    • The app remembers the last Google account (id, first name, email) and asks "Continue as Dhanesh?".
    • "Continue" uses signInSilently(), which shows no Google screen at all.
    • The code also checks the restored user is the same person (credential.data.user.id === account.id).
  • Double-tap protection: an isSigningIn flag stops two sign-ins running at once.
  • Cancelling is not an error. It returns "canceled", so the app shows no error message.

Phase 2: the API checks the proof itself

  • Never trust the phone. Anyone could send a fake token, so the server checks it with Google's public key itself.
  • What each check prevents:
CheckStops
Signature (RS256 with Google's key)Fake or edited tokens
aud = our client idA token made for another app being used on ours
iss = accounts.google.comTokens not from Google
expOld, replayed tokens
email_verifiedAccounts with unconfirmed emails
  • Two copies of the email: an email_token to search with (one-way HMAC) and an encrypted_email to read later (AES-GCM). The plain email is never stored.

Phase 3: whose account is this?

  • google_sub is Google's permanent id for the person. An email can change; the sub never does. So it's checked first.
  • Linking older accounts: someone who signed up earlier through the browser has no google_sub yet. We find them by email_token and attach the sub atomically (the update only succeeds if google_sub is still empty). Two logins at the same moment can't both link.
  • Why 409: for @gmail.com and Workspace addresses, Google really owns the email. For a personal domain on a Google account, Google can't prove the user still owns it. So we don't link silently; we send them to the browser sign-in.
  • Why loop twice: if a parallel request creates or links the same user between our read and our write, we get a duplicate-key error. Reading again resolves it instead of failing.
  • Background work: the new user's scratchpad (name, timezone) is created after the response is sent, so login isn't slowed down.

Phase 4: our own token

  • From here on, Google's token is thrown away. We issue our own JWT { sub: user_id, iat }, valid 7 days.
  • Why our own token: every API and WebSocket call can then be checked quickly with our secret, with no call to Google, and we decide how long a session lasts.

Phase 5: the app is signed in

  • The token goes into the iOS Keychain or Android Keystore via SecureStore, marked THIS_DEVICE_ONLY, so it's never copied to another phone through a backup.
  • It's read from the Keychain once per launch, kept in memory, and attached as Authorization: Bearer … by the axios interceptor.
  • The profile is loaded before home is shown, so the app knows whether setup is unfinished. It gives up after 4 seconds, so a slow network never blocks sign-in.

What can go wrong, and what the user sees

WhereWhat happensResult
Google picker closedSIGN_IN_CANCELLEDBack on the sign-in screen, no error
Fake or expired ID token401Error message, try again
Google's keys unreachable503"Temporarily unavailable"
Account deactivated or conflicting401Error message
Personal-domain Google account, old user409Browser sign-in starts automatically
Profile load slowgives up after 4 sSigns in anyway

Files to remember

SideFileWhat it does
Appfeatures/auth/hooks/use-sign-in-with-google.tsRuns the whole flow, including the 409 fallback
Appfeatures/auth/services/google-native-sign-in.tsGoogle SDK: account choice, silent or interactive sign-in
Appfeatures/auth/hooks/use-google-account-choice.ts"Continue as …" prompt
Appfeatures/auth/services/start-session.tsKeychain → profile → signed in → event
Appconfig/api-client.tsAdds the Bearer header; a 401 ends the session
APIrouters/auth/controllers/auth_c_v1.pyPOST /v1/auth/google
APIrouters/auth/services/auth_s_v1.pygoogle_native_signin
APIadapters/google_adp.pyverify_native_id_token (JWKS, aud, iss, email_verified)
APIentities/user_ent.pyresolve_google: find, link or create
APIadapters/crypto_adp.pytokenize, encrypt_transient, session JWT

Key ideas to say in an interview

  • Google proves the identity once, and our token does everything after. Swapping Google's ID token for our own JWT at the login step keeps every later request fast and independent of Google.
  • Look up by google_sub first, then email. The permanent id beats the changeable email, and the email path exists only to link older accounts, atomically and only when Google can vouch for the address.
  • Ask for the least. offlineAccess: false means a normal login gives AiRA no access to the user's Google data. Gmail, Calendar and Drive are separate, opt-in connectors with their own consent.
